(1 + \mu_ {\epsilon}) ^ {n} \leqslant 1 + 1. 0 1 n \mu_ {\epsilon}.\tag{3.12}
$$

By (3.7), (3.8) and (3.12), the proof is complete. $\square$ 

We consider the following example. 

Example 3.1 For given $\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in \mathbb{R}^n$ and $n\mu_{\epsilon}\leqslant 0.01$ , estimate the upper bound of 

$$
\left| f l \left(\mathbf {x} ^ {T} \mathbf {y}\right) - \mathbf {x} ^ {T} \mathbf {y} \right|.
$$

Let 

$$
S _ {k} = f l \left(\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {k} x _ {i} y _ {i}\right).
$$

By Theorem 3.10, we have 

$$
S _ {1} = x _ {1} y _ {1} (1 + \gamma_ {1}), \quad | \gamma_ {1} | \leqslant \mu_ {\epsilon},
$$

and 

$$
\begin{array}{l} S _ {k} = f l (S _ {k - 1} + f l (x _ {k} y _ {k})) \\ \qquad = [ S _ {k - 1} + x _ {k} y _ {k} (1 + \gamma_ {k}) ] (1 + \delta_ {k}), \qquad | \delta_ {k} |, | \gamma_ {k} | \leqslant \mu_ {\epsilon}. \end{array}
$$

Therefore, 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} f l (\mathbf {x} ^ {T} \mathbf {y}) & = S _ {n} = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} x _ {i} y _ {i} (1 + \gamma_ {i}) \prod_ {j = i} ^ {n} (1 + \delta_ {j}) \\ & = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} (1 + \epsilon_ {i}) x _ {i} y _ {i}, \end{array}
$$

where 

$$
1 + \epsilon_ {i} = (1 + \gamma_ {i}) \prod_ {j = i} ^ {n} (1 + \delta_ {j})
$$

with $\delta_1 = 0$ . Thus, if $n\mu_{\epsilon} \leqslant 0.01$ , we then have by Theorem 3.11, 

$$
\left| f l \left(\mathbf {x} ^ {T} \mathbf {y}\right) - \mathbf {x} ^ {T} \mathbf {y} \right| \leqslant \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \left| \epsilon_ {i} \right| \cdot \left| x _ {i} y _ {i} \right| \leqslant 1. 0 1 n \mu_ {\epsilon} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \left| x _ {i} y _ {i} \right|.
$$

Before we finish this section, let us briefly discuss the floating point analysis on elementary matrix operations. We recall the following notations: 

$$
| E | = [ | e _ {i j} | ],
$$

where $E = [e_{ij}] \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ and 

$$
| E | \leqslant | F | \Leftrightarrow | e _ {i j} | \leqslant | f _ {i j} |
$$

for $i, j = 1,2,\ldots,n$ . Let $A, B \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ be matrices with entries in $\mathcal{F}$ , and $\alpha \in \mathcal{F}$ . By Theorem 3.10, we have 

$$
f l (\alpha A) = \alpha A + E, \quad | E | \leqslant \mu_ {\epsilon} | \alpha A |,
$$

and 

$$
f l (A + B) = (A + B) + E, \quad | E | \leqslant \mu_ {\epsilon} | A + B |.
$$

From Example 3.1, we also have 

$$
f l (A B) = A B + E, \quad | E | \leqslant 1. 0 1 n \mu_ {\epsilon} | A | \cdot | B |.
$$

Note that $|A| \cdot |B|$ maybe is much larger than $|AB|$ . Therefore the relative error of AB may not be small. For a detailed study of numerical computing with floating point arithmetic, we refer to [65]. 

## 3.4 Error analysis on partial pivoting

We show that if Gaussian elimination with partial pivoting is used to solve $A\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{b}$ , then the computational solution $\tilde{\mathbf{x}}$ satisfies 

$$
(A + E) \tilde {\mathbf {x}} = \mathbf {b},
$$

where $E$ is an error matrix. An upper bound of $E$ is also given. We first study the rounding error of the LU factorization of $A$ . 

Lemma 3.1 Let $A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ with floating point entries. Assume that $A$ has an LU factorization and $6n\mu_{\epsilon} \leqslant 1$ where $\mu_{\epsilon}$ is the machine precision. Then by using Gaussian elimination, we have 

$$
\widetilde {L} \widetilde {U} = A + E,
$$

where 

$$
| E | \leqslant 3 n \mu_ {\epsilon} (| A | + | \widetilde {L} | \cdot | \widetilde {U} |).
$$

Proof We use induction on $n$ . Obviously, Lemma 3.1 is true for $n = 1$ . Assume that the lemma holds for $n - 1$ . Now, we consider a matrix $A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ : 

$$
A = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \alpha & \mathbf {w} ^ {T} \\ \mathbf {u} & A _ {1} \end{array} \right],
$$

where $A_{1} \in \mathbb{R}^{(n-1) \times (n-1)}$ . At the first step of Gaussian elimination, we should compute the vector $\tilde{\mathbf{v}}_{1} = fl(\mathbf{u}/\alpha)$ and modify the matrix $A_{1}$ as 

$$
\widetilde {A} _ {1} = f l (A _ {1} - f l (\tilde {\mathbf {v}} _ {1} \mathbf {w} ^ {T})).
$$

By Theorem 3.10, we have 

$$
\tilde {\mathbf {v}} _ {1} = \mathbf {u} / \alpha + \mathbf {f}, \quad | \mathbf {f} | \leqslant \frac {\mu_ {\epsilon}}{| \alpha |} | \mathbf {u} |\tag{3.13}
$$

and 

$$
\widetilde {A} _ {1} = A _ {1} - \tilde {\mathbf {v}} _ {1} \mathbf {w} ^ {T} + F, \quad | F | \leqslant (2 + \mu_ {\epsilon}) \mu_ {\epsilon} (| A _ {1} | + | \tilde {\mathbf {v}} _ {1} | \cdot | \mathbf {w} | ^ {T}).\tag{3.14}
$$

For $\widetilde{A}_1$ , by using the assumption, we obtain an $LU$ factorization with a unit lower triangular matrix $\widetilde{L}_1$ and an upper triangular matrix $\widetilde{U}_1$ such that 

$$
\widetilde {L} _ {1} \widetilde {U} _ {1} = \widetilde {A} _ {1} + E _ {1},
$$

where 

$$
\left| E _ {1} \right| \leqslant 3 (n - 1) \mu_ {\epsilon} \left(\left| \widetilde {A} _ {1} \right| + \left| \widetilde {L} _ {1} \right| \cdot \left| \widetilde {U} _ {1} \right|\right).
$$

Thus, we have 

$$
\widetilde {L} \widetilde {U} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} 1 & \mathbf {0} \\ \widetilde {\mathbf {v}} _ {1} & \widetilde {L} _ {1} \end{array} \right] \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \alpha & \mathbf {w} ^ {T} \\ \mathbf {0} & \widetilde {U} _ {1} \end{array} \right] = A + E,
$$

where 

$$
E = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} {\bf 0} & {\bf 0} \\ {\alpha {\bf f}} & {E _ {1} + F} \end{array} \right].
$$

By using (3.14), we obtain 

$$
\left| \widetilde {A} _ {1} \right| \leqslant \left(1 + 2 \mu_ {\epsilon} + \mu_ {\epsilon} ^ {2}\right) \left(\left| A _ {1} \right| + \left| \tilde {\mathbf {v}} _ {1} \right| \cdot \left| \mathbf {w} \right| ^ {T}\right).
$$

Therefore, by using the condition $6n\mu_{\epsilon} \leqslant 1$ , we have 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & {| E _ {1} + F | \leqslant | E _ {1} | + | F |} \\ & {\quad \leqslant 3 (n - 1) \mu_ {\epsilon} (| \widetilde {A} _ {1} | + | \widetilde {L} _ {1} | \cdot | \widetilde {U} _ {1} |) + (2 + \mu_ {\epsilon}) \mu_ {\epsilon} (| A _ {1} | + | \widetilde {\mathbf {v}} _ {1} | \cdot | \mathbf {w} | ^ {T})} \\ & {\quad \leqslant 3 (n - 1) \mu_ {\epsilon} \left((1 + 2 \mu_ {\epsilon} + \mu_ {\epsilon} ^ {2}) (| A _ {1} | + | \widetilde {\mathbf {v}} _ {1} | \cdot | \mathbf {w} | ^ {T}) + | \widetilde {L} _ {1} | \cdot | \widetilde {U} _ {1} |\right)} \\ & {\quad \quad + (2 + \mu_ {\epsilon}) \mu_ {\epsilon} (| A _ {1} | + | \widetilde {\mathbf {v}} _ {1} | \cdot | \mathbf {w} | ^ {T})} \\ & {\quad \leqslant \mu_ {\epsilon} \Big (3 n - 1 + [ 6 n + 3 (n - 1) \mu_ {\epsilon} - 5 ] \mu_ {\epsilon} \Big) (| A _ {1} | + | \widetilde {\mathbf {v}} _ {1} | \cdot | \mathbf {w} | ^ {T})} \\ & {\quad \quad + 3 (n - 1) \mu_ {\epsilon} (| \widetilde {L} _ {1} | \cdot | \widetilde {U} _ {1} |)} \\ & {\quad \leqslant 3 n \mu_ {\epsilon} (| A _ {1} | + | \widetilde {\mathbf {v}} _ {1} | \cdot | \mathbf {w} | ^ {T} + | \widetilde {L} _ {1} | \cdot | \widetilde {U} _ {1} |).} \end{array}
$$

Combining with (3.13), we obtain 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & {| E | = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \mathbf {0} & \mathbf {0} \\ | \alpha | | \mathbf {f} | & | E _ {1} + F | \end{array} \right]} \\ & {\leqslant 3 n \mu_ {\epsilon} \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \mathbf {0} & \mathbf {0} \\ | \mathbf {u} | & | A _ {1} | + | \tilde {\mathbf {v}} _ {1} | \cdot | \mathbf {w} | ^ {T} + | \widetilde {L} _ {1} | \cdot | \widetilde {U} _ {1} | \end{array} \right]} \\ & {\leqslant 3 n \mu_ {\epsilon} \left(\left[ \begin{array}{c c} | \alpha | & | \mathbf {w} | ^ {T} \\ | \mathbf {u} | & | A _ {1} | \end{array} \right] + \left[ \begin{array}{c c} 1 & \mathbf {0} \\ | \tilde {\mathbf {v}} _ {1} | & | \widetilde {L} _ {1} | \end{array} \right] \left[ \begin{array}{c c} | \alpha | & | \mathbf {w} | ^ {T} \\ \mathbf {0} & | \widetilde {U} _ {1} | \end{array} \right]\right)} \\ & {= 3 n \mu_ {\epsilon} (| A | + | \widetilde {L} | \cdot | \widetilde {U} |).} \end{array}
$$

The proof is complete. $\square$ 

Corollary 3.3 Let $A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ be nonsingular with floating point entries and $6n\mu_{\epsilon} \leqslant 1$ . Assume that by using Gaussian elimination with partial pivoting, we obtain 

$$
\widetilde {L} \widetilde {U} = P A + E,
$$

where $\widetilde{L} = [l_{ij}]$ is a unit lower triangular matrix with $|l_{ij}| \leqslant 1$ , $\widetilde{U}$ is an upper triangular matrix and $P$ is a permutation matrix. Then $E$ satisfies the following inequality: 

$$
| E | \leqslant 3 n \mu_ {\epsilon} (| P A | + | \widetilde {L} | \cdot | \widetilde {U} |).
$$

## 3.4 Error analysis on partial pivoting

After we obtain the LU factorization of A, the problem of solving Ax = b becomes the problem of solving the following two triangular systems: 

$$
\widetilde {L} \mathbf {y} = P \mathbf {b}, \quad \widetilde {U} \mathbf {x} = \mathbf {y}.
$$

Therefore, we need to estimate the rounding error of solving triangular systems. 

Lemma 3.2 Let $S \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ be a nonsingular triangular matrix with floating point entries and $1.01n\mu_{\epsilon} \leqslant 0.01$ . By using the method proposed in Subsection 2.1.1 to solve $S\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{b}$ , we then obtain a computational solution $\tilde{\mathbf{x}}$ which satisfies 

$$
(S + H) \tilde {\mathbf {x}} = \mathbf {b},
$$

where 

$$
| H | \leqslant 1. 0 1 n \mu_ {\epsilon} | S |.
$$

Proof We use induction on n. Without loss of generality, let S = L be a lower triangular matrix. Obviously, Lemma 3.2 is true for n = 1. Assume that the lemma is true for n - 1. Now, we consider a lower triangular matrix $L \in R^{n \times n}$ . Let $\tilde{x}$ be the computational solution of Lx = b and we partition L, b and $\tilde{x}$ as follows: 

$$
L = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} l _ {1 1} & \mathbf {0} \\ \mathbf {v} _ {1} & L _ {1} \end{array} \right], \qquad \mathbf {b} = \left[ \begin{array}{c} b _ {1} \\ \mathbf {c c} \end{array} \right], \qquad \tilde {\mathbf {x}} = \left[ \begin{array}{c} \tilde {x} _ {1} \\ \tilde {\mathbf {y}} \end{array} \right],
$$

where $\mathbf{c},\tilde{\mathbf{y}}\in \mathbb{R}^{n - 1}$ and $L_{1}\in \mathbb{R}^{(n - 1)\times (n - 1)}$ . By Theorem 3.10, we have 

$$
\tilde {x} _ {1} = f l (b _ {1} / l _ {1 1}) = \frac {b _ {1}}{l _ {1 1} (1 + \delta_ {1})}, \quad | \delta_ {1} | \leqslant \mu_ {\epsilon}.\tag{3.15}
$$

Note that $\tilde{\mathbf{y}}$ is the computational solution of the $(n - 1)$ -by- $(n - 1)$ system 

$$
L _ {1} \mathbf {y} = f l (\mathbf {c} - \tilde {x} _ {1} \mathbf {v} _ {1}).
$$

By assumption, we have 

$$
(L _ {1} + H _ {1}) \tilde {\mathbf {y}} = f l (\mathbf {c} - \tilde {x} _ {1} \mathbf {v} _ {1})
$$

where 

$$
\left| H _ {1} \right| \leqslant 1. 0 1 (n - 1) \mu_ {\epsilon} \left| L _ {1} \right|.\tag{3.16}
$$

By Theorem 3.10 again, we obtain 

$$
f l (\mathbf {c} - \tilde {x} _ {1} \mathbf {v} _ {1}) = f l (\mathbf {c} - f l (\tilde {x} _ {1} \mathbf {v} _ {1})) = (I + D _ {\gamma}) ^ {- 1} (\mathbf {c} - \tilde {x} _ {1} \mathbf {v} _ {1} - \tilde {x} _ {1} D _ {\delta} \mathbf {v} _ {1}),
$$

where 

$$
D _ {\gamma} = \operatorname{diag} (\gamma_ {2}, \dots , \gamma_ {n}), \quad D _ {\delta} = \operatorname{diag} (\delta_ {2}, \dots , \delta_ {n})
$$

with 

$$
\left| \gamma_ {i} \right|, \left| \delta_ {i} \right| \leqslant \mu_ {\epsilon}, \quad i = 2, \dots , n.
$$

Therefore, 

$$
\tilde {x} _ {1} \mathbf {v} _ {1} + \tilde {x} _ {1} D _ {\delta} \mathbf {v} _ {1} + (I + D _ {\gamma}) (L _ {1} + H _ {1}) \tilde {\mathbf {y}} = \mathbf {c},
$$

and then 

$$
(L + H) \tilde {\mathbf {x}} = \mathbf {b},
$$

where 

$$
H = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \delta_ {1} l _ {1 1} & \mathbf {0} \\ D _ {\delta} \mathbf {v} _ {1} & H _ {1} + D _ {\gamma} (L _ {1} + H _ {1}) \end{array} \right].
$$

By using (3.15), (3.16) and the condition $1.01n\mu_{\epsilon} \leqslant 0.01$ , we have 

$$
| H | \leqslant \left[ \begin{array}{c c} | \delta_ {1} | \cdot | l _ {1 1} | & \mathbf {0} \\ | D _ {\delta} | \cdot | \mathbf {v} _ {1} | & | H _ {1} | + | D _ {\gamma} | (| L _ {1} | + | H _ {1} |) \end{array} \right]
$$

$$
\leqslant \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \mu_ {\epsilon} | l _ {1 1} | & \mathbf {0} \\ \mu_ {\epsilon} | \mathbf {v} _ {1} | & | H _ {1} | + \mu_ {\epsilon} (| L _ {1} | + | H _ {1} |) \end{array} \right]
$$

$$
\leqslant \mu_ {\epsilon} \left[ \begin{array}{c c} | l _ {1 1} | & \mathbf {0} \\ | \mathbf {v} _ {1} | & [ 1. 0 1 (n - 1) + 1 + 1. 0 1 (n - 1) \mu_ {\epsilon} ] | L _ {1} | \end{array} \right]
$$

$$
\leqslant 1. 0 1 n \mu_ {\epsilon} | L |. \quad \square
$$

We then have the main theorem of this section. 

Theorem 3.12 Let $A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ be a nonsingular matrix with floating point entries and $1.01n\mu_{\epsilon} \leqslant 0.01$ . If Gaussian elimination with partial pivoting is used to solve $A\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{b}$ , we then obtain a computational solution $\tilde{\mathbf{x}}$ which satisfies 

$$
(A + \delta A) \tilde {\mathbf {x}} = \mathbf {b},
$$

where 

$$
\| \delta A \| _ {\infty} \leqslant \mu_ {\epsilon} (3 n + 5. 0 4 n ^ {3} \rho) \| A \| _ {\infty}\tag{3.17}
$$

with the growth factor 

$$
\rho \equiv \frac {1}{\| A \| _ {\infty}} \max _ {i, j, k} | a _ {i j} ^ {(k)} |.
$$

Proof By using Gaussian elimination with partial pivoting, we have the following two triangular systems: 

$$
\tilde {L} \mathbf {y} = P \mathbf {b}, \quad \tilde {U} \mathbf {x} = \mathbf {y}.
$$

By using Lemma 3.2, the computational solution $\tilde{\mathbf{x}}$ should satisfy 

$$
(\widetilde {L} + F) (\widetilde {U} + G) \widetilde {\mathbf {x}} = P \mathbf {b},
$$

## 3.4 Error analysis on partial pivoting

i.e., 

$$
(\widetilde {L} \widetilde {U} + F \widetilde {U} + \widetilde {L} G + F G) \widetilde {\mathbf {x}} = P \mathbf {b},\tag{3.18}
$$

where 

$$
| F | \leqslant 1. 0 1 n \mu_ {\epsilon} | \widetilde {L} |, \quad | G | \leqslant 1. 0 1 n \mu_ {\epsilon} | \widetilde {U} |.\tag{3.19}
$$

Substituting $\widetilde{L}\widetilde{U} = PA + E$ into (3.18), we have 

$$
(A + \delta A) \tilde {\mathbf {x}} = \mathbf {b},
$$

where 

$$
\delta A = P ^ {T} (E + F \widetilde {U} + \widetilde {L} G + F G).
$$

By using (3.19), Corollary 3.3 and the condition $1.01n\mu_{\epsilon} \leqslant 0.01$ , we have 

$$
| \delta A | \leqslant P ^ {T} (3 n \mu_ {\epsilon} | P A | + (3 n + 2. 0 4 n) \mu_ {\epsilon} | \widetilde {L} | \cdot | \widetilde {U} |)
$$

$$
= n \mu_ {\epsilon} P ^ {T} (3 | P A | + 5. 0 4 | \widetilde {L} | \cdot | \widetilde {U} |).\tag{3.20}
$$

By Corollary 3.3 again, the absolute values of entries in $\widetilde{L}$ are less than or equal to 1. Therefore, we have 

$$
\| \widetilde {L} \| _ {\infty} \leqslant n.\tag{3.21}
$$

We now define 

$$
\rho \equiv \frac {1}{\| A \| _ {\infty}} \max _ {i, j, k} | a _ {i j} ^ {(k)} |
$$

and then we have 

$$
\| \widetilde {U} \| _ {\infty} \leqslant n \rho \| A \| _ {\infty}.\tag{3.22}
$$

Substituting (3.21) and (3.22) into (3.20), we have (3.17). The proof is complete. $\square$ 

We remark that $\|\delta A\|_{\infty}$ usually is very small comparing with the initial error from given data. Thus, Gaussian elimination with partial pivoting is numerically stable. Growth factors in Gaussian elimination with complete pivoting can be found in [33,38]. 

## Exercises:

1. Let 

$$
A = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} 1 & 0. 9 9 9 9 9 9 \\ 0. 9 9 9 9 9 9 & 1 \end{array} \right].
$$

Compute $A^{-1}$ , $\det(A)$ and the condition number of A. 

2. Prove that $\|AB\|_{F} \leqslant \|A\|_{2}\|B\|_{F}$ and $\|AB\|_{F} \leqslant \|A\|_{F}\|B\|_{2}$ . 

3. Prove that $\|A\|_{2}^{2}\leqslant\|A\|_{1}\|A\|_{\infty}$ for any square matrix A. 

4. Show that 

$$
\left\| \left[ \begin{array}{c c} A _ {1 1} & 0 \\ 0 & A _ {2 2} \end{array} \right] \right\| _ {2} \leqslant \left\| \left[ \begin{array}{c c} A _ {1 1} & A _ {1 2} \\ A _ {2 1} & A _ {2 2} \end{array} \right] \right\| _ {2}.
$$

5. Let $A$ be nonsingular. Show that 

$$
\| A ^ {- 1} \| _ {2} ^ {- 1} = \min _ {\| \mathbf {x} \| _ {2} = 1} \| A \mathbf {x} \| _ {2}.
$$

6. Show that if $S$ is real and $S = -S^T$ , then $I - S$ is nonsingular and the matrix 

$$
(I - S) ^ {- 1} (I + S)
$$

is orthogonal. This is known as the Cayley transform of $S$ . 

7. Prove that if both $A$ and $A + E$ are nonsingular, then 

$$
\frac {\| (A + E) ^ {- 1} - A ^ {- 1} \|}{\| A ^ {- 1} \|} \leqslant \| (A + E) ^ {- 1} \| \cdot \| E \|.
$$

8. Let $A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ be nonsingular and let $\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{y}, \mathbf{z} \in \mathbb{R}^n$ such that $A\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{b}$ and $A\mathbf{y} = \mathbf{b} + \mathbf{z}$ . Show that 

$$
\frac {\| \mathbf {z} \| _ {2}}{\| A \| _ {2}} \leqslant \| \mathbf {x} - \mathbf {y} \| _ {2} \leqslant \| A ^ {- 1} \| _ {2} \| \mathbf {z} \| _ {2}.
$$

9. Let $A = [a_{ij}]$ be an $m$ -by- $n$ matrix. Define 

$$
| | | A | | | _ {l} = \max _ {i, j} | a _ {i j} |.
$$

Is $||| \cdot |||_{l}$ a matrix norm? Give a reason for your answer. 

10. Show that if $X \in \mathbb{C}^{n \times n}$ is nonsingular, then $\| A \|_X = \| X^{-1}AX \|_2$ defines a matrix norm. 

11. Let $A = LDL^{T} \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ be a symmetric positive definite matrix and 

$$
D = \operatorname{diag} (d _ {1 1}, \dots , d _ {n n}).
$$

Show that 

$$
\kappa_ {2} (A) \geqslant \frac {\max _ {i} \{d _ {i i} \}}{\min _ {i} \{d _ {i i} \}}.
$$

12. Verify that 

$$
\left\| \mathbf {x} \mathbf {y} ^ {*} \right\| _ {F} = \left\| \mathbf {x} \mathbf {y} ^ {*} \right\| _ {2} = \left\| \mathbf {x} \right\| _ {2} \left\| \mathbf {y} \right\| _ {2},
$$

for any $\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in \mathbb{C}^n$ 

13. Show that if $0 \neq v \in R^{n}$ and $E \in R^{n \times n}$ , then 

$$
\left\| E \left(I - \frac {\mathbf {v} \mathbf {v} ^ {T}}{\mathbf {v} ^ {T} \mathbf {v}}\right) \right\| _ {F} ^ {2} = \| E \| _ {F} ^ {2} - \frac {\| E \mathbf {v} \| _ {2} ^ {2}}{\mathbf {v} ^ {T} \mathbf {v}}.
$$

14. Let $A = [a_{ij}] \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ and $\mathbf{x} = [x_1, \ldots, x_n]^T \in \mathbb{R}^n$ . Show that 

$$
| A | \cdot | \mathbf {x} | \leqslant \tau \| A \| _ {\infty} | \mathbf {x} |,
$$

where $\tau=\max_{i}|x_{i}|/\min_{i}|x_{i}|$ . 

15. Prove the Sherman-Morrison-Woodbury formula. Let $U, V$ be $n$ -by- $k$ rectangular matrices with $k \leqslant n$ and $A$ be an $n$ -by- $n$ matrix. Then 

$$
T = I + V ^ {T} A ^ {- 1} U
$$

is nonsingular if and only if $A + UV^{T}$ is nonsingular. In this case, we have 

$$
(A + U V ^ {T}) ^ {- 1} = A ^ {- 1} - A ^ {- 1} U T ^ {- 1} V ^ {T} A ^ {- 1}.
$$

## Chapter 4

## Least Squares Problems

In this chapter, we study linear least squares problems: 

$$
\min _ {\mathbf {y} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n}} \| A \mathbf {y} - \mathbf {b} \| _ {2},
$$

where the data matrix $A \in R^{m \times n}$ with $m \geqslant n$ and the observation vector $b \in R^{m}$ are given. We introduce some well-known orthogonal transformations and the QR decomposition for constructing efficient algorithms for these problems. For a literature on least squares problems, we refer to [9,11,28,36,88,92,93,94]. 

## 4.1 Least squares problems

In practice, if we are given m points $t_{1}, t_{2}, \ldots, t_{m}$ with data on these points $y_{1}, y_{2}, \ldots, y_{m}$ , and functions $\phi_{1}(t), \phi_{2}(t), \ldots, \phi_{n}(t)$ defined on these points, we then try to find $f(x, t)$ defined by 

$$
f (\mathbf {x}, t) \equiv \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {n} x _ {j} \phi_ {j} (t),
$$

where $x = [x_{1}, \ldots, x_{n}]^{T}$ such that residuals defined by 

$$
r _ {i} (\mathbf {x}) \equiv y _ {i} - f (\mathbf {x}, t _ {i}) = y _ {i} - \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {n} x _ {j} \phi_ {j} (t _ {i}), \quad i = 1, 2, \dots , m,
$$

can be as small as possible. In matrix form, we have 

$$
\mathbf {r} (\mathbf {x}) = \mathbf {b} - A \mathbf {x},
$$

where 

$$
A = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c} {\phi_ {1} (t _ {1})} & {\dots} & {\phi_ {n} (t _ {1})} \\ {\vdots} & & {\vdots} \\ {\phi_ {1} (t _ {m})} & {\dots} & {\phi_ {n} (t _ {m})} \end{array} \right],
$$

and 

$$
\mathbf {b} = \left[ y _ {1}, \dots , y _ {m} \right] ^ {T}, \quad \mathbf {r} (\mathbf {x}) = \left[ r _ {1} (\mathbf {x}), \dots , r _ {m} (\mathbf {x}) \right] ^ {T}.
$$

## 4.1 Least squares problems

When m = n, we can require that $\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{0}$ and x can be found by solving the system Ax = b. When m > n, we require that $\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{x})$ can reach its minimum under the norm $\|\cdot\|_{2}$ . We therefore introduce the following definition of the least squares problem. 

Definition 4.1 Let $A \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ and $\mathbf{b} \in \mathbb{R}^m$ . Find $\mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{R}^n$ such that 

$$
\| \mathbf {b} - A \mathbf {x} \| _ {2} = \| \mathbf {r} (\mathbf {x}) \| _ {2} = \min _ {\mathbf {y} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n}} \| \mathbf {r} (\mathbf {y}) \| _ {2} = \min _ {\mathbf {y} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n}} \| \mathbf {b} - A \mathbf {y} \| _ {2}.\tag{4.1}
$$

It is called the least squares (LS) problem and $\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{x})$ is called the residual. 

In the following, we only consider the case of 

$$
\operatorname{rank} (A) = n <   m.
$$

We first study the solution $\mathbf{x}$ of the following equation 

$$
A \mathbf {x} = \mathbf {b}, \quad A \in \mathbb {R} ^ {m \times n}.\tag{4.2}
$$

The range of matrix $A$ is defined by 

$$
\mathcal {R} (A) \equiv \{\mathbf {y} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {m}: \mathbf {y} = A \mathbf {x}, \mathbf {x} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n} \}.
$$

It is easy to see that 

$$
\mathcal {R} (A) = \operatorname{span} \left\{\mathbf {a} _ {1}, \dots , \mathbf {a} _ {n} \right\}
$$

where $\mathbf{a}_{i}\ (i=1,\ldots,n)$ are column vectors of A. The nullspace of A is defined by 

$$
\mathcal {N} (A) \equiv \{\mathbf {x} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n}: A \mathbf {x} = \mathbf {0} \}.
$$

The dimension of $\mathcal{N}(A)$ is denoted by nullity(A). The orthogonal complement of a subspace $S \subset R^{n}$ is defined by 

$$
\mathcal {S} ^ {\perp} \equiv \left\{\mathbf {y} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n}: \mathbf {y} ^ {T} \mathbf {x} = 0, \text {   for   all   } \mathbf {x} \in \mathcal {S} \right\}.
$$

We have the following theorems for (4.2). 

Theorem 4.1 The equation (4.2) has solutions $\Leftrightarrow \operatorname{rank}(A) = \operatorname{rank}([A, \mathbf{b}])$ . 

Theorem 4.2 Let $\mathbf{x}$ be a special solution of (4.2). Then the solution set of (4.2) is given by $\mathbf{x} + \mathcal{N}(A)$ . 

Corollary 4.1 Assume that the equation (4.2) has some solution. The solution is unique $\Leftrightarrow$ nullity $(A) = 0$ . 

We have the following essential theorem for the solution of (4.1). 

Theorem 4.3 The LS problem (4.1) always has solutions. The solution is unique if and only if nullity(A) = 0. 

Proof Since 

$$
\mathbb {R} ^ {m} = \mathcal {R} (A) \oplus \mathcal {R} (A) ^ {\perp},
$$

the vector b can be expressed uniquely by 

$$
\mathbf {b} = \mathbf {b} _ {1} + \mathbf {b} _ {2}
$$

where $\mathbf{b}_1 \in \mathcal{R}(A)$ and $\mathbf{b}_2 \in \mathcal{R}(A)^\perp$ . For any $\mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{R}^n$ , since $\mathbf{b}_1 - A\mathbf{x} \in \mathcal{R}(A)$ and is orthogonal to $\mathbf{b}_2$ , we therefore have 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & {\| \mathbf {r} (\mathbf {x}) \| _ {2} ^ {2} = \| \mathbf {b} - A \mathbf {x} \| _ {2} ^ {2} = \| (\mathbf {b} _ {1} - A \mathbf {x}) + \mathbf {b} _ {2} \| _ {2} ^ {2}} \\ & {\qquad = \| \mathbf {b} _ {1} - A \mathbf {x} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + \| \mathbf {b} _ {2} \| _ {2} ^ {2}.} \end{array}
$$

Note that $\|\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{x})\|_{2}^{2}$ reaches the minimum if and only if $\|b_{1}-Ax\|_{2}^{2}$ reaches the minimum. Since $\mathbf{b}_{1}\in\mathcal{R}(A)$ , $\|\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{x})\|_{2}^{2}$ reaches its minimum if and only if 

$$
A \mathbf {x} = \mathbf {b} _ {1},
$$

i.e., 

$$
\left\| \mathbf {b} _ {1} - A \mathbf {x} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} = 0.
$$

Thus, by Corollary 4.1, we know that the solution of $A\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{b}_1$ is unique, i.e., the solution of (4.1) is unique, if and only if 

$$
\text { nullity } (A) = 0. \quad \square
$$

Let 

$$
\mathcal {X} = \{\mathbf {x} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n}: \mathbf {x} \text {   is   a   solution   of   (4.1) } \}.
$$

We have 

Theorem 4.4 A vector $\mathbf{x} \in \mathcal{X}$ if and only if 

$$
A ^ {T} A \mathbf {x} = A ^ {T} \mathbf {b}.\tag{4.3}
$$

Proof Let $x \in X$ . By Theorem 4.3, we know that $Ax = b_{1}$ where $b_{1} \in \mathcal{R}(A)$ and 

$$
\mathbf {r} (\mathbf {x}) = \mathbf {b} - A \mathbf {x} = \mathbf {b} - \mathbf {b} _ {1} = \mathbf {b} _ {2} \in \mathcal {R} (A) ^ {\perp}.
$$

Therefore 

$$
A ^ {T} \mathbf {r} (\mathbf {x}) = A ^ {T} \mathbf {b} _ {2} = \mathbf {0}.
$$

Substituting $\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{b} - A\mathbf{x}$ into $A^T\mathbf{r}(\mathbf{x}) = \mathbf{0}$ , we obtain (4.3). 

Conversely, let $\mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{R}^n$ satisfy 

$$
A ^ {T} A \mathbf {x} = A ^ {T} \mathbf {b},
$$

then for any $\mathbf{y} \in \mathbb{R}^n$ , we have 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & {\| \mathbf {b} - A (\mathbf {x} + \mathbf {y}) \| _ {2} ^ {2} = \| \mathbf {b} - A \mathbf {x} \| _ {2} ^ {2} - 2 \mathbf {y} ^ {T} A ^ {T} (\mathbf {b} - A \mathbf {x}) + \| A \mathbf {y} \| _ {2} ^ {2}} \\ & {\qquad = \| \mathbf {b} - A \mathbf {x} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + \| A \mathbf {y} \| _ {2} ^ {2}} \\ & {\qquad \geqslant \| \mathbf {b} - A \mathbf {x} \| _ {2} ^ {2}.} \end{array}
$$

Thus, $\mathbf{x} \in \mathcal{X}$ . 

We therefore have the following algorithm for LS problems: 

(1) Compute $C = A^{T}A$ and $\mathbf{d} = A^{T}\mathbf{b}$ . 

(2) Find the Cholesky factorization of $C = LL^{T}$ . 

(3) Solve triangular linear systems: $L\mathbf{y} = \mathbf{d}$ and $L^T\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{y}$ . 

We remark that in computation of $A^{T}A$ , usually, the operation cost is $\mathcal{O}(n^{2}m)$ , and some information of matrix A could be lost. For example, we consider 

$$
A = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c} 1 & 1 & 1 \\ \epsilon & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & \epsilon & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & \epsilon \end{array} \right].
$$

We have 

$$
A ^ {T} A = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c} 1 + \epsilon^ {2} & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & 1 + \epsilon^ {2} & 1 \\ 1 & 1 & 1 + \epsilon^ {2} \end{array} \right].
$$

Assume that $\epsilon = 10^{-3}$ and a 6-digital decimal floating system is used. Then $1 + \epsilon^2 = 1 + 10^{-6}$ is rounded off to be 1, which means that $A^T A$ is singular! 

We note that the solution $\mathbf{x}$ of (4.3) can be expressed as 

$$
\mathbf {x} = (A ^ {T} A) ^ {- 1} A ^ {T} \mathbf {b}.
$$

If we let 

$$
A ^ {\dagger} = (A ^ {T} A) ^ {- 1} A ^ {T},
$$

then the LS solution x could be written as 

$$
\mathbf {x} = A ^ {\dagger} \mathbf {b}.
$$

Actually, the n-by-m matrix $A^{\dagger}$ is the Moore-Penrose generalized inverse of A, which is unique [30,39,88]. In general, we have 

Definition 4.2 Let $X \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times m}$ . If it satisfies the following conditions: 

$$
A X A = A, \quad X A X = X, \quad (A X) ^ {T} = A X, \quad (X A) ^ {T} = X A,
$$

then $X$ is called the Moore-Penrose generalized inverse of $A$ and denoted by $A^{\dagger}$ . 

Now we develop the perturbation analysis of LS problems. Assume that there is a perturbation $\delta b$ on b and let x, $x + \delta x$ denote the solutions of the following LS problems, respectively, 

$$
\min _ {\mathbf {x}} \| \mathbf {b} - A \mathbf {x} \| _ {2}, \quad \min _ {\mathbf {x}} \| (\mathbf {b} + \delta \mathbf {b}) - A \mathbf {x} \| _ {2}.
$$

Then 

$$
\mathbf {x} = A ^ {\dagger} \mathbf {b},
$$

and 

$$
\mathbf {x} + \delta \mathbf {x} = A ^ {\dagger} (\mathbf {b} + \delta \mathbf {b}) = A ^ {\dagger} \tilde {\mathbf {b}},
$$

where $\tilde{\mathbf{b}} = \mathbf{b} + \delta \mathbf{b}$ . The matrix 2-norm for a matrix $A \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ is defined as 

$$
\| A \| _ {2} \equiv \max _ {\mathbf {0} \neq \mathbf {x} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n}} \frac {\| A \mathbf {x} \| _ {2}}{\| \mathbf {x} \| _ {2}} = \max _ {\| \mathbf {x} \| _ {2} = 1} \| A \mathbf {x} \| _ {2},
$$

which is the same as the matrix 2-norm for square matrices. Here we have 

Theorem 4.5 Let $b_{1}$ and $\tilde{b}_{1}$ denote orthogonal projections of b and $\tilde{b}$ on $\mathcal{R}(A)$ , respectively. If $b_{1} \neq 0$ , then 

$$
\frac {\| \delta \mathbf {x} \| _ {2}}{\| \mathbf {x} \| _ {2}} \leqslant \kappa_ {2} (A) \frac {\| \delta \mathbf {b} _ {1} \| _ {2}}{\| \mathbf {b} _ {1} \| _ {2}},
$$

where $\kappa_{2}(A) = \| A\|_{2}\| A^{\dagger}\|_{2}$ and $\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_1 = \mathbf{b}_1 + \delta \mathbf{b}_1$ 

Proof Let $b_{2}$ denote the orthogonal projection of b on $\mathcal{R}(A)^{\perp}$ . Then $b = b_{1} + b_{2}$ and $A^{T}b_{2} = 0$ . Note that 

$$
A ^ {\dagger} \mathbf {b} = A ^ {\dagger} \mathbf {b} _ {1} + A ^ {\dagger} \mathbf {b} _ {2} = A ^ {\dagger} \mathbf {b} _ {1} + (A ^ {T} A) ^ {- 1} A ^ {T} \mathbf {b} _ {2} = A ^ {\dagger} \mathbf {b} _ {1}.
$$

Similarly, $A^{\dagger}\tilde{b}=A^{\dagger}\tilde{b}_{1}$ . Therefore, 

$$
\left\| \delta \mathbf {x} \right\| _ {2} = \left\| A ^ {\dagger} \mathbf {b} - A ^ {\dagger} \tilde {\mathbf {b}} \right\| _ {2} = \left\| A ^ {\dagger} \left(\mathbf {b} _ {1} - \tilde {\mathbf {b}} _ {1}\right) \right\| _ {2}\tag{4.4}
$$

$$
\leqslant \| A ^ {\dagger} \| _ {2} \| \mathbf {b} _ {1} - \tilde {\mathbf {b}} _ {1} \| _ {2} = \| A ^ {\dagger} \| _ {2} \| \delta \mathbf {b} _ {1} \| _ {2}.
$$

Since $Ax = b_{1}$ , we have 

$$
\left\| \mathbf {b} _ {1} \right\| _ {2} \leqslant \left\| A \right\| _ {2} \left\| \mathbf {x} \right\| _ {2}.\tag{4.5}
$$

By combining (4.4) and (4.5), the proof is complete. $\square$ 

## 4.2 Orthogonal transformations

We remark that the condition number $\kappa_{2}(A)$ is important for LS problems. When $\kappa_{2}(A)$ is large, we say that the LS problem is ill-conditioned. When $\kappa_{2}(A)$ is small, we say that the LS problem is well-conditioned. 

Theorem 4.6 Suppose that column vectors of $A$ are linearly independent. Then 

$$
\kappa_ {2} (A) ^ {2} = \kappa_ {2} (A ^ {T} A).
$$

Proof By Theorem 3.3 (ii) and the given condition, we have 

$$
\| A \| _ {2} ^ {2} = \| A ^ {T} A \| _ {2}, \quad \| A ^ {\dagger} \| _ {2} ^ {2} = \| A ^ {\dagger} (A ^ {\dagger}) ^ {T} \| _ {2} = \| (A ^ {T} A) ^ {- 1} \| _ {2}.
$$

Therefore, 

$$
\kappa_ {2} (A) ^ {2} = \| A \| _ {2} ^ {2} \| A ^ {\dagger} \| _ {2} ^ {2} = \| A ^ {T} A \| _ {2} \| (A ^ {T} A) ^ {- 1} \| _ {2} = \kappa_ {2} (A ^ {T} A).
$$

## 4.2 Orthogonal transformations

In order to construct efficient algorithms for solving LS problems, we introduce some well-known orthogonal transformations. 

## 4.2.1 Householder transformation

We first introduce the following definition of Householder transformation. 

Definition 4.3 Let $\mathbf{w} \in \mathbb{R}^n$ with $\| \mathbf{w} \|_2 = 1$ . Define $H \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ as follows: 

$$
H = I - 2 \mathbf {w} \mathbf {w} ^ {T}.\tag{4.6}
$$

The matrix $H$ is called the Householder transformation. 

Theorem 4.7 Let $H$ be defined as in (4.6). Then $H$ has the following properties: 

(i) $H$ is a symmetric orthogonal matrix. 

(ii) $H^{2} = I$ . 

(iii) $H$ is called a reflection because $H\mathbf{x}$ is the reflection of $\mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{R}^n$ in the plane through $\mathbf{0}$ perpendicular to $\mathbf{w}$ . 

Proof We only prove (iii). Note that for any vector $\mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{R}^n$ , it can be expressed as: 

$$
\mathbf {x} = \mathbf {u} + \alpha \mathbf {w},
$$

where $\mathbf{u} \in \operatorname{span}\{\mathbf{w}\}^{\perp}$ and $\alpha \in \mathbb{R}$ . By using $\mathbf{u}^T\mathbf{w} = 0$ and $\mathbf{w}^T\mathbf{w} = 1$ , we have 

$$
H \mathbf {x} = (I - 2 \mathbf {w w} ^ {T}) (\mathbf {u} + \alpha \mathbf {w}) = \mathbf {u} + \alpha \mathbf {w} - 2 \mathbf {w w} ^ {T} \mathbf {u} - 2 \alpha \mathbf {w w} ^ {T} \mathbf {w} = \mathbf {u} - \alpha \mathbf {w}.
$$

Theorem 4.8 For any $\mathbf{0} \neq \mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{R}^n$ , we can construct a unit vector $\mathbf{w}$ such that the Householder transformation defined as in (4.6) satisfies 

$$
H \mathbf {x} = \alpha \mathbf {e} _ {1},
$$

where $\alpha = \pm ||\mathbf{x}||_2$ 

Proof Note that $H\mathbf{x} = (I - 2\mathbf{w}\mathbf{w}^T)\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{x} - 2(\mathbf{w}^T\mathbf{x})\mathbf{w}$ . Let 

$$
\mathbf {w} = \frac {\mathbf {x} - \alpha \mathbf {e} _ {1}}{\| \mathbf {x} - \alpha \mathbf {e} _ {1} \| _ {2}}.
$$

We then have 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & H \mathbf {x} = \mathbf {x} - 2 (\mathbf {w} ^ {T} \mathbf {x}) \mathbf {w} \\ & \qquad = \mathbf {x} - \frac {2}{\| \mathbf {x} - \alpha \mathbf {e} _ {1} \| _ {2} ^ {2}} \left[ (\mathbf {x} ^ {T} - \alpha \mathbf {e} _ {1} ^ {T}) \mathbf {x} \right] (\mathbf {x} - \alpha \mathbf {e} _ {1}) \\ & \qquad = \mathbf {x} - \frac {2 (\| \mathbf {x} \| _ {2} ^ {2} - \alpha x _ {1})}{\| \mathbf {x} - \alpha \mathbf {e} _ {1} \| _ {2} ^ {2}} \mathbf {x} + \frac {2 (\| \mathbf {x} \| _ {2} ^ {2} - \alpha x _ {1}) \alpha}{\| \mathbf {x} - \alpha \mathbf {e} _ {1} \| _ {2} ^ {2}} \mathbf {e} _ {1} \\ & \qquad = \left[ 1 - \frac {2 (\| \mathbf {x} \| _ {2} ^ {2} - \alpha x _ {1})}{\| \mathbf {x} - \alpha \mathbf {e} _ {1} \| _ {2} ^ {2}} \right] \mathbf {x} + \frac {2 (\| \mathbf {x} \| _ {2} ^ {2} - \alpha x _ {1}) \alpha}{\| \mathbf {x} - \alpha \mathbf {e} _ {1} \| _ {2} ^ {2}} \mathbf {e} _ {1}, \end{array}\tag{4.7}
$$

where $x_{1}$ is the first component of the vector x. Let the coefficient of x be zero and then we have the following equation: 

$$
1 - \frac {2 (\| \mathbf {x} \| _ {2} ^ {2} - \alpha x _ {1})}{\| \mathbf {x} - \alpha \mathbf {e} _ {1} \| _ {2} ^ {2}} = 0.
$$

Solving this equation for $\alpha$ , we have $\alpha = \pm \| \mathbf{x} \|_2$ . Substituting it into (4.7), we therefore have 

$$
H \mathbf {x} = \mp \| \mathbf {x} \| _ {2} \mathbf {e} _ {1}. \qquad \square
$$

We remark that for any vector $0 \neq x \in R^{n}$ , by Theorem 4.8, one can construct a Householder matrix H such that the last n - 1 components of Hx are zeros. We can use the following two steps to construct the unit vector w of H: 

(1) compute $v = x \pm \|x\|_{2}e_{1}$ ; 

(2) compute $w = v / \|v\|_{2}$ . 

Now a natural question is: how to choose the sign in front of $\|x\|_{2}$ ? Usually, we choose 

$$
\mathbf {v} = \mathbf {x} + \operatorname{sign} (x _ {1}) \| \mathbf {x} \| _ {2} \mathbf {e} _ {1},
$$

## 4.2 Orthogonal transformations

where $x_{1} \neq 0$ is the first component of the vector x [80]. Since 

$$
H = I - 2 \mathbf {w} \mathbf {w} ^ {T} = I - \frac {2}{\mathbf {v} ^ {T} \mathbf {v}} \mathbf {v} \mathbf {v} ^ {T} = I - \beta \mathbf {v} \mathbf {v} ^ {T}
$$

where $\beta = 2/\mathbf{v}^{T}\mathbf{v}$ , we only need to compute $\beta$ and v instead of forming w. Thus, we have the following algorithm. 

Algorithm 4.1 (Householder transformation) 

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l} \text {function:} [ \mathbf {v}, \beta ] = \text {house} (\mathbf {x}) \\ \quad n = \operatorname{length} (\mathbf {x}) \\ \quad \sigma = \mathbf {x} (2: n) ^ {T} \mathbf {x} (2: n) \\ \quad \mathbf {v} (1) = \mathbf {x} (1) + \operatorname{sign} (\mathbf {x} (1)) \sqrt {\mathbf {x} (1) ^ {2} + \sigma} \\ \quad \mathbf {v} (2: n) = \mathbf {x} (2: n) \\ \quad \text {if} \sigma = 0 \\ \quad \beta = 0 \\ \quad \text {else} \\ \quad \beta = 2 / (\mathbf {v} (1) ^ {2} + \sigma) \\ \quad \text {end} \end{array} \right.
$$

## 4.2.2 Givens rotation

A Givens rotation is defined as follows: 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & G (i, k, \theta) = I + s (\mathbf {e} _ {i} \mathbf {e} _ {k} ^ {T} - \mathbf {e} _ {k} \mathbf {e} _ {i} ^ {T}) + (c - 1) (\mathbf {e} _ {i} \mathbf {e} _ {i} ^ {T} + \mathbf {e} _ {k} \mathbf {e} _ {k} ^ {T}) \\ & = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c c c} 1 & & \vdots & & \vdots & & \\ & \ddots & \vdots & & \vdots & & \\ \dots & \dots & c & \dots & s & \dots & \dots \\ & & \vdots & & \vdots & & \\ \dots & \dots & - s & \dots & c & \dots & \dots \\ & & \vdots & & \vdots & \ddots & \\ & & \vdots & & \vdots & & 1 \end{array} \right], \end{array}
$$

where $c = \cos \theta$ and $s = \sin \theta$ . It is easy to prove that $G(i, k, \theta)$ is an orthogonal matrix. 

Let $\mathbf{x} = [x_1, \ldots, x_n]^T \in \mathbb{R}^n$ and $\mathbf{y} = G(i, k, \theta)\mathbf{x}$ . We then have 

$$
y _ {i} = c x _ {i} + s x _ {k}, \quad y _ {k} = - s x _ {i} + c x _ {k}, \quad y _ {j} = x _ {j}, \quad j \neq i, k.
$$

If we want to make $y_{k} = 0$ , then we only need to take 

$$
c = \frac {x _ {i}}{\sqrt {x _ {i} ^ {2} + x _ {k} ^ {2}}}, \qquad s = \frac {x _ {k}}{\sqrt {x _ {i} ^ {2} + x _ {k} ^ {2}}}.
$$

Therefore, 

$$
y _ {i} = \sqrt {x _ {i} ^ {2} + x _ {k} ^ {2}}, \quad y _ {k} = 0.
$$

We remark that for any vector $0 \neq x \in R^{n}$ , one can construct a Givens rotation $G(i, k, \theta)$ acting on x to make a nonzero component of x be zero. 

## 4.3 QR decomposition

Let $A \in R^{m \times n}$ and $b \in R^{m}$ . By Theorem 3.3 (iii), for any orthogonal matrix Q, we have 

$$
\left\| A \mathbf {x} - \mathbf {b} \right\| _ {2} = \left\| Q ^ {T} (A \mathbf {x} - \mathbf {b}) \right\| _ {2}.
$$

Therefore, the LS problem 

$$
\min _ {\mathbf {x}} \| Q ^ {T} A \mathbf {x} - Q ^ {T} \mathbf {b} \| _ {2}
$$

is equivalent to (4.1). We wish that we could find a suitable orthogonal matrix $Q$ such that the original LS problem becomes an easier solvable LS problem. We have 

Theorem 4.9 (QR decomposition) Let $A \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ with $m \geqslant n$ . Then $A$ has a QR decomposition: 

$$
A = Q \left[ \begin{array}{c} R \\ \mathbf {0} \end{array} \right],\tag{4.8}
$$

where $Q \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times m}$ is an orthogonal matrix and $R \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ is an upper triangular matrix with nonnegative diagonal entries. The decomposition is unique when $m = n$ and $A$ is nonsingular. 

Proof We use induction. When $n = 1$ , we note that it is true by using Theorem 4.8. Now, we assume that the theorem is true for all the matrices in $\mathbb{R}^{p \times (n - 1)}$ with $p \geqslant n - 1$ . Let the first column of $A \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ be $\mathbf{a}_1$ . By Theorem 4.8 again, there exists an orthogonal matrix $Q_1 \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times m}$ such that 

$$
Q _ {1} ^ {T} \mathbf {a} _ {1} = \| \mathbf {a} _ {1} \| _ {2} \mathbf {e} _ {1}.
$$

Therefore, we have 

$$
Q _ {1} ^ {T} A = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \| \mathbf {a} _ {1} \| _ {2} & \mathbf {v} ^ {T} \\ \mathbf {0} & A _ {1} \end{array} \right].
$$

For the matrix $A_{1} \in \mathbb{R}^{(m - 1) \times (n - 1)}$ , we obtain by assumption, 

$$
A _ {1} = Q _ {2} \left[ \begin{array}{c} R _ {2} \\ \mathbf {0} \end{array} \right],
$$

where $Q_{2} \in \mathbb{R}^{(m - 1) \times (m - 1)}$ is an orthogonal matrix and $R_{2}$ is an upper triangular matrix with nonnegative diagonal entries. Thus, let 

$$
Q = Q _ {1} \left[ \begin{array}{c c} 1 & \mathbf {0} \\ \mathbf {0} & Q _ {2} \end{array} \right], \qquad R = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \| \mathbf {a} _ {1} \| _ {2} & \mathbf {v} ^ {T} \\ \mathbf {0} & R _ {2} \\ \mathbf {0} & \mathbf {0} \end{array} \right].
$$

Then $Q$ and $R$ are the matrices satisfying the conditions of the theorem. 

When $A \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times m}$ is nonsingular, we want to show that the QR decomposition is unique. Let 

$$
A = Q R = \widetilde {Q} \widetilde {R},
$$

where $Q$ , $\widetilde{Q} \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times m}$ are orthogonal matrices, and $R$ , $\widetilde{R} \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times m}$ are upper triangular matrices with nonnegative diagonal entries. Since $A$ is nonsingular, we know that the diagonal entries of $R$ and $\widetilde{R}$ are positive. Therefore, the matrices 

$$
\widetilde {Q} ^ {T} Q = \widetilde {R} R ^ {- 1}
$$

are both orthogonal and upper triangular matrices with positive diagonal entries. Thus 

$$
\widetilde {Q} ^ {T} Q = \widetilde {R} R ^ {- 1} = I,
$$

i.e., 

$$
\widetilde {Q} = Q, \qquad \widetilde {R} = R. \qquad \square
$$

A complex version of the QR decomposition is needed later on. 

Corollary 4.2 Let $A \in \mathbb{C}^{m \times n}$ with $m \geqslant n$ . Then $A$ has a QR decomposition: 

$$
A = Q \left[ \begin{array}{c} R \\ \mathbf {0} \end{array} \right],
$$

where $Q \in \mathbb{C}^{m \times m}$ is a unitary matrix and $R \in \mathbb{C}^{n \times n}$ is an upper triangular matrix with nonnegative diagonal entries. The decomposition is unique when $m = n$ and $A$ is nonsingular. 

Now we use the QR decomposition to solve the LS problem (4.1). Suppose that $A \in R^{m \times n}$ ( $m \geqslant n$ ) has linearly independent columns, $b \in R^{m}$ , and A has a QR decomposition (4.8). Let Q be partitioned as 

$$
Q = \left[ Q _ {1} \mid Q _ {2} \right],
$$

and 

$$
Q ^ {T} \mathbf {b} = \left[ \begin{array}{l} Q _ {1} ^ {T} \\ Q _ {2} ^ {T} \end{array} \right] \mathbf {b} = \left[ \begin{array}{l} \mathbf {c} _ {1} \\ \mathbf {c} _ {2} \end{array} \right].
$$

Then 

$$
\left\| A \mathbf {x} - \mathbf {b} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} = \left\| Q ^ {T} A \mathbf {x} - Q ^ {T} \mathbf {b} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} = \left\| R \mathbf {x} - \mathbf {c} _ {1} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2} + \left\| \mathbf {c} _ {2} \right\| _ {2} ^ {2}.
$$

The x is the solution of the LS problem (4.1) if and only if it is the solution of $Rx = c_{1}$ . Note that it is much easier to get the solution of (4.1) by solving $Rx = c_{1}$ since R is an upper triangular matrix. We have the following algorithm for LS problems: 

(1) Compute a QR decomposition of A. 

(2) Compute $\mathbf{c}_1 = Q_1^T\mathbf{b}$ . 

(3) Solve the upper triangular system $R\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{c}_1$ . 

Finally, we discuss how to use Householder transformations to compute the QR decomposition of A. Let m = 7 and n = 5. Assume that we have already found Householder transformations $H_{1}$ and $H_{2}$ such that 

$$
H _ {2} H _ {1} A = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c} \times & \times & \times & \times & \times \\ 0 & \times & \times & \times & \times \\ 0 & 0 & + & \times & \times \\ 0 & 0 & + & \times & \times \\ 0 & 0 & + & \times & \times \\ 0 & 0 & + & \times & \times \\ 0 & 0 & + & \times & \times \end{array} \right].
$$

Now we construct a Householder transformation $\widetilde{H}_3\in \mathbb{R}^{5\times 5}$ such that 

$$
\widetilde {H} _ {3} \left[ \begin{array}{l} + \\ + \\ + \\ + \\ + \end{array} \right] = \left[ \begin{array}{l} \times \\ 0 \\ 0 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{array} \right].
$$

Let $H_{3} = \mathrm{diag}(I_{2},\widetilde{H}_{3})$ . We obtain 

$$
H _ {3} H _ {2} H _ {1} A = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c} \times & \times & \times & \times & \times \\ 0 & \times & \times & \times & \times \\ 0 & 0 & \times & \times & \times \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & \times & \times \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & \times & \times \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & \times & \times \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & \times & \times \end{array} \right].
$$

In general, after $n$ such steps, we can reduce the matrix $A$ into the following form, 

$$
H _ {n} H _ {n - 1} \dots H _ {1} A = {\left[ \begin{array}{l} {R} \\ {\mathbf {0}} \end{array} \right]},
$$

where $R$ is an upper triangular matrix with nonnegative diagonal entries. By setting $Q = H_{1} \cdots H_{n}$ , we obtain 

$$
A = Q \left[ \begin{array}{c} R \\ \mathbf {0} \end{array} \right].
$$

Thus, we have the following algorithm. 

Algorithm 4.2 (QR decomposition: Householder transformation) 

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l} \text {for} j = 1: n \\ \quad [ \mathbf {v}, \beta ] = \text {house} (A (j: m, j)) \\ \quad A (j: m, j: n) = (I _ {m - j + 1} - \beta \mathbf {v v} ^ {T}) A (j: m, j: n) \\ \quad \text {if} j <   m \\ \quad A (j + 1: m, j) = \mathbf {v} (2: m - j + 1) \\ \quad \text {end} \\ \text {end} \end{array} \right.
$$

We remark that the QR decomposition is not only a basic tool for solving LS problems but also an important tool for solving some other fundamental problems in NLA $[20, 23, 24]$ . 

## Exercises:

1. Let $A \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ have full column rank. Prove that $A + E$ also has full column rank if $E$ satisfies $\| E \|_2 \leqslant \frac{1}{\|A^\dagger\|_2}$ , where $A^\dagger = (A^T A)^{-1} A^T$ . 

2. Let $U = [u_{ij}]$ be a nonsingular upper triangular matrix. Show that 

$$
\kappa_ {\infty} (U) \geqslant \frac {\underset {i} {\max} | u _ {i i} |}{\underset {i} {\min} | u _ {i i} |},
$$

where $\kappa_{\infty}(U)=\|U\|_{\infty}\|U^{-1}\|_{\infty}.$ 

3. Let $A \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ with $m \geqslant n$ and have full column rank. Show that 

$$
\left[ \begin{array}{c c} I & A \\ A ^ {T} & \mathbf {0} \end{array} \right] \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {r} \\ \mathbf {x} \end{array} \right] = \left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathbf {b} \\ \mathbf {0} \end{array} \right]
$$

has a solution where x minimizes $\|Ax - b\|_{2}$ . 

4. Let $\mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{R}^n$ and $P$ be a Householder transformation such that 

$$
P \mathbf {x} = \pm \| \mathbf {x} \| _ {2} \mathbf {e} _ {1}.
$$

Let $G_{12}, G_{23}, \ldots, G_{n-1,n}$ be Givens rotations, and let 

$$
Q = G _ {1 2} G _ {2 3} \dots G _ {n - 1, n}.
$$

Suppose $Q\mathbf{x} = \pm \| \mathbf{x}\| _2\mathbf{e}_1$ . Is $P$ equal to $Q$ ? Give a proof or a counterexample. 

5. Let $A \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ . Show that $X = A^{\dagger}$ minimizes $\| AX - I \|_F$ over all $X \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times m}$ . What is the minimum? 

6. Let $\mathbf{x} = \left[ \begin{array}{l}x_{1}\\ x_{2} \end{array} \right]\in \mathbb{C}^{2}$ . Find an algorithm to compute the following unitary matrix 

$$
Q = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} c & \bar {s} \\ - s & c \end{array} \right], \quad c \in \mathbb {R}, \quad c ^ {2} + | s | ^ {2} = 1
$$

such that $Qx = \begin{bmatrix} * \\ 0 \end{bmatrix}$ . 

7. Suppose an $m$ -by- $n$ matrix $A$ has the form 

$$
A = \left[ \begin{array}{c} A _ {1} \\ A _ {2} \end{array} \right],
$$

where $A_{1}$ is an n-by-n nonsingular matrix and $A_{2}$ is an $(m-n)$ -by-n arbitrary matrix. Prove that $\|A^{\dagger}\|_{2}\leqslant\|A_{1}^{-1}\|_{2}$ . 

8. Consider the following well-known ill-conditioned matrix 

$$
A = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c} {1} & {1} & {1} \\ {\epsilon} & {0} & {0} \\ {0} & {\epsilon} & {0} \\ {0} & {0} & {\epsilon} \end{array} \right], \qquad | \epsilon | \ll 1.
$$

(a) Choose a small $\epsilon$ such that $\operatorname{rank}(A) = 3$ . Then compute $\kappa_{2}(A)$ to show that $A$ is ill-conditioned. 

(b) Find the LS solution with A given as above and $b = [3, \epsilon, \epsilon, \epsilon]^{T}$ by using 

(i) the normalized equation method; 

(ii) the QR method. 

9. Let $A = BC$ where $B \in \mathbb{C}^{m \times r}$ and $C \in \mathbb{C}^{r \times n}$ with 

$$
r = \operatorname{rank} (A) = \operatorname{rank} (B) = \operatorname{rank} (C).
$$

Show that 

$$
A ^ {\dagger} = C ^ {*} (C C ^ {*}) ^ {- 1} (B ^ {*} B) ^ {- 1} B ^ {*}.
$$

10. Let $A = U\Sigma V^{*} \in \mathbb{C}^{m \times n}$ , where $U \in \mathbb{C}^{m \times n}$ satisfies $U^{*}U = I$ , $V \in \mathbb{C}^{n \times n}$ satisfies $V^{*}V = I$ and $\Sigma$ is an $n$ -by- $n$ diagonal matrix. Show that 

$$
A ^ {\dagger} = V \Sigma^ {\dagger} U ^ {*}.
$$

11. Prove that 

$$
A ^ {\dagger} = \lim _ {\epsilon \rightarrow 0} (A ^ {*} A + \epsilon I) ^ {- 1} A ^ {*} = \lim _ {\epsilon \rightarrow 0} A ^ {*} (A A ^ {*} + \epsilon I) ^ {- 1}.
$$

12. Show that 

$$
\mathcal {R} (A) \cap \mathcal {N} (A ^ {*}) = \{\mathbf {0} \}.
$$

13. Let $A = [a_{ij}] \in \mathbb{C}^{n \times n}$ be idempotent. Then 

$$
\mathcal {R} (A) \oplus \mathcal {N} (A) = \mathbb {C} ^ {n}, \qquad \operatorname{rank} (A) = \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} a _ {i i}.
$$

14. Let $A \in \mathbb{C}^{m \times n}$ . Prove that 

$$
\mathcal {R} (A A ^ {\dagger}) = \mathcal {R} (A A ^ {*}) = \mathcal {R} (A),
$$

$$
\mathcal {R} (A ^ {\dagger} A) = \mathcal {R} (A ^ {*} A) = \mathcal {R} (A ^ {\dagger}) = \mathcal {R} (A ^ {*}),
$$

$$
\mathcal {N} (A A ^ {\dagger}) = \mathcal {N} (A A ^ {*}) = \mathcal {N} (A ^ {\dagger}) = \mathcal {N} (A ^ {*}),
$$

$$
\mathcal {N} (A ^ {\dagger} A) = \mathcal {N} (A ^ {*} A) = \mathcal {N} (A).
$$

Therefore $AA^{\dagger}$ and $A^{\dagger}A$ are orthogonal projectors. 

15. Prove Corollary 4.2. 

## Chapter 5

## Classical Iterative Methods

We study classical iterative methods for the solution of Ax = b. Iterative methods, originally proposed by Gauss in 1823, Liouville in 1837, and Jacobi in 1845, are quite different from direct methods such as Gaussian elimination. See [2]. 

Direct methods based on an LU factorization of A become prohibitive in terms of computing time and computer storage if the matrix A is quite large. In some practical situation such as the discretization of partial differential equations, the matrix size can be as large as several hundreds of thousands. For such problems, direct methods become impractical. Furthermore, most large problems are sparse, and usually the sparsity is lost during LU factorizations. Therefore, we have to face a very large matrix with many nonzero entries at the end of LU factorizations, and then the storage becomes a crucial issue. For such kind of problems, we can use a class of methods called iterative methods. In this chapter, we only consider some classical iterative methods. 

We remark that the disadvantage with classical iterative methods is that the convergence rate maybe is slow or they may even diverge, and a stopping criterion is needed to be found. 

## 5.1 Jacobi and Gauss-Seidel method

## 5.1.1 Jacobi method

Consider the following linear system 

$$
A \mathbf {x} = \mathbf {b},
$$

where $A = [a_{ij}] \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ . We can write the matrix $A$ in the following form 

$$
A = D - L - U,
$$

where 

$$
D = \operatorname{diag} (a _ {1 1}, a _ {2 2}, \dots , a _ {n n}),
$$

$$
L = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c} 0 & & & & \\ - a _ {2 1} & 0 & & & \\ - a _ {3 1} & - a _ {3 2} & 0 & & \\ \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \ddots & \\ - a _ {n 1} & - a _ {n 2} & \dots & - a _ {n, n - 1} & 0 \end{array} \right],
$$

and 

$$
U = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c} 0 & - a _ {1 2} & - a _ {1 3} & \dots & - a _ {1 n} \\ & 0 & - a _ {2 3} & \dots & - a _ {2 n} \\ & & \ddots & \ddots & \vdots \\ & & & 0 & - a _ {n - 1, n} \\ & & & & 0 \end{array} \right].
$$

Then it is easy to see that 

$$
\mathbf {x} = B _ {J} \mathbf {x} + \mathbf {g},
$$

where 

$$
B _ {J} = D ^ {- 1} (L + U), \quad \mathbf {g} = D ^ {- 1} \mathbf {b}.
$$

The matrix $B_{J}$ is called the Jacobi iteration matrix. The corresponding iteration 

$$
\mathbf {x} _ {k} = B _ {J} \mathbf {x} _ {k - 1} + \mathbf {g}, \quad k = 1, 2, \dots ,\tag{5.1}
$$

is known as the Jacobi method if an initial vector $\mathbf{x}_0 = \left[x_1^{(0)}, x_2^{(0)}, \ldots, x_n^{(0)}\right]^T$ is given. 

## 5.1.2 Gauss-Seidel method

In the Jacobi method, to compute the components of the vector 

$$
\mathbf {x} _ {k + 1} = \left[ x _ {1} ^ {(k + 1)}, x _ {2} ^ {(k + 1)}, \dots , x _ {n} ^ {(k + 1)} \right] ^ {T},
$$

only the components of the vector $x_{k}$ are used. However, note that to compute $x_{i}^{(k+1)}$ , we could use $x_{1}^{(k+1)}, x_{2}^{(k+1)}, \ldots, x_{i-1}^{(k+1)}$ , which were already available for us. Thus a natural modification of the Jacobi method is to rewrite the Jacobi iteration (5.1) in the following form 

$$
\mathbf {x} _ {k} = (D - L) ^ {- 1} U \mathbf {x} _ {k - 1} + (D - L) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {b}, \quad k = 1, 2, \dots\tag{5.2}
$$

The idea is to use each new component as soon as it is available in the computation of the next component. The iteration (5.2) is known as the Gauss-Seidel method. 

Note that the matrix D-L is a lower triangular matrix with $a_{11},\ldots,a_{nn}$ on the diagonal. Because these entries are assumed to be nonzero, the matrix D-L is nonsingular. The matrix 

$$
B _ {G S} = (D - L) ^ {- 1} U
$$

is called the Gauss-Seidel iteration matrix. 

## 5.2 Convergence analysis

## 5.2.1 Convergence theorems

It is often hard to make a good initial approximation $x_{0}$ . Thus, it is nice to have conditions that guarantee the convergence of Jacobi, Gauss-Seidel methods for any arbitrary choice of the initial approximation. 

Both of the Jacobi iteration and the Gauss-Seidel iteration can be expressed by 

$$
\mathbf {x} _ {k + 1} = B \mathbf {x} _ {k} + \mathbf {g}, \quad k = 0, 1, \dots\tag{5.3}
$$

For the Jacobi iteration, we have 

$$
B _ {J} = D ^ {- 1} (L + U), \quad \mathbf {g} = D ^ {- 1} \mathbf {b};
$$

and for the Gauss-Seidel iteration, we have 

$$
B _ {G S} = (D - L) ^ {- 1} U, \quad \mathbf {g} = (D - L) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {b}.
$$

The iteration (5.3) is called linear stationary iteration, where $B \in R^{n \times n}$ is called the iteration matrix, $g \in R^{n}$ the constant term, and $x_{0} \in R^{n}$ the initial vector. In the following, we give a convergence theorem. 

Theorem 5.1 The iteration (5.3) converges with an arbitrary initial guess $x_{0}$ if and only if the matrix $B^{k} \to 0$ as $k \to \infty$ . 

Proof From $\mathbf{x} = B\mathbf{x} + \mathbf{g}$ and $\mathbf{x}_{k + 1} = B\mathbf{x}_k + \mathbf{g}$ , we have 

$$
\mathbf {x} - \mathbf {x} _ {k + 1} = B (\mathbf {x} - \mathbf {x} _ {k}).\tag{5.4}
$$

Because it is true for any value of $k$ , we can write 

$$
\mathbf {x} - \mathbf {x} _ {k} = B (\mathbf {x} - \mathbf {x} _ {k - 1}).\tag{5.5}
$$

Substituting (5.5) into (5.4), we have 

$$
\mathbf {x} - \mathbf {x} _ {k + 1} = B ^ {2} (\mathbf {x} - \mathbf {x} _ {k - 1}).
$$

Continuing this process k times, we can write 

$$
\mathbf {x} - \mathbf {x} _ {k + 1} = B ^ {k + 1} (\mathbf {x} - \mathbf {x} _ {0}).
$$

This shows that $\{\mathbf{x}_k\}$ converges to the solution $\mathbf{x}$ for any choice $\mathbf{x}_0$ if and only if $B^k\to 0$ as $k\to \infty$ . 

## 5.2 Convergence analysis

Recall that $B^{k} \rightarrow 0$ as $k \rightarrow \infty$ if and only if the spectral radius $\rho(B) < 1$ . Since $|\lambda_{i}| \leqslant \|B\|$ , a good way to see whether $\rho(B) < 1$ is to see whether $\|B\| < 1$ by computing $\|B\|$ with a row-sum or column-sum norm. Note that the converse is not true. Combining the result of Theorem 5.1 with the above observation, we have the following theorem. 

Theorem 5.2 The iteration (5.3) converges for any choice of $\mathbf{x}_0$ if and only if $\rho(B) < 1$ . Moreover, if $\|B\| < 1$ for some matrix norm, then the iteration (5.3) converges. 

Let us consider the examples: 

$$
A _ {1} = \left[ \begin{array}{l l l} 1 & 2 & - 2 \\ 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 2 & 2 & 1 \end{array} \right], \qquad A _ {2} = \left[ \begin{array}{l l l} 2 & - 1 & 1 \\ 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & 1 & - 2 \end{array} \right].
$$

It is easy to verify that for $A_{1}$ , the Jacobi method converges even if the Gauss-Seidel method does not. For $A_{2}$ , the Jacobi method diverges while the Gauss-Seidel method converges. 

## 5.2.2 Sufficient conditions for convergence

We now apply Theorem 5.2 to give a sequence of criteria that guarantee the convergence of the Jacobi and (or) Gauss-Seidel methods with any choice of initial approximation $x_{0}$ . 

Definition 5.1 Let $A = [a_{ij}] \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ . If 

$$
|a_{ii}| > \sum_{\substack{j = 1\\ j\neq i}}^{n}|a_{ij}|,\qquad i = 1,2,\ldots ,n,
$$

then the matrix $A$ is called strictly diagonally dominant. The matrix $A$ is called weakly diagonally dominant if 

$$
|a_{ii}|\geqslant \sum_{\substack{j = 1\\ j\neq i}}^{n}|a_{ij}|,\qquad i = 1,2,\ldots ,n,
$$

with at least one strict inequality. 

Theorem 5.3 If $A$ is strictly diagonally dominant, then the Jacobi method converges for any initial approximation $\mathbf{x}_0$ . 

Proof Because $A = [a_{ij}]$ is strictly diagonally dominant, we have 

$$
|a_{ii}| > \sum_{\substack{j = 1\\ j\neq i}}^{n}|a_{ij}|,\qquad i = 1,2,\ldots ,n.
$$

Recall that the Jacobi iteration matrix 

$$
B _ {J} = D ^ {- 1} (L + U)
$$

is given by 

$$
B _ {J} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c} 0 & - \frac {a _ {1 2}}{a _ {1 1}} & \dots & \dots & - \frac {a _ {1 n}}{a _ {1 1}} \\ - \frac {a _ {2 1}}{a _ {2 2}} & 0 & - \frac {a _ {2 3}}{a _ {2 2}} & \dots & - \frac {a _ {2 n}}{a _ {2 2}} \\ \vdots & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & \vdots \\ \vdots & & \ddots & \ddots & - \frac {a _ {n - 1 , n}}{a _ {n - 1 , n - 1}} \\ - \frac {a _ {n 1}}{a _ {n n}} & \dots & \dots & \frac {a _ {n , n - 1}}{a _ {n n}} & 0 \end{array} \right].
$$

We know that the absolute row sum of each row is less than 1, which means 

$$
\| B _ {J} \| _ {\infty} <   1.
$$

Thus by Theorem 5.2, the Jacobi method converges. $\square$ 

Theorem 5.4 If $A = [a_{ij}] \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ is strictly diagonally dominant, then the Gauss-Seidel method converges for any initial approximation $\mathbf{x}_0$ . 

Proof The Gauss-Seidel iteration matrix is given by 

$$
B _ {G S} = (D - L) ^ {- 1} U.
$$

Let $\lambda$ be an eigenvalue of this matrix and $\mathbf{x} = [x_1, x_2, \ldots, x_n]^T$ be the corresponding eigenvector with the largest component having the magnitude 1. Then from 

$$
B _ {G S} \mathbf {x} = \lambda \mathbf {x},
$$

we have 

$$
U \mathbf {x} = \lambda (D - L) \mathbf {x},
$$

i.e., 

$$
- \sum_ {j = i + 1} ^ {n} a _ {i j} x _ {j} = \lambda \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {i} a _ {i j} x _ {j}, \quad 1 \leqslant i \leqslant n,
$$

## 5.2 Convergence analysis

which can be rewritten as 

$$
\lambda a _ {i i} x _ {i} = - \lambda \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {i - 1} a _ {i j} x _ {j} - \sum_ {j = i + 1} ^ {n} a _ {i j} x _ {j}, \quad 1 \leqslant i \leqslant n.\tag{5.6}
$$

Let $x_{k}$ be the largest component having the magnitude 1 of the vector $\mathbf{x}$ . Then by (5.6), we have 

$$
| \lambda | \cdot | a _ {k k} | \leqslant | \lambda | \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {k - 1} | a _ {k j} | + \sum_ {j = k + 1} ^ {n} | a _ {k j} |,
$$

i.e., 

$$
| \lambda | \left(| a _ {k k} | - \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {k - 1} | a _ {k j} |\right) \leqslant \sum_ {j = k + 1} ^ {n} | a _ {k j} |,
$$

or 

$$
| \lambda | \leqslant \frac {\sum_ {j = k + 1} ^ {n} | a _ {k j} |}{| a _ {k k} | - \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {k - 1} | a _ {k j} |}.\tag{5.7}
$$

Since $A$ is strictly diagonally dominant, we have 

$$
\left| a _ {k k} \right| - \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {k - 1} \left| a _ {k j} \right| > \sum_ {j = k + 1} ^ {n} \left| a _ {k j} \right|.
$$

Thus from (5.7), we conclude that $|\lambda| < 1$ , i.e., $\rho(B_{GS}) < 1$ . By Theorem 5.2, the Gauss-Seidel method converges. 

We now discuss the convergence of the Jacobi, Gauss-Seidel methods for the symmetric positive definite matrices. 

Theorem 5.5 Let $A = [a_{ij}] \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ be symmetric with diagonal entries $a_{ii} > 0$ ( $i = 1, 2, \ldots, n$ ). Then the Jacobi method converges if and only if both $A$ and $2D - A$ are positive definite. 

Proof Since 

$$
B _ {J} = D ^ {- 1} (L + U) = D ^ {- 1} (D - A) = I - D ^ {- 1} A,
$$

and 

$$
D = \operatorname{diag} (a _ {1 1}, a _ {2 2}, \dots , a _ {n n})
$$

with $a_{ii} > 0 (i = 1,2,\dots,n)$ , then 

$$
B _ {J} = I - D ^ {- 1} A = D ^ {- 1 / 2} \left(I - D ^ {- 1 / 2} A D ^ {- 1 / 2}\right) D ^ {1 / 2}.
$$

It is easy to see that $I - D^{-1/2}AD^{-1/2}$ symmetric and similar to $B_{J}$ . Then the eigenvalues of $B_{J}$ are real. 

Now, we first suppose that the Jacobi method converges, then $\rho(B_{J}) < 1$ by Theorem 5.2. The absolute value of the eigenvalues of 

$$
I - D ^ {- 1 / 2} A D ^ {- 1 / 2}
$$

is less than 1, i.e., the eigenvalues of $D^{-1/2}AD^{-1/2}$ lies on (0, 2). Thus $A$ is positive definite. On the other hand, the eigenvalues of $2I - D^{-1/2}AD^{-1/2}$ are positive, so the matrix 

$$
2 I - D ^ {- 1 / 2} A D ^ {- 1 / 2}
$$

is positive definite. Since 

$$
D ^ {- 1 / 2} (2 D - A) D ^ {- 1 / 2} = 2 I - D ^ {- 1 / 2} A D ^ {- 1 / 2},
$$

we know that $2D - A$ is positive definite too. 

Conversely, since 

$$
D ^ {1 / 2} (I - B _ {J}) D ^ {- 1 / 2} = D ^ {- 1 / 2} A D ^ {- 1 / 2}
$$

and A is positive definite, it follows that the eigenvalues of $I - B_{J}$ are positive, i.e., the eigenvalues of $B_{J}$ are less than 1. Because 2D - A is positive definite and 

$$
D ^ {- 1 / 2} (2 D - A) D ^ {- 1 / 2} = D ^ {1 / 2} \left(I + B _ {J}\right) D ^ {- 1 / 2},
$$

we can deduce that the eigenvalues of $I + B_{J}$ are positive, i.e., the eigenvalues of $B_{J}$ are greater than -1. Thus $\rho(B_{J}) < 1$ . By Theorem 5.2 again, the Jacobi method converges. ☐ 

Theorem 5.6 Let $A$ be a symmetric positive definite matrix. Then the Gauss-Seidel method converges for any initial approximation $\mathbf{x}_0$ . 

Proof Let $\lambda$ be an eigenvalue of the iteration matrix $B_{GS}$ and $\mathbf{u}$ the corresponding eigenvector. Then 

$$
(D - L) ^ {- 1} U \mathbf {u} = \lambda \mathbf {u}.
$$

Since $A$ is symmetric positive definite, we have $U = L^{T}$ and 

$$
\lambda (D - L) \mathbf {u} = L ^ {T} \mathbf {u}.
$$

Therefore, 

$$
\lambda \mathbf {u} ^ {*} (D - L) \mathbf {u} = \mathbf {u} ^ {*} L ^ {T} \mathbf {u}.
$$

Let 

$$
\mathbf {u} ^ {*} D \mathbf {u} = \delta , \quad \mathbf {u} ^ {*} L \mathbf {u} = \alpha + \mathbf {i} \beta .
$$

## 5.2 Convergence analysis

We then have 

$$
\mathbf {u} ^ {*} L ^ {T} \mathbf {u} = (L \mathbf {u}) ^ {*} \mathbf {u} = \overline {{{{\mathbf {u} ^ {*} L \mathbf {u}}}}} = \alpha - \mathbf {i} \beta .
$$

Thus 

$$
\lambda [ \delta - (\alpha + \mathbf {i} \beta) ] = \alpha - \mathbf {i} \beta .
$$

Taking the modulus of both sides, we have 

$$
| \lambda | ^ {2} = \frac {\alpha^ {2} + \beta^ {2}}{(\delta - \alpha) ^ {2} + \beta^ {2}}.
$$

On the other hand, 

$$
0 <   \mathbf {u} ^ {*} A \mathbf {u} = \mathbf {u} ^ {*} (D - L - L ^ {T}) \mathbf {u} = \delta - 2 \alpha .
$$

Hence, 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} (\delta - \alpha) ^ {2} + \beta^ {2} & = \delta^ {2} + \alpha^ {2} + \beta^ {2} - 2 \delta \alpha \\ & = \delta (\delta - 2 \alpha) + \alpha^ {2} + \beta^ {2} \\ & > \alpha^ {2} + \beta^ {2}. \end{array}
$$

So we get $|\lambda| < 1$ . By Theorem 5.2, the Gauss-Seidel method converges. 

Definition 5.2 A matrix $A$ is called irreducible if there is no permutation matrix $P$ such that 

$$
P A P ^ {T} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} A _ {1 1} & A _ {1 2} \\ \mathbf {0} & A _ {2 2} \end{array} \right],
$$

where $A_{11}$ and $A_{22}$ are square matrices. 

We have the following lemma [85]. 

Lemma 5.1 If a matrix $A$ is strictly diagonally dominant; or irreducible and weakly diagonally dominant, then $A$ is nonsingular. 

Furthermore, 

Theorem 5.7 We have 

(i) If $A$ is strictly diagonally dominant, then both the Jacobi and the Gauss-Seidel methods converge. In fact, 

$$
\| B _ {G S} \| _ {\infty} \leqslant \| B _ {J} \| _ {\infty} <   1.
$$

(ii) If $A$ is irreducible and weakly diagonally dominant, then both the Jacobi and the Gauss-Seidel methods converge. Moreover, 

$$
\rho (B _ {G S}) <   \rho (B _ {J}) <   1.
$$

For the proof of this theorem, we refer to [30,85]. 

## 5.3 Convergence rate

We consider a linear stationary iteration method, 

$$
\mathbf {x} _ {k + 1} = M \mathbf {x} _ {k} + \mathbf {g}, \quad k = 0, 1, \dots ,
$$

where $M$ is an $n$ -by- $n$ matrix. If $I - M$ is nonsingular, then there exists a unique solution of 

$$
(I - M) \mathbf {x} _ {*} = \mathbf {g}.
$$

The error vectors $\mathbf{y}_k$ are defined as $\mathbf{y}_k = \mathbf{x}_k - \mathbf{x}_*$ for $k = 0,1,\ldots$ , and then 

$$
\mathbf {y} _ {k} = M \mathbf {y} _ {k - 1} = \dots = M ^ {k} \mathbf {y} _ {0}.
$$

Using matrix and vector norms, we have 

$$
\| \mathbf {y} _ {k} \| \leqslant \| M ^ {k} \| \| \mathbf {y} _ {0} \|
$$

with the equality possible for each k for some vector $y_{0}$ . Thus, if $y_{0}$ is not the zero vector, then $\|M^{k}\|$ gives us a sharp upper-bound estimate for the ratio $\|y_{k}\|/\|y_{0}\|$ . Since the initial vector $y_{0}$ is unknown in practical problems, $\|M^{k}\|$ serves as a measurement of comparison of different iterative methods. 

Definition 5.3 Let $M$ be an $n$ -by- $n$ iteration matrix. If $\| M^k \| < 1$ for some positive integer $k$ , then 

$$
R _ {k} (M) \equiv - \ln [ (\| M ^ {k} \|) ^ {1 / k} ] = - \frac {\ln \| M ^ {k} \|}{k}
$$

is called the average rate of convergence for $k$ iterations of $M$ . 

In terms of actual computations, the significance of the average rate of convergence $R_{k}(M)$ is given as follows. Clearly, the quantity 

$$
\sigma = \left(\frac {\| \mathbf {y} _ {k} \|}{\| \mathbf {y} _ {0} \|}\right) ^ {1 / k}
$$

is the average reduction factor per iteration for the norm of error. If $\|M^{k}\| < 1$ , then by Definition 5.3, we have 

$$
\sigma \leqslant (\| M ^ {k} \|) ^ {1 / k} = e ^ {- R _ {k} (M)},
$$

where $e$ is the base of the natural logarithm. 

If $M$ is symmetric (or Hermitian, or normal, i.e., $MM^{*} = M^{*}M$ ), by using the spectral radius of the iteration matrix $M$ , we then have 

$$
\| M ^ {k} \| _ {2} = [ \rho (M) ] ^ {k},
$$

## 5.4 SOR method

and thus, 

$$
R _ {k} (M) = - \ln \rho (M),
$$

which is independent of k. 

Next we consider the asymptotic convergence rate 

$$
R _ {\infty} (M) \equiv \lim _ {k \rightarrow \infty} R _ {k} (M).
$$

Theorem 5.8 We have 

$$
R _ {\infty} (M) = - \ln \rho (M).
$$

Proof We only need to prove that 

$$
\lim _ {k \rightarrow \infty} \| M ^ {k} \| ^ {1 / k} = \rho (M).
$$

Since 

$$
[ \rho (M) ] ^ {k} = \rho (M ^ {k}) \leqslant \| M ^ {k} \|,
$$

we have 

$$
\rho (M) \leqslant \| M ^ {k} \| ^ {1 / k}.
$$

On the other hand, for any $\epsilon > 0$ , consider the matrix 

$$
B _ {\epsilon} = \frac {1}{\rho (M) + \epsilon} M.
$$

It is obvious that $\rho(B_{\epsilon}) < 1$ and then $\lim_{k \to \infty} B_{\epsilon}^{k} = 0$ . Hence, there exists a natural number $K$ , for $k \geqslant K$ , we have 

$$
\left\| B _ {\epsilon} ^ {k} \right\| \leqslant 1,
$$

i.e., 

$$
\| M ^ {k} \| \leqslant [ \rho (M) + \epsilon ] ^ {k}.
$$

Thus, 

$$
\rho (M) \leqslant \| M ^ {k} \| ^ {1 / k} \leqslant \rho (M) + \epsilon ,
$$

which means 

$$
\lim _ {k \to \infty} \| M ^ {k} \| ^ {1 / k} = \rho (M). \qquad \square
$$

## 5.4 SOR method

The Gauss-Seidel method is very slow when $\rho(B_{GS})$ is close to unity. However, the convergence rate of the Gauss-Seidel iteration, in certain cases, can be improved by introducing a parameter $\omega$ , known as the relaxation parameter. This method is called the successive overrelaxation (SOR) method. 

## 5.4.1 Iterative form

Let $A = [a_{ij}] \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ and $A = D - L - U$ defined as in Subsection 5.1.1. Consider the solution of $A\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{b}$ again. The motivation of the SOR method is to improve the Gauss-Seidel iteration by taking an appropriately weighted average of the $x_i^{(k)}$ and $x_i^{(k+1)}$ yielding the following algorithm 

$$
x _ {i} ^ {(k + 1)} = (1 - \omega) x _ {i} ^ {(k)} + \omega \left(\sum_ {j = 1} ^ {i - 1} c _ {i j} x _ {j} ^ {(k + 1)} + \sum_ {j = i + 1} ^ {n} c _ {i j} x _ {j} ^ {(k)} + g _ {i}\right).\tag{5.8}
$$

Here $D^{-1}(L+U)=[c_{ij}]$ , $\mathbf{x}_{k}=\left[x_{1}^{(k)},x_{2}^{(k)},\ldots,x_{n}^{(k)}\right]^{T}$ , and $g=D^{-1}b=[g_{1},g_{2},\ldots,g_{n}]^{T}$ . In matrix form, we have 

$$
\mathbf {x} _ {k + 1} = L _ {\omega} \mathbf {x} _ {k} + \omega (D - \omega L) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {b},
$$

where 

$$
L _ {\omega} = (D - \omega L) ^ {- 1} [ (1 - \omega) D + \omega U ]
$$

is called the iteration matrix of the SOR method and $\omega$ is the relaxation parameter. 

We have three cases depending on the values of $\omega$ : 

(1) if $\omega = 1$ , (5.8) is equivalent to the Gauss-Seidel method; 

(2) if $\omega < 1$ , (5.8) is called underrelaxation; 

(3) if $\omega > 1$ , (5.8) is called overrelaxation. 

## 5.4.2 Convergence criteria

It is natural to ask for which range of $\omega$ the SOR iteration converges. To this end, we first prove the following important result [30,85]. 

Theorem 5.9 The SOR iteration cannot converge for any initial approximation if $\omega$ lies outside the interval (0,2). 

Proof Recall that the SOR iteration matrix $L_{\omega}$ is given by 

$$
L _ {\omega} = (D - \omega L) ^ {- 1} [ (1 - \omega) D + \omega U ],
$$

where $A = [a_{ij}] = D - L - U$ . 

The matrix $(D-\omega L)^{-1}$ is a lower triangular matrix with $1/a_{ii}$ ( $i=1,2,\ldots,n$ ) as diagonal entries, and the matrix 

$$
(1 - \omega) D + \omega U
$$

is an upper triangular matrix with $(1-\omega)a_{ii}$ ( $i=1,2,\ldots,n$ ) as diagonal entries. Therefore, 

$$
\det (L _ {\omega}) = (1 - \omega) ^ {n}.
$$

Since the determinant of a matrix is equal to the product of its eigenvalues, we conclude that 

$$
\rho (L _ {\omega}) \geqslant | 1 - \omega |,
$$

where $\rho(L_{\omega})$ is the spectral radius of $L_{\omega}$ . By Theorem 5.2, the spectral radius of the iteration matrix should be less than 1 for convergence. We then conclude that $0 < \omega < 2$ is required for the convergence of the SOR method. ☐ 

Theorem 5.10 If $A$ is symmetric positive definite, then 

$$
\rho (L _ {\omega}) <   1
$$

for all $0 < \omega < 2$ . 

Proof Let $\lambda$ be any eigenvalue of the SOR iteration matrix 

$$
L _ {\omega} = (D - \omega L) ^ {- 1} [ (1 - \omega) D + \omega L ^ {T} ]
$$

and $\mathbf{x}$ be the corresponding eigenvector. We have 

$$
[ (1 - \omega) D + \omega L ^ {T} ] \mathbf {x} = \lambda (D - \omega L) \mathbf {x}
$$

or 

$$
\mathbf {x} ^ {*} [ (1 - \omega) D + \omega L ^ {T} ] \mathbf {x} = \lambda \mathbf {x} ^ {*} (D - \omega L) \mathbf {x}.
$$

Let 

$$
\mathbf {x} ^ {*} D \mathbf {x} = \delta , \quad \mathbf {x} ^ {*} L \mathbf {x} = \alpha + \mathrm{i} \beta .
$$

Therefore, $\mathbf{x}^{*}L^{T}\mathbf{x} = \alpha -\mathbf{i}\beta$ and then 

$$
(1 - \omega) \delta + \omega (\alpha - \mathbf {i} \beta) = \lambda [ \delta - \omega (\alpha + \mathbf {i} \beta) ].
$$

Taking modulus of both sides, we obtain 

$$
| \lambda | ^ {2} = \frac {[ (1 - \omega) \delta + \omega \alpha ] ^ {2} + \omega^ {2} \beta^ {2}}{(\delta - \omega \alpha) ^ {2} + \omega^ {2} \beta^ {2}}.\tag{5.9}
$$

Note that 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & {[ (1 - \omega) \delta + \omega \alpha ] ^ {2} + \omega^ {2} \beta^ {2} - (\delta - \omega \alpha) ^ {2} - \omega^ {2} \beta^ {2}} \\ & {= [ \delta - \omega (\delta - \alpha) ] ^ {2} - (\delta - \omega \alpha) ^ {2}} \\ & {= \omega \delta (\delta - 2 \alpha) (\omega - 2).} \end{array}
$$

Since $A$ is symmetric positive definite, we have 

$$
\delta > 0, \qquad \delta - 2 \alpha > 0.
$$

Therefore, if $0 < \omega < 2$ , we have 

$$
[ (1 - \omega) \delta + \omega \alpha ] ^ {2} + \omega^ {2} \beta^ {2} <   (\delta - \omega \alpha) ^ {2} + \omega^ {2} \beta^ {2}.\tag{5.10}
$$

Thus, for $0 < \omega < 2$ , we obtain by (5.9) and (5.10), 

$$
| \lambda | ^ {2} <   1,
$$

i.e., the SOR method converges. □ 

## 5.4.3 Optimal $\omega$ in SOR iteration

For further comparison of the Jacobi, Gauss-Seidel and SOR methods, we impose another condition on matrices. This condition allows us to compute $\rho(B_{GS})$ and $\rho(L_{\omega})$ explicitly in terms of $\rho(B_{J})$ . 

Definition 5.4 A matrix $M$ has property $A$ if there exists a permutation $P$ such that 

$$
P M P ^ {T} = \left[ \begin{array}{l l} D _ {1 1} & M _ {1 2} \\ M _ {2 1} & D _ {2 2} \end{array} \right],
$$

where $D_{11}$ and $D_{22}$ are diagonal matrices. 

If $M$ has property $A$ , then we can write 

$$
P M P ^ {T} = \widehat {D} - \widehat {L} - \widehat {U},
$$

where 

$$
\widehat {D} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} D _ {1 1} & \mathbf {0} \\ \mathbf {0} & D _ {2 2} \end{array} \right], \qquad \widehat {L} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \mathbf {0} & \mathbf {0} \\ - M _ {2 1} & \mathbf {0} \end{array} \right], \qquad \widehat {U} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \mathbf {0} & - M _ {1 2} \\ \mathbf {0} & \mathbf {0} \end{array} \right].
$$

Let 

$$
\widehat {B _ {J}} (\alpha) \equiv \alpha \widehat {D} ^ {- 1} \widehat {L} + \frac {1}{\alpha} \widehat {D} ^ {- 1} \widehat {U}.
$$

We have 

Theorem 5.11 The eigenvalues of $\widehat{B_J} (\alpha)$ are independent of $\alpha$ . 

Proof Just note that the matrix 

$$
\widehat {B _ {J}} (\alpha) = - \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \mathbf {0} & \frac {1}{\alpha} D _ {1 1} ^ {- 1} M _ {1 2} \\ \alpha D _ {2 2} ^ {- 1} M _ {2 1} & \mathbf {0} \end{array} \right]
$$

is similar to the matrix 

$$
\left[ \begin{array}{c c} I & \mathbf {0} \\ \mathbf {0} & \alpha I \end{array} \right] ^ {- 1} \widehat {B _ {J}} (\alpha) \left[ \begin{array}{c c} I & \mathbf {0} \\ \mathbf {0} & \alpha I \end{array} \right] = - \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \mathbf {0} & D _ {1 1} ^ {- 1} M _ {1 2} \\ D _ {2 2} ^ {- 1} M _ {2 1} & \mathbf {0} \end{array} \right] = \widehat {B _ {J}} (1).
$$

Definition 5.5 Let $M = D - L - U$ and 

$$
B _ {J} (\alpha) = \alpha D ^ {- 1} L + \frac {1}{\alpha} D ^ {- 1} U.
$$

If the eigenvalues of $B_{J}(\alpha)$ are independent of $\alpha$ , then M is called consistent ordering (or said to be consistently ordered). 

Note that $B_{J}(1) = B_{J}$ is the Jacobi iteration matrix. From Theorem 5.11, we know that if M has property A, then $PMP^{T}$ is consistently ordered where P is a permutation matrix such that 

$$
P M P ^ {T} = \left[ \begin{array}{l l} D _ {1 1} & M _ {1 2} \\ M _ {2 1} & D _ {2 2} \end{array} \right]
$$

with $D_{11}$ and $D_{22}$ being diagonal. It is not true that consistent ordering implies property $A$ . 

Example 5.1 Any block tridiagonal matrix 

$$
\left[ \begin{array}{c c c c} D _ {1} & A _ {1} & & \\ B _ {1} & \ddots & \ddots & \\ & \ddots & \ddots & A _ {n - 1} \\ & & B _ {n - 1} & D _ {n} \end{array} \right]
$$

is consistently ordered when $D_{i}$ are diagonal. 

The following theorem gives a relation between the eigenvalues of $B_{J}$ and the eigenvalues of $L_{\omega}$ [30,85]. 

Theorem 5.12 If $A$ is consistently ordered and $\omega \neq 0$ , then 

(i) The eigenvalues of $B_{J}$ appear in $\pm$ pairs. 

(ii) If $\mu$ is an eigenvalue of $B_{J}$ and $\lambda$ satisfies 

$$
(\lambda + \omega - 1) ^ {2} = \lambda \omega^ {2} \mu^ {2},\tag{5.11}
$$

then $\lambda$ is an eigenvalue of $L_{\omega}$ . 

(iii) If $\lambda \neq 0$ is an eigenvalue of $L_{\omega}$ and $\mu$ satisfies (5.11), then $\mu$ is an eigenvalue of $B_{J}$ . 

Corollary 5.1 If $A$ is consistently ordered, then 

$$
\rho (B _ {G S}) = [ \rho (B _ {J}) ] ^ {2}.
$$

This means that the convergence rate of the Gauss-Seidel method is twice as fast as that of the Jacobi method. 

Proof The choice of $\omega = 1$ for the SOR method is equivalent to the Gauss-Seidel method. Therefore, by (5.11), we have $\lambda^2 = \lambda \mu^2$ and then 

$$
\lambda = \mu^ {2}. \quad \square
$$

To get the most benefit from overrelaxation, we would like to find an optimal $\omega$ , denoted by $\omega_{opt}$ , minimizing $\rho(L_{\omega})$ . We have the following theorem [30,85]. 

Theorem 5.13 Suppose that $A$ is consistently ordered and $B_{J}$ has real eigenvalues with $\mu = \rho(B_{J}) < 1$ . Then 

$$
\omega_ {o p t} = \frac {2}{1 + \sqrt {1 - \mu^ {2}}},
$$

$$
\rho (L _ {\omega_ {o p t}}) = \frac {\mu^ {2}}{(1 + \sqrt {1 - \mu^ {2}}) ^ {2}},
$$

and 

$$
\rho (L _ {\omega}) = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \omega - 1, & \omega_ {o p t} \leqslant \omega <   2, \\ 1 - \omega + \frac {1}{2} \omega^ {2} \mu^ {2} + \omega \mu \sqrt {1 - \omega + \frac {1}{4} \omega^ {2} \mu^ {2}}, & 0 <   \omega \leqslant \omega_ {o p t}. \end{array} \right.
$$

For more convergence analysis of classical iterative methods, one can refer to [14, 74]. 

## Exercises:

1. Judge the convergence of the Jacobi method and the Gauss-Seidel method for the following examples: 

$$
A _ {1} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c} 1 & 2 & - 2 \\ 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 2 & 2 & 1 \end{array} \right], \qquad A _ {2} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c} 2 & - 1 & 1 \\ 1 & 1 & 1 \\ 1 & 1 & - 2 \end{array} \right].
$$

2. Show that the Jacobi method converges for 2-by-2 symmetric positive definite systems. 

3. Show that if $A = M - N$ is singular, then we can never have $\rho(M^{-1}N) < 1$ even if $M$ is nonsingular. 

4. Consider $A\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{b}$ where 

$$
A = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c} {1} & {0} & {\alpha} \\ {0} & {1} & {0} \\ {\alpha} & {0} & {1} \end{array} \right].
$$

(1) For which $\alpha$ , is $A$ positive definite? 

(2) For which $\alpha$ , does the Jacobi method converge? 

(3) For which $\alpha$ , does the Gauss-Seidel method converge? 

5. Let $A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ be nonsingular. Show that there exists a permutation matrix $P$ such that the diagonal entries of $PA$ are nonzero. 

6. Prove that 

$$
A = \left[ \begin{array}{r r r r} 4 & - 1 & - 1 & 0 \\ - 1 & 4 & 0 & - 1 \\ - 1 & 0 & 4 & - 1 \\ 0 & - 1 & - 1 & 4 \end{array} \right]
$$

is consistently ordered. 

7. Let $A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ . Then $\rho(A) < 1$ if and only if $I - A$ nonsingular and each eigenvalue of $(I - A)^{-1}(I + A)$ has a positive real part. 

8. Let $B \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ satisfy $\rho(B) = 0$ . Show that for any $\mathbf{g}, \mathbf{x}_0 \in \mathbb{R}^n$ , the iterative formula 

$$
\mathbf {x} _ {k + 1} = B \mathbf {x} _ {k} + \mathbf {g}, \quad k = 0, 1, \dots
$$

converges to the exact solution of $\mathbf{x} = B\mathbf{x} + \mathbf{g}$ for at most $n$ iterations. 

9. If $A = [a_{ij}] \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ is irreducible with $a_{ij} \geqslant 0$ and $B = [b_{ij}] \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ with $b_{ij} \geqslant 0$ . Show that $A + B$ is irreducible. 

10. Let 

$$
A = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c} 0 & a & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & b \\ c & 0 & 0 \end{array} \right].
$$

Is $A^k$ irreducible $(k = 1,2,3)$ ? 

11. Let 

$$
A = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} {2} & {- 1} \\ {1} & {0} \end{array} \right].
$$

Show that 

(1) 

$$
A ^ {k} = k \left[ \begin{array}{c c} 1 + 1 / k & - 1 \\ 1 & 1 / k - 1 \end{array} \right], \qquad k = 1, 2, \ldots
$$

(2) 

$$
\lim _ {k \rightarrow \infty} \frac {\| A ^ {k} \| _ {p}}{k} = 2, \quad p = 1, 2, \infty .
$$

(3) $\rho(A^{k})=1.$ 

12. Let 

$$
B _ {k} = B _ {k - 1} + B _ {k - 1} (I - A B _ {k - 1}), \quad k = 1, 2, \dots
$$

Show that if $\|I - AB_{0}\| = c < 1$ , then 

$$
\lim _ {k \to \infty} B _ {k} = A ^ {- 1}
$$

and 

$$
\| A ^ {- 1} - B _ {k} \| \leqslant \frac {c ^ {2 ^ {k}}}{1 - c} \| B _ {0} \|.
$$

13. Prove Theorem 5.12. 

14. Prove Theorem 5.13. 

## Chapter 6

## Krylov Subspace Methods

In this chapter, we introduce a class of iterative methods called Krylov subspace methods. Among Krylov subspace methods developed for large sparse problems, we mainly study two methods: the conjugate gradient (CG) method and the generalized minimum residual (GMRES) method. The CG method proposed by Hestenes and Stiefel in 1952 is one of the best known iterative methods for solving symmetric positive definite linear systems [37]. The GMRES method was proposed by Saad and Schultz in 1986 for solving nonsymmetric linear systems [71]. As usual, let us begin our discussion from the steepest descent method. 

## 6.1 Steepest descent method

We consider the linear system 

$$
A \mathbf {x} = \mathbf {b},
$$

where $A = [a_{ij}] \in R^{n \times n}$ is a symmetric positive definite matrix, $x = [x_{1}, x_{2}, \ldots, x_{n}]^{T} \in R^{n}$ is an unknown vector, and $b = [b_{1}, b_{2}, \ldots, b_{n}]^{T} \in R^{n}$ is a known vector. We define the following quadratic function 

$$
\phi (\mathbf {x}) \equiv \mathbf {x} ^ {T} A \mathbf {x} - 2 \mathbf {b} ^ {T} \mathbf {x}.\tag{6.1}
$$

Theorem 6.1 Let $A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ be a symmetric positive definite matrix. Then finding the solution of $A\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{b}$ is equivalent to finding the minimum of function (6.1). 

Proof Note that 

$$
\frac {\partial \phi}{\partial x _ {i}} = 2 (a _ {i 1} x _ {1} + \dots + a _ {i n} x _ {n}) - 2 b _ {i}, \quad i = 1, 2, \dots , n.
$$

We therefore have 

$$
\operatorname{grad} \phi (\mathbf {x}) = 2 (A \mathbf {x} - \mathbf {b}) = - 2 \mathbf {r},\tag{6.2}
$$

where $\text{grad}\phi(\mathbf{x})$ denotes the gradient of $\phi(\mathbf{x})$ and $r = b - Ax$ . If $\phi(\mathbf{x})$ reaches its minimum at a point $x_{*}$ , then 

$$
\operatorname{grad} \phi (\mathbf {x} _ {*}) = \mathbf {0},
$$

i.e., $Ax_{*} = b$ which means that $x_{*}$ is the solution of the system. 

Conversely, if $x_{*}$ is the solution of the system, then for any vector y, we have 

$$
