In this section, we introduce the well-known QR method which is one of main important developments in matrix computations. For any given $A_{0} = A \in C^{n \times n}$ , the basic iterative scheme of the QR algorithm is given as follows: 

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l} A _ {m - 1} = Q _ {m} R _ {m}, \\ A _ {m} = R _ {m} Q _ {m}, \end{array} \right.\tag{7.6}
$$

for $m = 1, 2, \ldots$ , where $Q_{m}$ is a unitary matrix and $R_{m}$ is an upper triangular matrix. For simplicity in later analysis, we require that diagonal entries of $R_{m}$ are nonnegative. By (7.6), one can easily obtain 

$$
A _ {m} = Q _ {m} ^ {*} A _ {m - 1} Q _ {m},\tag{7.7}
$$

i.e., each matrix in the sequence $\{A_m\}$ is similar to the matrix $A$ . By using (7.7) again and again, we have 

$$
A _ {m} = \widetilde {Q} _ {m} ^ {*} A \widetilde {Q} _ {m},\tag{7.8}
$$

where $\tilde{Q}_{m}=Q_{1}Q_{2}\cdots Q_{m}$ . Substituting $A_{m}=Q_{m+1}R_{m+1}$ into (7.8), we obtain 

$$
\widetilde {Q} _ {m} Q _ {m + 1} R _ {m + 1} = A \widetilde {Q} _ {m}.
$$

Therefore, 

$$
\widetilde {Q} _ {m} Q _ {m + 1} R _ {m + 1} R _ {m} \dots R _ {1} = A \widetilde {Q} _ {m} R _ {m} \dots R _ {1},
$$

i.e., 

$$
\widetilde {Q} _ {m + 1} \widetilde {R} _ {m + 1} = A \widetilde {Q} _ {m} \widetilde {R} _ {m},
$$

where $\widetilde{R}_{k}=R_{k}R_{k-1}\cdots R_{1}$ for $k=m,m+1$ . Moreover, we have 

$$
A ^ {m} = \widetilde {Q} _ {m} \widetilde {R} _ {m}.\tag{7.9}
$$

Theorem 7.4 Suppose that the eigenvalues of $A$ satisfy: 

$$
\left| \lambda_ {1} \right| > \left| \lambda_ {2} \right| > \dots > \left| \lambda_ {n} \right| > 0.
$$

Let $Y$ be a matrix and $\mathbf{y}_i^T$ denote the ith row of $Y$ satisfying 

$$
\mathbf {y} _ {i} ^ {T} A = \lambda_ {i} \mathbf {y} _ {i} ^ {T}.
$$

If $Y$ has an LU factorization, then the entries under the diagonal of the matrix 

$$
A _ {m} = [ \alpha_ {i j} ^ {(m)} ]
$$

produced by (7.6) tend to zero as $m \rightarrow \infty$ . At the same time, 

$$
\alpha_ {i i} ^ {(m)} \rightarrow \lambda_ {i},
$$

for $i = 1, 2, \ldots, n.$ 

Proof Let 

$$
X = Y ^ {- 1}, \quad \Lambda = \operatorname{diag} (\lambda_ {1}, \dots , \lambda_ {n}).
$$

Then $A = X \Lambda Y$ . By assumption, $Y$ has an LU factorization 

$$
Y = L U,
$$

where $L$ is a unit lower triangular matrix and $U$ is an upper triangular matrix. Hence 

$$
A ^ {m} = X \Lambda^ {m} Y = X \Lambda^ {m} L U = X (\Lambda^ {m} L \Lambda^ {- m}) \Lambda^ {m} U
$$

$$
= X (I + E _ {m}) \Lambda^ {m} U,\tag{7.10}
$$

where $I + E_{m} = \Lambda^{m}L\Lambda^{-m}$ . Since $L$ is a unit lower triangular matrix and $|\lambda_i| < |\lambda_j|$ for $i > j$ , we have 

$$
\lim _ {m \to \infty} E _ {m} = 0.\tag{7.11}
$$

Let $X = QR$ where $Q$ is a unitary matrix and $R$ is an upper triangular matrix. Since $X$ is nonsingular, we can require that diagonal entries of $R$ are positive. Substituting $X = QR$ into (7.10), we obtain 

$$
A ^ {m} = Q R (I + E _ {m}) \Lambda^ {m} U = Q (I + R E _ {m} R ^ {- 1}) R \Lambda^ {m} U.\tag{7.12}
$$

## 7.4 QR method

When $m$ is sufficiently large, $I + RE_{m}R^{-1}$ is nonsingular and has the following QR decomposition, 

$$
I + R E _ {m} R ^ {- 1} = \widehat {Q} _ {m} \widehat {R} _ {m},\tag{7.13}
$$

where diagonal entries of $\widehat{R}_m$ are positive. By using (7.11) and (7.13), it is easy to show that 

$$
\lim _ {m \to \infty} \widehat {Q} _ {m} = \lim _ {m \to \infty} \widehat {R} _ {m} = I.\tag{7.14}
$$

Substituting (7.13) into (7.12), we have 

$$
A ^ {m} = (Q \widehat {Q} _ {m}) (\widehat {R} _ {m} R \Lambda^ {m} U),
$$

which is a QR decomposition of $A^{m}$ . In order to guarantee all the diagonal entries of the upper triangular matrix to be positive, we define 

$$
D _ {1} = \operatorname{diag} \left(\frac {\lambda_ {1}}{| \lambda_ {1} |}, \dots , \frac {\lambda_ {n}}{| \lambda_ {n} |}\right),
$$

and 

$$
D _ {2} = \operatorname{diag} \left(\frac {u _ {1 1}}{| u _ {1 1} |}, \dots , \frac {u _ {n n}}{| u _ {n n} |}\right),
$$

where $u_{ii}$ is the $i$ th diagonal entry of $U$ . Hence, we have 

$$
A ^ {m} = (Q \widehat {Q} _ {m} D _ {1} ^ {m} D _ {2}) (D _ {2} ^ {- 1} D _ {1} ^ {- m} \widehat {R} _ {m} R \Lambda^ {m} U).
$$

Comparing with (7.9) and noting that the QR decomposition is unique, we obtain 

$$
\widetilde {Q} _ {m} = Q \widehat {Q} _ {m} D _ {1} ^ {m} D _ {2}, \quad \widetilde {R} _ {m} = D _ {2} ^ {- 1} D _ {1} ^ {- m} \widehat {R} _ {m} R \Lambda^ {m} U.
$$

Substituting them into (7.8), we have 

$$
A _ {m} = D _ {2} ^ {*} (D _ {1} ^ {*}) ^ {m} \widehat {Q} _ {m} ^ {*} Q ^ {*} A Q \widehat {Q} _ {m} D _ {1} ^ {m} D _ {2}.
$$

Note that 

$$
A = X \Lambda Y = X \Lambda X ^ {- 1} = Q R \Lambda R ^ {- 1} Q ^ {*}.
$$

We finally have 

$$
A _ {m} = D _ {2} ^ {*} (D _ {1} ^ {*}) ^ {m} \widehat {Q} _ {m} ^ {*} R \Lambda R ^ {- 1} \widehat {Q} _ {m} D _ {1} ^ {m} D _ {2}.
$$

When $m \to \infty$ , we know that by (7.14) the entries under the diagonal of the matrix $A_{m}$ produced by (7.6) tend to zero. At the same time, 

$$
\alpha_ {i i} ^ {(m)} \rightarrow \lambda_ {i},
$$

for $i = 1,2,\ldots ,n$ 

From Theorem 7.4, we know that the sequence $\{A_m\}$ produced by (7.6) converges to the Schur decomposition of $A$ . 

## 7.5 Real version of QR algorithm

If $A \in R^{n \times n}$ , we wish that we could find a fast and effective QR algorithm with real number operations only. We concentrate on developing the real analog of (7.6) as follows: let $A_{1} = A$ and then construct an iterative algorithm: 

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l} A _ {m} = Q _ {m} R _ {m}, \\ A _ {m + 1} = R _ {m} Q _ {m}, \end{array} \right.\tag{7.15}
$$

for $m = 1, 2, \ldots$ , where $Q_{m} \in R^{n \times n}$ is an orthogonal matrix and $R_{m} \in R^{n \times n}$ is an upper triangular matrix. A difficulty associated with (7.15) is that $A_{m}$ can never converge to a strictly upper triangular form in the case that A has complex eigenvalues. By Theorem 7.2, we can expect that (7.15) converges to the real Schur decomposition of A. 

We note that in practice that (7.15) is not a good iterative method because the following two reasons: 

(i) the operation cost in each iteration is too large; 

(ii) the convergence rate is too slow. 

We therefore need to reduce the operation cost in each iteration and increase the convergence rate of the method. Thus, the upper Hessenberg reduction and the shift strategy are introduced. 

## 7.5.1 Upper Hessenberg reduction

We construct an orthogonal matrix $Q_{0}$ such that 

$$
Q _ {0} ^ {T} A Q _ {0} = H\tag{7.16}
$$

has some special structure (Hessenberg) with many zero entries. Afterwards, we can apply the QR algorithm (7.15) to the matrix H in (7.16). Then the operation cost per iteration can be dramatically reduced. 

For $A = [\alpha_{ij}] \in R^{n \times n}$ , at the first step, we can choose a Householder transformation $H_{1}$ such that the first column of $H_{1}A$ has many zero entries (at most n - 1 zero entries). In order to keep a similarity transformation, we need to add one more column transformation: 

$$
H _ {1} A H _ {1}.
$$

Hence $H_{1}$ could have the following form 

$$
H _ {1} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} 1 & \mathbf {0} \\ \mathbf {0} & \widetilde {H} _ {1} \end{array} \right]\tag{7.17}
$$

to keep the zero entries in the first column unchanged. By using $H_{1}$ defined as in (7.17), we have 

$$
H _ {1} A H _ {1} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \alpha_ {1 1} & \mathbf {a} _ {2} ^ {T} \widetilde {H} _ {1} \\ \widetilde {H} _ {1} \mathbf {a} _ {1} & \widetilde {H} _ {1} A _ {2 2} \widetilde {H} _ {1} \end{array} \right]\tag{7.18}
$$

where $\mathbf{a}_1^T = [\alpha_{21},\alpha_{31},\dots ,\alpha_{n1}]$ , $\mathbf{a}_2^T = [\alpha_{12},\alpha_{13},\dots ,\alpha_{1n}]$ , and $A_{22}$ is the $(n - 1)$ -by- $(n - 1)$ principal submatrix in the lower right corner of $A$ . We know by (7.18) that the best choice of the Householder transformation $\widetilde{H}_1$ should be 

$$
\tilde {H} _ {1} \mathbf {a} _ {1} = p \mathbf {e} _ {1},\tag{7.19}
$$

where $p \in R$ and $e_{1} \in R^{n-1}$ is the first unit vector. Therefore, the first column of the matrix in (7.18) has n - 2 zeros by using $H_{1}$ defined by (7.17) and (7.19). 

Afterwards, for $\widetilde{A}_{22} = \widetilde{H}_1A_{22}\widetilde{H}_1$ , we could find a Householder transformation 

$$
\widetilde {H} _ {2} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} 1 & \mathbf {0} \\ \mathbf {0} & \widehat {H} _ {2} \end{array} \right]
$$

such that 

$$
(\widetilde {H} _ {2} \widetilde {A} _ {2 2} \widetilde {H} _ {2}) \mathbf {e} _ {1} = [ *, *, 0, \dots , 0 ] ^ {T}.
$$

Let 

$$
H _ {2} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} 1 & \mathbf {0} \\ \mathbf {0} & \widetilde {H} _ {2} \end{array} \right].
$$

We therefore have 

$$
H _ {2} H _ {1} A H _ {1} H _ {2} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c} h _ {1 1} & h _ {1 2} & \dots & * \\ h _ {2 1} & h _ {2 2} & \vdots & \vdots \\ 0 & h _ {3 2} & \vdots & * \\ 0 & 0 & \vdots & * \\ \vdots & \vdots & \vdots & \vdots \\ 0 & 0 & \dots & * \end{array} \right].
$$

After n-2 steps, we have found n-2 Householder transformations $H_{1}, H_{2}, \ldots, H_{n-2}$ , such that 

$$
H _ {n - 2} \cdot \cdot \cdot H _ {2} H _ {1} A H _ {1} H _ {2} \cdot \cdot \cdot H _ {n - 2} = H,
$$

where 

$$
H = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c c} h _ {1 1} & h _ {1 2} & h _ {1 3} & \dots & h _ {1, n - 1} & h _ {1 n} \\ h _ {2 1} & h _ {2 2} & h _ {2 3} & \dots & h _ {2, n - 1} & h _ {2 n} \\ 0 & h _ {3 2} & h _ {3 3} & \dots & h _ {3, n - 1} & h _ {3 n} \\ \vdots & 0 & h _ {4 3} & & \vdots & \vdots \\ \vdots & \vdots & & \ddots & \vdots & \vdots \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & \dots & h _ {n, n - 1} & h _ {n n} \end{array} \right]
$$

with $h_{ij} = 0$ for $i > j + 1$ , and is called the upper Hessenberg matrix. Let 

$$
Q _ {0} = H _ {1} H _ {2} \dots H _ {n - 2}
$$

and therefore, 

$$
Q _ {0} ^ {T} A Q _ {0} = H,
$$

which is called the upper Hessenberg decomposition of $A$ . We have the following algorithm by using Householder transformations. 

Algorithm 7.1 (Upper Hessenberg decomposition) 

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \text {for} & k = 1: n - 2 \\ & [ \mathbf {v}, \beta ] = \text {house} (A (k + 1: n, k)) \\ & A (k + 1: n, k: n) = (I - \beta \mathbf {v v} ^ {T}) A (k + 1: n, k: n) \\ & A (1: n, k + 1: n) = A (1: n, k + 1: n) (I - \beta \mathbf {v v} ^ {T}) \\ \text {end} \end{array} \right.
$$

The operation cost of the Hessenberg decomposition by Householder transformations is $\mathcal{O}(n^{3})$ . Of course, the Hessenberg decomposition can also be obtained by using Givens rotations but the operation cost will be double of that by using Householder transformations. Although the Hessenberg decomposition of a matrix is not unique, we have the following theorem. 

Theorem 7.5 Suppose that $A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ has the following two upper Hessenberg decompositions: 

$$
U ^ {T} A U = H, \quad V ^ {T} A V = G,\tag{7.20}
$$

where 

$$
U = \left[ \mathbf {u} _ {1} \mid \mathbf {u} _ {2} \mid \dots \mid \mathbf {u} _ {n} \right], \quad V = \left[ \mathbf {v} _ {1} \mid \mathbf {v} _ {2} \mid \dots \mid \mathbf {v} _ {n} \right]
$$

are $n$ -by- $n$ orthogonal matrices, and $H = [h_{ij}]$ , $G = [g_{ij}]$ are upper Hessenberg matrices. If $\mathbf{u}_1 = \mathbf{v}_1$ and all the entries $h_{i+1,i}$ are not zero, then there exists a diagonal matrix $D$ with diagonal entries being either 1 or -1 such that 

$$
U = V D, \qquad H = D G D.
$$

Proof Assume that we have proved for some $m, 1 \leqslant m < n$ , that 

$$
\mathbf {u} _ {j} = \epsilon_ {j} \mathbf {v} _ {j}, \quad j = 1, 2, \dots , m,\tag{7.21}
$$

where $\epsilon_{1} = 1$ , $\epsilon_{j} = 1$ or $-1$ ( $j = 2,3,\ldots,m$ ). Now, we want to show that there exists $\epsilon_{m+1} = 1$ or $-1$ such that 

$$
\mathbf {u} _ {m + 1} = \epsilon_ {m + 1} \mathbf {v} _ {m + 1}.
$$

## 7.5 Real version of QR algorithm

From (7.20), we have 

$$
A U = U H, \qquad A V = V G.
$$

Comparing with the mth column of above matrix equalities respectively, we obtain 

$$
A \mathbf {u} _ {m} = h _ {1 m} \mathbf {u} _ {1} + \dots + h _ {m m} \mathbf {u} _ {m} + h _ {m + 1, m} \mathbf {u} _ {m + 1},\tag{7.22}
$$

and 

$$
A \mathbf {v} _ {m} = g _ {1 m} \mathbf {v} _ {1} + \dots + g _ {m m} \mathbf {v} _ {m} + g _ {m + 1, m} \mathbf {v} _ {m + 1}.\tag{7.23}
$$

Multiplying (7.22) and (7.23) by $\mathbf{u}_i^T$ and $\mathbf{v}_i^T$ , respectively, we have 

$$
h _ {i m} = \mathbf {u} _ {i} ^ {T} A \mathbf {u} _ {m}, \qquad g _ {i m} = \mathbf {v} _ {i} ^ {T} A \mathbf {v} _ {m}, \qquad i = 1, 2, \ldots , m.
$$

Therefore by (7.21), 

$$
h _ {i m} = \epsilon_ {i} \epsilon_ {m} g _ {i m}, \quad i = 1, 2, \dots , m.\tag{7.24}
$$

Substituting (7.24) into (7.22), and using (7.21) and (7.23), we obtain 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & h _ {m + 1, m} \mathbf {u} _ {m + 1} = \epsilon_ {m} (A \mathbf {v} _ {m} - \epsilon_ {1} ^ {2} g _ {1 m} \mathbf {v} _ {1} - \dots - \epsilon_ {m} ^ {2} g _ {m m} \mathbf {v} _ {m}) \\ & \qquad = \epsilon_ {m} (A \mathbf {v} _ {m} - g _ {1 m} \mathbf {v} _ {1} - \dots - g _ {m m} \mathbf {v} _ {m}) \\ & \qquad = \epsilon_ {m} g _ {m + 1, m} \mathbf {v} _ {m + 1}. \end{array}\tag{7.25}
$$

Hence, 

$$
\left| h _ {m + 1, m} \right| = \left| g _ {m + 1, m} \right|.
$$

Since $h_{m+1,m} \neq 0$ , from (7.25), we know that 

$$
\mathbf {u} _ {m + 1} = \epsilon_ {m + 1} \mathbf {v} _ {m + 1},
$$

where $\epsilon_{m + 1} = 1$ or -1. By induction, the proof is complete. 

We note that for an upper Hessenberg matrix $H = [h_{ij}]$ , if $h_{i+1,i} \neq 0 (i = 1, 2, \ldots, n-1)$ , then it is irreducible. Theorem 7.5 said that if $Q^{T}AQ = H$ is irreducible upper Hessenberg where Q is an orthogonal matrix, then Q and H are determined completely by the first column of Q (up to $\pm1$ ). 

Now, suppose that $H \in R^{n \times n}$ is an upper Hessenberg matrix, we want to apply a QR iteration to H. Firstly, we need to construct a QR decomposition for H. Since H has a special structure, we can use n - 1 Givens rotations to obtain the QR decomposition. For simplicity, in the following, we consider the case of n = 5. Suppose that we have already found two Givens rotations $P_{12}$ and $P_{23}$ such that 

$$
P _ {2 3} P _ {1 2} H = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c} \times & \times & \times & \times & \times \\ 0 & \times & \times & \times & \times \\ 0 & 0 & h _ {3 3} & \times & \times \\ 0 & 0 & h _ {4 3} & \times & \times \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & \times & \times \end{array} \right].
$$

Then we construct a Givens rotation $P_{34} = G(3, 4, \theta_3)$ such that the $\theta_3$ satisfies 

$$
\left[ \begin{array}{c c} \cos \theta_ {3} & \sin \theta_ {3} \\ - \sin \theta_ {3} & \cos \theta_ {3} \end{array} \right] \left[ \begin{array}{c} h _ {3 3} \\ h _ {4 3} \end{array} \right] = \left[ \begin{array}{c} \times \\ 0 \end{array} \right].
$$

Hence, 

$$
P _ {3 4} P _ {2 3} P _ {1 2} H = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c} \times & \times & \times & \times & \times \\ 0 & \times & \times & \times & \times \\ 0 & 0 & \times & \times & \times \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & \times & \times \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & \times & \times \end{array} \right].
$$

Therefore, it is easy to see that for $n$ -by- $n$ upper Hessenberg matrix $H$ , we can construct $n - 1$ Givens rotations $P_{12}, P_{23}, \ldots, P_{n-1,n}$ such that 

$$
P _ {n - 1, n} P _ {n - 2, n - 1} \dots P _ {1 2} H = R
$$

is an upper triangular matrix. Let 

$$
Q = (P _ {n - 1, n} P _ {n - 2, n - 1} \dots P _ {1 2}) ^ {T}.
$$

Then we have $H = QR$ . In order to complete a QR iteration, we have to compute 

$$
\widetilde {H} = R Q = R P _ {1 2} ^ {T} P _ {2 3} ^ {T} \dots P _ {n - 1, n} ^ {T}.
$$

Note that $RP_{12}^{T}$ is different from R only in the first two columns. Since R is an upper triangular matrix, $RP_{12}^{T}$ should have the following form (for n = 5), 

$$
R P _ {1 2} ^ {T} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c} \times & \times & \times & \times & \times \\ \times & \times & \times & \times & \times \\ 0 & 0 & \times & \times & \times \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & \times & \times \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & 0 & \times \end{array} \right].
$$

Similarly, $RP_{12}^{T}P_{23}^{T}$ is different from $RP_{12}^{T}$ only in the second column and the third column. Hence, 

$$
R P _ {1 2} ^ {T} P _ {2 3} ^ {T} = \left[ \begin{array}{l l l l l} \times & \times & \times & \times & \times \\ \times & \times & \times & \times & \times \\ 0 & \times & \times & \times & \times \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & \times & \times \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & 0 & \times \end{array} \right].
$$

Continuously, we finally obtain the matrix $\widetilde{H}$ which is also an upper Hessenberg matrix. It is easy to know that the operation cost of a QR iteration for an upper Hessenberg matrix is $\mathcal{O}(n^{2})$ . Note that the operation cost of a QR iteration for a general matrix is $\mathcal{O}(n^{3})$ . 

## 7.5.2 QR iteration with single shift

Now, we only need to discuss the Hessenberg form. From Theorem 7.4, we know that the convergence rate of the basic QR algorithm is linear and is depending on the distance between eigenvalues. In order to speed up the convergence rate, we introduce the shift strategy. The QR iteration with a single shift is given as follows: 

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l l} H _ {m} - \mu_ {m} I = Q _ {m} R _ {m}, \\ H _ {m + 1} = R _ {m} Q _ {m} + \mu_ {m} I, \end{array} \right.\tag{7.26}
$$

for $m = 1, 2, \ldots$ , where $H_{1} = H \in R^{n \times n}$ is a given upper Hessenberg matrix satisfying the conditions of Theorem 7.4 and $\mu_{m} \in R$ . We consider how to choose a shift if all the eigenvalues of H are assumed to be real. Since $H_{m}$ is upper Hessenberg, there are only two nonzero entries $h_{n,n-1}^{(m)}$ and $h_{nn}^{(m)}$ in the last row (for n = 5): 

$$
H _ {m} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c} \times & \times & \times & \times & \times \\ \times & \times & \times & \times & \times \\ 0 & \times & \times & \times & \times \\ 0 & 0 & \times & \times & \times \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & h _ {n, n - 1} ^ {(m)} & h _ {n n} ^ {(m)} \end{array} \right].
$$

When the QR algorithm converges, $h_{n,n-1}^{(m)}$ is very small and $h_{nn}^{(m)}$ approaches to an eigenvalue of H. Therefore, we can choose a shift $\mu_{m}=h_{nn}^{(m)}$ . In fact, we can prove that if $h_{n,n-1}^{(m)}=\epsilon$ is very small, then after one iteration of the QR algorithm, we have 

$$
h _ {n, n - 1} ^ {(m + 1)} = \mathcal {O} (\epsilon^ {2}).\tag{7.27}
$$

From the discussion above, we know that there are $n - 1$ steps to reduce $H_{m} - h_{nn}^{(m)}I$ to be an upper triangular matrix. Assume that the first $n - 2$ steps are completed: 

$$
\widehat {H} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c} \times & \times & \times & \times & \times \\ 0 & \times & \times & \times & \times \\ 0 & 0 & \times & \times & \times \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & \alpha & \beta \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & \epsilon & 0 \end{array} \right].
$$

Actually, we only need to study the 2-by-2 submatrix 

$$
H _ {m 2} = \left[ \begin{array}{l l} \alpha & \beta \\ \epsilon & 0 \end{array} \right]
$$

in the lower right corner of the matrix $\widehat{H}$ . In the $(n - 1)$ th step of reduction, we want to determine $c = \cos \theta$ and $s = \sin \theta$ such that 

$$
\left[ \begin{array}{c c} {c} & {s} \\ {- s} & {c} \end{array} \right] \left[ \begin{array}{l} {\alpha} \\ {\epsilon} \end{array} \right] = \left[ \begin{array}{l} {\sigma} \\ {0} \end{array} \right].
$$

It is easy to see that 

$$
c = \alpha / \sigma , \qquad s = \epsilon / \sigma , \qquad \sigma = \sqrt {\alpha^ {2} + \epsilon^ {2}}.
$$

Thus, after a few simple computations on the 2-by-2 submatrix in the lower right corner of the matrix $H_{m+1} = R_m Q_m + h_{nn}^{(m)} I$ , we have 

$$
h _ {n, n - 1} ^ {(m + 1)} = - s ^ {2} \beta = - \beta \epsilon^ {2} / \sigma^ {2} = \mathcal {O} (\epsilon^ {2}),
$$

i.e., (7.27) holds. Through a shift, the convergence rate of the QR iteration is expected to be quadratic. If H has complex eigenvalues, then a double shift strategy can be used to speed up the convergence rate of the QR iteration. 

## 7.5.3 QR iteration with double shift

Note that difficulties with $(7.26)$ can be expected if the submatrix 

$$
G = \left[ \begin{array}{l l} h _ {p p} ^ {(m)} & h _ {p n} ^ {(m)} \\ h _ {n p} ^ {(m)} & h _ {n n} ^ {(m)} \end{array} \right], \qquad p = n - 1
$$

in the lower right corner of $H_{m}$ has a pair of complex conjugate eigenvalues $\mu_{1}$ and $\mu_{2}$ . We cannot expect that $h_{nn}^{(m)}$ tends to an eigenvalue of A. A way around this difficulty is to perform the following QR algorithm with double shifts: 

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l} H - \mu_ {1} I = Q _ {1} R _ {1}, \\ H _ {1} = R _ {1} Q _ {1} + \mu_ {1} I, \\ H _ {1} - \mu_ {2} I = Q _ {2} R _ {2}, \\ H _ {2} = R _ {2} Q _ {2} + \mu_ {2} I, \end{array} \right.\tag{7.28}
$$

where $H = H_{m}$ . Let 

$$
M \equiv (H - \mu_ {1} I) (H - \mu_ {2} I).\tag{7.29}
$$

By a few simple computations, we have 

$$
M = Q R,\tag{7.30}
$$

and 

$$
H _ {2} = Q ^ {T} H Q,\tag{7.31}
$$

where 

$$
Q = Q _ {1} Q _ {2}, \qquad R = R _ {2} R _ {1}.
$$

By (7.29), we obtain 

$$
M = H ^ {2} - s H + t I,
$$

## 7.5 Real version of QR algorithm

where 

$$
s = \mu_ {1} + \mu_ {2} = h _ {p p} ^ {(m)} + h _ {n n} ^ {(m)} \in \mathbb {R},
$$

and 

$$
t = \mu_ {1} \mu_ {2} = \det (G) \in \mathbb {R}.
$$

Hence M is also real. If $\mu_{1}$ , $\mu_{2}$ are not eigenvalues of H and diagonal entries of $R_{1}$ , $R_{2}$ in each iteration are chosen to be positive, then by using (7.30), Q is also real. By (7.31), it then follows that $H_{2}$ is real. Therefore, under an assumption of without rounding error, by using (7.28), $H_{2}$ is still a real upper Hessenberg matrix. In practice, because of rounding error, usually, $H_{2}$ is not real. In order to keep the reality of $H_{2}$ , by using (7.30) and (7.31), we propose the following process to compute $H_{2}$ : 

(1) Compute $M = H^{2} - sH + tI$ . 

(2) Compute the QR decomposition of M: M = QR. 

(3) Compute $H_{2}=Q^{T}HQ$ . 

Note that the operation cost of forming the matrix M in (1) needs $\mathcal{O}(n^{3})$ . We remark that actually, we do not need to form matrix M explicitly [30]. For a practical implementation of the QR algorithm with the shift strategy, we refer to [30, 32, 98]. 

## Exercises:

1. Show that if $T \in \mathbb{C}^{n \times n}$ is upper triangular and normal, then $T$ is diagonal. 

2. Let $A, B \in \mathbb{C}^{n \times n}$ . Prove that the spectrum of $AB$ is equal to the spectrum of $BA$ . 

3. Let $A \in \mathbb{C}^{n \times n}$ , $\mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{C}^n$ and $X = [\mathbf{x} \mid A\mathbf{x} \mid \ldots \mid A^{n-1}\mathbf{x}]$ . Show that if $X$ is nonsingular, then $X^{-1}AX$ is an upper Hessenberg matrix. 

4. Suppose that $A \in \mathbb{C}^{n \times n}$ has distinct eigenvalues. Show that if $Q^{*}AQ = T$ is the Schur decomposition and $AB = BA$ , then $Q^{*}BQ$ is upper triangular. 

5. Suppose that $A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ and $\mathbf{z} \in \mathbb{R}^n$ . Find a detailed algorithm for computing an orthogonal matrix $Q$ such that $Q^T AQ$ is upper Hessenberg and $Q^T \mathbf{z}$ is a multiple of $\mathbf{e}_1$ where $\mathbf{e}_1$ is the first unit vector. 

6. Suppose that $W, Y \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ and define matrices $C, B$ by 

$$
C = W + \mathbf {i} Y, \quad B = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} W & - Y \\ Y & W \end{array} \right].
$$

Show that if $\lambda \in \mathbb{R}$ is an eigenvalue of $C$ , then $\lambda$ is also an eigenvalue of $B$ . What is the relation between two corresponding eigenvectors? 

## 7. Suppose that

$$
A = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} {w} & {x} \\ {y} & {z} \end{array} \right] \in \mathbb {R} ^ {2 \times 2}
$$

has eigenvalues $\lambda \pm \mathbf{i}\mu$ , where $\mu \neq 0$ . Find an algorithm that determines $c = \cos \theta$ and $s = \sin \theta$ stably such that 

$$
\left[ \begin{array}{c c} c & s \\ - s & c \end{array} \right] ^ {T} \left[ \begin{array}{c c} w & x \\ y & z \end{array} \right] \left[ \begin{array}{c c} c & s \\ - s & c \end{array} \right] = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \lambda & \beta \\ \alpha & \lambda \end{array} \right],
$$

where $\alpha\beta = -\mu^{2}$ . 

8. Find a 2-by-2 diagonal matrix $D$ that minimizes $\| D^{-1}AD\|_F$ where 

$$
A = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} {w} & {x} \\ {y} & {z} \end{array} \right].
$$

9. Let $H = H_{1}$ be a given matrix. We generate matrices $H_{k}$ via 

$$
H _ {k} - \mu_ {k} I = Q _ {k} R _ {k}, \quad H _ {k + 1} = R _ {k} Q _ {k} + \mu_ {k} I.
$$

Show that 

$$
(Q _ {1} \dots Q _ {j}) (R _ {j} \dots R _ {1}) = (H - \mu_ {1} I) \dots (H - \mu_ {j} I).
$$

10. Show that if 

$$
Y = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} I & Z \\ \mathbf {0} & I \end{array} \right],
$$

then 

$$
\kappa_ {2} (Y) \equiv \| Y \| _ {2} \| Y ^ {- 1} \| _ {2} = \frac {1}{2} \left(2 + \sigma^ {2} + \sqrt {4 \sigma^ {2} + \sigma^ {4}}\right),
$$

where $\sigma = \|Z\|_{2}$ . 

11. Let $A$ be a matrix with real diagonal entries. Show that 

$$
| \mathrm{Im} (\lambda) | \leqslant \max _ {i} \sum_ {j \neq i} | a _ {i j} |,
$$

where $\lambda$ is any eigenvalue of $A$ and $\operatorname{Im}(\cdot)$ denotes the imaginary part of a complex number. 

12. Show that if 

$$
\frac {1}{2} (A + A ^ {T})
$$

is positive definite, then $\operatorname{Re}(\lambda) > 0$ , where $\lambda$ is any eigenvalue of A and $\operatorname{Re}(\cdot)$ denotes the real part of a complex number. 

13. Let $B$ be a matrix with $\| B \|_2 < 1$ . Show that $I - B$ is nonsingular and the eigenvalues of $I + 2(B - I)^{-1}$ have negative real parts. 

## Chapter 8

## Symmetric Eigenvalue Problems

The symmetric eigenvalue problem with its nice properties and rich mathematical theory is one of the most pleasing topics in NLA. In this chapter, we study symmetric eigenvalue problems. We begin by introducing some basic spectral properties of symmetric matrices. Then the symmetric QR method, the Jacobi method and the bisection method are discussed. Finally, a divide-and-conquer algorithm is described. 

## 8.1 Basic spectral properties

We first introduce some basic properties of eigenvalues and eigenvectors of any symmetric matrix $A \in R^{n \times n}$ . It is well known that the eigenvalues of any symmetric matrix A are real and there is an orthonormal basis of $R^{n}$ formed by the eigenvectors of A. 

Theorem 8.1 (Spectral Decomposition Theorem [39]) If $A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ is symmetric, then there exists an orthogonal matrix $Q \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ such that 

$$
Q ^ {T} A Q = \operatorname{diag} (\lambda_ {1}, \dots , \lambda_ {n}).
$$

The eigenvalues of a symmetric matrix have minimax properties based on the values called the Rayleigh quotient: 

$$
\frac {\mathbf {x} ^ {T} A \mathbf {x}}{\mathbf {x} ^ {T} \mathbf {x}}.
$$

We have the following theorem and its proof can be found in [32]. 

Theorem 8.2 (Courant-Fischer Minimax Theorem) Let $A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ be symmetric and its eigenvalues be ordered as 

$$
\lambda_ {1} \geqslant \dots \geqslant \lambda_ {n}.
$$

Then 

$$
\lambda_ {i} = \max _ {\dim (S) = i} \min _ {\mathbf {0} \neq \mathbf {u} \in S} \frac {\mathbf {u} ^ {T} A \mathbf {u}}{\mathbf {u} ^ {T} \mathbf {u}} = \min _ {\dim (S) = n - i + 1} \max _ {\mathbf {0} \neq \mathbf {u} \in S} \frac {\mathbf {u} ^ {T} A \mathbf {u}}{\mathbf {u} ^ {T} \mathbf {u}},
$$

where S is any subspace of $R^{n}$ . 

The next theorem [95] shows the sensitivity of eigenvalues of symmetric matrices. 

Theorem 8.3 (Weyl, Wielandt-Hoffman Theorem) If $A, B \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ are symmetric matrices and the eigenvalues of $A, B$ are ordered respectively as follows, 

$$
\lambda_ {1} (A) \geqslant \dots \geqslant \lambda_ {n} (A), \quad \mu_ {1} (B) \geqslant \dots \geqslant \mu_ {n} (B),
$$

then 

$$
\left| \lambda_ {i} (A) - \mu_ {i} (B) \right| \leqslant \| A - B \| _ {2}, \quad i = 1, 2, \dots , n,
$$

and 

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \left(\lambda_ {i} (A) - \mu_ {i} (B)\right) ^ {2} \leqslant \| A - B \| _ {F} ^ {2}.
$$

Theorem 8.3 tells us that the eigenvalues of any symmetric matrix are well conditioned, i.e., small perturbations on the entries of A cause only small changes in the eigenvalues of A. 

Theorem 8.4 (Cauchy Interlace Theorem [39,95]) If $A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ is symmetric and $A_r$ denotes the $r$ -by- $r$ leading principal submatrix of $A$ , then 

$$
\lambda_ {r + 1} \left(A _ {r + 1}\right) \leqslant \lambda_ {r} \left(A _ {r}\right) \leqslant \lambda_ {r} \left(A _ {r + 1}\right) \leqslant \dots \leqslant \lambda_ {2} \left(A _ {r + 1}\right) \leqslant \lambda_ {1} \left(A _ {r}\right) \leqslant \lambda_ {1} \left(A _ {r + 1}\right),
$$

for $r = 1, 2, \ldots, n - 1$ . 

As for the sensitivity of eigenvectors, we have the following theorem [95]. 

Theorem 8.5 Suppose $A, A + E \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ are symmetric matrices and 

$$
Q = \left[ \mathbf {q} _ {1} \mid Q _ {2} \right] \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n \times n}
$$

is an orthogonal matrix where $\mathbf{q}_1$ is a unit eigenvector of $A$ . Partition the matrices $Q^T AQ$ and $Q^T EQ$ as follows: 

$$
Q ^ {T} A Q = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} {\lambda} & {\mathbf {0}} \\ {\mathbf {0}} & {D _ {2 2}} \end{array} \right], \qquad Q ^ {T} E Q = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} {\epsilon} & {\mathbf {e} ^ {T}} \\ {\mathbf {e}} & {E _ {2 2}} \end{array} \right],
$$

where $D_{22}, E_{22} \in \mathbb{R}^{(n - 1) \times (n - 1)}$ . If 

$$
d = \min _ {\mu} | \lambda - \mu | > 0, \quad \| E \| _ {2} \leqslant d / 4,
$$

where $\mu$ is any eigenvalue of $D_{22}$ , then there exists a unit eigenvector $\tilde{q}_{1}$ such that 

$$
\sin \theta = \sqrt {1 - | \mathbf {q} _ {1} ^ {T} \tilde {\mathbf {q}} _ {1} | ^ {2}} \leqslant \frac {4}{d} \| \mathbf {e} \| _ {2} \leqslant \frac {4}{d} \| E \| _ {2},
$$

where $\theta = \arccos |\mathbf{q}_1^T\tilde{\mathbf{q}}_1|$ . 

It seems that $\theta$ could be a good measurement between $q_{1}$ and $\tilde{q}_{1}$ . We can see that the sensitivity to a perturbation of a single eigenvector depends on the separation of its corresponding eigenvalue from the rest of eigenvalues. 

The eigenvalues of any symmetric matrix are closely related with the singular values of the matrix. The singular value decomposition [32] is essential in NLA. 

Theorem 8.6 (Singular Value Decomposition Theorem) Let $A \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ with $\operatorname{rank}(A) = r$ . Then there exist orthogonal matrices 

$$
U = \left[ \mathbf {u} _ {1} \mid \mathbf {u} _ {2} \mid \dots \mid \mathbf {u} _ {m} \right] \in \mathbb {R} ^ {m \times m}, \quad V = \left[ \mathbf {v} _ {1} \mid \mathbf {v} _ {2} \mid \dots \mid \mathbf {v} _ {n} \right] \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n \times n}
$$

such that 

$$
U ^ {T} A V = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \Sigma_ {r} & \mathbf {0} \\ \mathbf {0} & \mathbf {0} \end{array} \right],
$$

where 

$$
\Sigma_ {r} = \mathrm{diag} (\sigma_ {1}, \dots , \sigma_ {r})
$$

with $\sigma_{1}\geqslant\sigma_{2}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\sigma_{r}>0.$ 

The $\sigma_{i}$ ( $i = 1, 2, \ldots, n$ ) are called the singular values of A. The vectors $u_{i}$ and $v_{i}$ are called the ith left singular vector and the ith right singular vector respectively. The next corollary is the Weyl, Wielandt-Hoffman Theorem for the singular values. 

Corollary 8.1 Let $A, B \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ and their singular values be ordered respectively as follows: 

$$
\sigma_ {1} (A) \geqslant \dots \geqslant \sigma_ {n} (A), \quad \tau_ {1} (B) \geqslant \dots \geqslant \tau_ {n} (B),
$$

then 

$$
\left| \sigma_ {i} (A) - \tau_ {i} (B) \right| \leqslant \| A - B \| _ {2}, \quad i = 1, 2, \dots , n,
$$

and 

$$
\sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \left(\sigma_ {i} (A) - \tau_ {i} (B)\right) ^ {2} \leqslant \| A - B \| _ {F} ^ {2}.
$$

The corollary shows that the singular values of any real matrix are also well conditioned, i.e., small perturbations on the entries of A cause only small changes in the singular values of A. 

## 8.2 Symmetric QR method

The symmetric QR method is a QR iteration for solving symmetric eigenvalue problem. It applies the QR algorithm to a symmetric matrix A by using its symmetry. In order to construct an efficient QR method, we first reduce the symmetric matrix A to a tridiagonal matrix T and then apply the QR iteration to the matrix T. 

## 8.2.1 Tridiagonal QR iteration

Let $A$ be symmetric and suppose that $A$ can be decomposed as 

$$
Q ^ {T} A Q = T,
$$

where $Q$ is an orthogonal matrix and $T$ is an upper Hessenberg matrix. Then, $T$ should be symmetric tridiagonal. In this case we only need to handle with an eigenvalue problem of a symmetric tridiagonal matrix $T$ . 

Partition A as follows, 

$$
A = \left[ \begin{array}{l l} \alpha_ {1} & \mathbf {v} _ {0} ^ {T} \\ \mathbf {v} _ {0} & A _ {0} \end{array} \right],
$$

where $A_{0} \in \mathbb{R}^{(n-1) \times (n-1)}$ . By using Householder transformations, we can reduce the matrix A into a symmetric tridiagonal matrix as follows: at the kth step, 

(1) Compute a Householder transformation $\tilde{H}_k\in \mathbb{R}^{(n - k)\times (n - k)}$ such that 

$$
\tilde {H} _ {k} \mathbf {v} _ {k - 1} = \beta_ {k} \mathbf {e} _ {1}, \quad \beta_ {k} \in \mathbb {R}.
$$

(2) Compute 

$$
\left[ \begin{array}{c c} \alpha_ {k + 1} & \mathbf {v} _ {k} ^ {T} \\ \mathbf {v} _ {k} & A _ {k} \end{array} \right] = \tilde {H} _ {k} A _ {k - 1} \tilde {H} _ {k},
$$

where $A_{k} \in \mathbb{R}^{(n-k-1) \times (n-k-1)}$ . 

If we use $\alpha_{k},\beta_{k}$ and $\tilde{H}_k$ generated by the reduction above to define 

$$
T = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c} \alpha_ {1} & \beta_ {1} & & \mathbf {0} \\ \beta_ {1} & \alpha_ {2} & \ddots & \\ & \ddots & \ddots & \beta_ {n - 1} \\ \mathbf {0} & & \beta_ {n - 1} & \alpha_ {n} \end{array} \right],
$$

$$
H _ {k} = \operatorname{diag} (I _ {k}, \tilde {H} _ {k}) \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n \times n}, \quad Q = H _ {1} H _ {2} \dots H _ {n - 2},
$$

where 

$$
\left[ \begin{array}{c c} \alpha_ {n - 1} & \beta_ {n - 1} \\ \beta_ {n - 1} & \alpha_ {n} \end{array} \right] = \tilde {H} _ {n - 2} A _ {n - 3} \tilde {H} _ {n - 2},
$$

then we have 

$$
Q ^ {T} A Q = T.
$$

From the reduction above, it is easy to see that the main operation cost of the kth step is to compute $\tilde{H}_{k}A_{k-1}\tilde{H}_{k}$ . Let 

$$
\tilde {H} _ {k} = I - \beta \mathbf {v v} ^ {T}, \qquad \mathbf {v} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n - k}.
$$

## 8.2 Symmetric QR method

By using the symmetry of $A_{k - 1}$ , we then have 

$$
\tilde {H} _ {k} A _ {k - 1} \tilde {H} _ {k} = A _ {k - 1} - \mathbf {v w} ^ {T} - \mathbf {w v} ^ {T},
$$

where 

$$
\mathbf {w} = \mathbf {u} - \frac {1}{2} \beta (\mathbf {v} ^ {T} \mathbf {u}) \mathbf {v}, \quad \mathbf {u} = \beta A _ {k - 1} \mathbf {v}.
$$

Since only the upper triangular portion of this matrix needs to be computed, we see that the transition from $A_{k-1}$ to $A_{k}$ can be computed in $4(n-k)^{2}$ operations only. Given a symmetric matrix $A \in R^{n \times n}$ , the following algorithm overwrites A with $T = Q^{T}AQ$ , where T is a tridiagonal matrix and Q is a product of Householder transformations. 

## Algorithm 8.1 (Householder tridiagonalization)

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \text {for} & k = 1: n - 2 \\ & [ \mathbf {v}, \beta ] = \mathbf {h o u s e} (A (k + 1: n, k)) \\ & \mathbf {u} = \beta A (k + 1: n, k + 1: n) \mathbf {v} \\ & \mathbf {w} = \mathbf {u} - (\beta \mathbf {u} ^ {T} \mathbf {v} / 2) \mathbf {v} \\ & A (k + 1, k) = \| A (k + 1: n, k) \| _ {2} \\ & A (k, k + 1) = A (k + 1, k) \\ & A (k + 1: n, k + 1: n) = A (k + 1: n, k + 1: n) - \mathbf {v w} ^ {T} - \mathbf {w v} ^ {T} \\ \text {end} \end{array} \right.
$$

This algorithm requires $4n^{3}/3$ operations. If Q is explicitly required, then it can be formed with additional $4n^{3}/3$ operations. 

After a symmetric matrix A has been reduced to a symmetric tridiagonal matrix T, our aim turns to choose a suitable shift for the QR iteration. Consider the following QR iteration with a single shift $\mu_{k}$ : 

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l} T _ {k} - \mu_ {k} I = Q _ {k} R _ {k}, \\ T _ {k + 1} = R _ {k} Q _ {k} + \mu_ {k} I, \end{array} \right.\tag{8.1}
$$

for $k = 1, 2, \ldots$ , where $T_{1} = T$ is a symmetric tridiagonal matrix and so is each matrix $T_{k}$ in (8.1). Just as the QR algorithm for nonsymmetric case, $T_{k}$ is assumed to be irreducible, i.e., sub-diagonal entries are nonzero. Let us discuss how to choose the shift $\mu_{k}$ . We can take $\mu_{k} = T_{k}(n, n)$ , the $(n, n)$ th entry at each iteration as a shift. However, a better way is to select 

$$
\mu_ {k} = \alpha_ {n} + \delta - \mathrm{sign} (\delta) \sqrt {\delta^ {2} + \beta_ {n - 1} ^ {2}},
$$

where $\delta = (\alpha_{n-1} - \alpha_n)/2$ . This is the well-known Wilkinson shift [95]. Note that $\mu_k$ is just the eigenvalue of the matrix 

$$
T _ {k} (n - 1: n, n - 1: n) = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} \alpha_ {n - 1} & \beta_ {n - 1} \\ \beta_ {n - 1} & \alpha_ {n} \end{array} \right],
$$

which is closer to $\alpha_{n}$ . 

## 8.2.2 Implicit symmetric QR iteration

For the explicit QR algorithm (8.1), it is possible to execute the transition from 

$$
T - \mu I = Q R
$$

to 

$$
\tilde {T} = R Q + \mu I
$$

without explicitly forming the matrix $T - \mu I$ . The essence of (8.1) is to transform T to $\tilde{T}$ by orthogonal similarity transformations. It follows from Theorem 7.5 that $\tilde{T}$ can be determined completely by the first column of Q. From the process of the QR decomposition of $T - \mu I$ by Givens rotations, we know that 

$$
Q \mathbf {e} _ {1} = G _ {1} \mathbf {e} _ {1},
$$

where $G_{1} = G(1,2,\theta_{1})$ is a rotation which makes the second entry in the first column of $T - \mu I$ to be zero. The $\theta_{1}$ can be computed from 

$$
\left[ \begin{array}{c c} \cos \theta_ {1} & \sin \theta_ {1} \\ - \sin \theta_ {1} & \cos \theta_ {1} \end{array} \right] \left[ \begin{array}{c} \alpha_ {1} - \mu \\ \beta_ {1} \end{array} \right] = \left[ \begin{array}{c} * \\ 0 \end{array} \right].
$$

Let 

$$
B = G _ {1} T G _ {1} ^ {T}.
$$

Then $B$ has the following form $(n = 4)$ , 

$$
B = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c} * & * & + & 0 \\ * & * & * & 0 \\ + & * & * & * \\ 0 & 0 & * & * \end{array} \right].
$$

Let $G_{i} = G(i, i + 1, \theta_{i})$ , $i = 2, 3$ . Then $G_{i}$ of this form can be used to chase the unwanted nonzero entry “+” out of the matrix $B$ as follows: 

$$
B \stackrel {{G _ {2}}} {{\rightarrow}} \left[\begin{array}{c c c c}*&*&0&0\\*&*&*&+\\0&*&*&*\\0&+&*&*\end{array}\right] \stackrel {{G _ {3}}} {{\rightarrow}} \left[\begin{array}{c c c c}*&*&0&0\\*&*&*&0\\0&*&*&*\\0&0&*&*\end{array}\right].
$$

In general, if $Z = G_{1}G_{2} \cdots G_{n-1}$ , then 

$$
Z \mathbf {e} _ {1} = G _ {1} \mathbf {e} _ {1} = Q \mathbf {e} _ {1}
$$

and $ZTZ^{T}$ is tridiagonal. Thus, from Theorem 7.5, the tridiagonal matrix $ZTZ^{T}$ produced by this zero-chasing technique is essentially the same as the tridiagonal matrix T obtained by the explicit method (8.1). Overall, we obtain 

Algorithm 8.2 (Implicit symmetric QR iteration with Wilkinson shift) 

$\left\{ \begin{array}{ll}d = (T(n - 1,n - 1) - T(n,n)) / 2\\ \mu = T(n,n) - T(n,n - 1)^2 /(d + \mathrm{sign}(d)\sqrt{d^2 + T(n,n - 1)^2})\\ x = T(1,1) - \mu ;\quad z = T(2,1)\\ \mathbf{for}\quad k = 1:n - 1\\ [c,s] = \mathbf{givens}(x,z)\\ T = G_kTG_k^T,\quad \text{where}\quad G_k = G(k,k + 1,\theta_k)\\ \mathbf{if}\quad k <   n - 1\\ x = T(k + 1,k);\quad z = T(k + 2,k)\\ \mathbf{end}\\ \mathbf{end} \end{array} \right.$ 

This algorithm requires about 30n operations and n square roots. Of course, the tridiagonal matrix T would be stored in a pair of n-vectors in any practical implementation. 

## 8.2.3 Implicit symmetric QR algorithm

Algorithm 8.2 is a base of the symmetric QR algorithm – the standard means for computing the spectral decomposition of any symmetric matrix. By applying Algorithm 8.2, we develop the following algorithm. 

Algorithm 8.3 (Implicit symmetric QR algorithm) 

(1) Input A (real symmetric matrix). 

(2) Tridiagonalization: Compute the tridiagonalization of $A$ by Algorithm 8.1, 

$$
T = U _ {0} ^ {T} A U _ {0}.
$$

Set $Q = U_{0}$ . 

(3) Criterion for convergence: 

(i) For $i = 1, \ldots, n - 1$ , let $t_{i+1,i}$ and $t_{i,i+1}$ be zero if 

$$
\left| t _ {i + 1, i} \right| = \left| t _ {i, i + 1} \right| \leqslant \left(\left| t _ {i, i} \right| + \left| t _ {i + 1, i + 1} \right|\right) \mu_ {\epsilon},
$$

where $\mu_{\epsilon}$ is the machine precision. 

(ii) Find the largest integer $m \geqslant 0$ and the smallest integer $l \geqslant 0$ such that 

$$
T = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c} T _ {1 1} & \mathbf {0} & \mathbf {0} \\ \mathbf {0} & T _ {2 2} & \mathbf {0} \\ \mathbf {0} & \mathbf {0} & T _ {3 3} \end{array} \right],
$$

where $T_{11} \in \mathbb{R}^{l \times l}$ , $T_{22} \in \mathbb{R}^{(n - l - m) \times (n - l - m)}$ is an irreducible tridiagonal matrix and $T_{33} \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times m}$ is a diagonal matrix. 

(iii) If $m = n$ , then output; otherwise 

(4) QR iteration: Apply Algorithm 8.2 to $T_{22}$ : 

$$
T _ {2 2} = G T _ {2 2} G ^ {T}, \qquad G = G _ {1} G _ {2} \dots G _ {n - l - m - 1}.
$$

(5) Set $Q = Q\mathrm{diag}(I_l, G, I_m)$ , then go to (3). 

If we only need to compute the eigenvalues, this algorithm requires about $4n^{3}/3$ operations. If we need both the eigenvalues and eigenvectors, it requires about $9n^{3}$ operations. It can be shown [95] that the computed eigenvalues $\tilde{\lambda}_{i} (i = 1, 2, \ldots, n)$ obtained by Algorithm 8.3 satisfy 

$$
Q ^ {T} (A + E) Q = \operatorname{diag} (\tilde {\lambda} _ {1}, \dots , \tilde {\lambda} _ {n}),
$$

where $Q \in R^{n \times n}$ is orthogonal and $\|E\|_{2} \approx \|A\|_{2}\mu_{\epsilon}$ where $\mu_{\epsilon}$ is the machine precision. Using Theorem 8.3, we have 

$$
\left| \lambda_ {i} - \tilde {\lambda} _ {i} \right| \approx \| A \| _ {2} \mu_ {\epsilon}, \quad i = 1, 2, \dots , n,
$$

where each $\lambda_{i}$ is an eigenvalue of A. The absolute error in each $\tilde{\lambda}_{i}$ is small and the relative error is less than the machine precision $\mu_{\epsilon}$ . If $\tilde{Q} = [\tilde{\mathbf{q}}_{1} \mid \tilde{\mathbf{q}}_{2} \mid \ldots \mid \tilde{\mathbf{q}}_{n}]$ is the matrix of computed orthonormal eigenvectors, then the accuracy of each $\tilde{q}_{i}$ depends on the separation of $\lambda_{i}$ from the rest of eigenvalues. 

## 8.3 Jacobi method

The Jacobi method is one of the earliest methods for computing the symmetric eigenvalue problem. It was developed by Jacobi in 1846 (see [32]). It is well-known that a real symmetric matrix can be reduced to a diagonal matrix by orthogonal similarity transformations. The Jacobi method exploits the symmetry of the matrix and chooses suitable rotations to reduce a symmetric matrix to a diagonal form. The Jacobi method is usually much slower than the symmetric QR algorithm. However, the Jacobi method remains to be interesting because the method is capable of programming and inherently parallelism recognized in recent years. 

## 8.3.1 Basic idea

Let $A = [a_{ij}] \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ be a symmetric matrix and 

$$
\operatorname{off}(A)\equiv \Bigl (\| A\|_{F}^{2} - \sum_{i = 1}^{n}a_{ii}^{2}\Bigr)^{1 / 2} = \Bigl (\sum_{i = 1}^{n}\sum_{\substack{j = 1\\ j\neq i}}^{n}a_{ij}^{2}\Bigr)^{1 / 2}.
$$

The idea of the Jacobi method is to systematically reduce the off(A) to be zero. The basic tools for doing this are called Jacobi rotations defined as follows, 

$$
J (p, q, \theta) = I + \sin \theta (\mathbf {e} _ {p} \mathbf {e} _ {q} ^ {T} - \mathbf {e} _ {q} \mathbf {e} _ {p} ^ {T}) + (\cos \theta - 1) (\mathbf {e} _ {p} \mathbf {e} _ {p} ^ {T} + \mathbf {e} _ {q} \mathbf {e} _ {q} ^ {T}),
$$

where p < q and $e_{k}$ denotes the kth unit vector. Note that Jacobi rotations are no different from Givens rotations (see Subsection 4.2.2). We change the name in this section to honour the inventor. The basic step in a Jacobi procedure involves: 

(1) Choose p and q for a rotation with $1 \leqslant p < q \leqslant n$ . 

(2) Compute a rotation angle $\theta$ such that 

$$
\left[ \begin{array}{l l} b _ {p p} & b _ {p q} \\ b _ {q p} & b _ {q q} \end{array} \right] = \left[ \begin{array}{l l} c & s \\ - s & c \end{array} \right] ^ {T} \left[ \begin{array}{l l} a _ {p p} & a _ {p q} \\ a _ {q p} & a _ {q q} \end{array} \right] \left[ \begin{array}{l l} c & s \\ - s & c \end{array} \right]\tag{8.2}
$$

is diagonal, i.e., $b_{pq} = b_{qp} = 0$ , where $c = \cos \theta$ and $s = \sin \theta$ . 

(3) Overwrite A with $B = [b_{ij}] = J^{T}AJ$ , where $J = J(p, q, \theta)$ . 

Note that the matrix B agrees with the matrix A except the pth row (column) and the qth row (column). The relations are: 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & b _ {i p} = b _ {p i} = c a _ {i p} - s a _ {i q}, \quad i \neq p, q, \\ & b _ {i q} = b _ {q i} = s a _ {i p} + c a _ {i q}, \quad i \neq p, q, \\ & b _ {p p} = c ^ {2} a _ {p p} - 2 s c a _ {p q} + s ^ {2} a _ {q q}, \\ & b _ {q q} = s ^ {2} a _ {p p} + 2 s c a _ {p q} + c ^ {2} a _ {q q}, \\ & b _ {p q} = b _ {q p} = (c ^ {2} - s ^ {2}) a _ {p q} + s c (a _ {p p} - a _ {q q}). \end{array}
$$

Let us first consider actual computations of $s = \sin \theta$ and $c = \cos \theta$ such that $b_{pq} = b_{qp} = 0$ in (8.2). Actually it is equivalent to 

$$
a _ {p q} (c ^ {2} - s ^ {2}) + (a _ {p p} - a _ {q q}) c s = 0.\tag{8.3}
$$

If $a_{pq} = 0$ , then we just set $c = 1$ and $s = 0$ . Otherwise define 

$$
\tau = \frac {a _ {q q} - a _ {p p}}{2 a _ {p q}}, \qquad t = \tan \theta = \frac {s}{c},
$$

and conclude from (8.3) that $t$ solves the quadratic equation 

$$
t ^ {2} + 2 \tau t - 1 = 0.
$$

Then, 

$$
t = - \tau \pm \sqrt {1 + \tau^ {2}}.
$$

We select t to be the smaller of the two roots which ensures that $|\theta| \leqslant \pi/4$ and has the effect of minimizing of $\|B - A\|_{F}^{2}$ because 

$$
\| B - A \| _ {F} ^ {2} = 4 (1 - c) \sum_ {\substack {i = 1 \\ i \neq p, q}} ^ {n} (a _ {i p} ^ {2} + a _ {i q} ^ {2}) + \frac {2 a _ {p q} ^ {2}}{c ^ {2}}.
$$

After t is determined, we can obtain c and s from the formulas 

$$
c = \frac {1}{\sqrt {1 + t ^ {2}}}, \qquad s = t c.
$$

We summarize the computation of Jacobi rotations $J(p,q,\theta)$ as follows. Given a symmetric matrix $A \in R^{n \times n}$ and indices p, q with $1 \leqslant p < q \leqslant n$ , the following algorithm computes a cosine-sine pair such that $b_{pq} = b_{qp} = 0$ where $b_{jk}$ is the $(j,k)$ th entry of the matrix $B = J(p,q,\theta)^{T}AJ(p,q,\theta)$ . 

Algorithm 8.4 

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l} \text {function:} [ c, s ] = \mathbf {s y m} (A, p, q) \\ \quad \text {if} A (p, q) \neq 0 \\ \quad \tau = (A (q, q) - A (p, p)) / (2 A (p, q)) \\ \quad \text {if} \tau \geqslant 0 \\ \quad t = 1 / (\tau + \sqrt {1 + \tau^ {2}}) \\ \quad \text {else} \\ \quad t = - 1 / (- \tau + \sqrt {1 + \tau^ {2}}) \\ \quad \text {end} \\ \quad c = 1 / \sqrt {1 + t ^ {2}} \\ \quad s = t c \\ \quad \text {else} \\ \quad c = 1 \\ \quad s = 0 \\ \quad \text {end} \end{array} \right.
$$

Once $J(p,q,\theta)$ is determined, then the updated 

$$
A \gets J (p, q, \theta) ^ {T} A J (p, q, \theta)
$$

can be computed in 6n operations. 

How can we choose the integers p and q? Since the Frobenius norm is preserved by the orthogonal transformation (see Section 3.1), we have $\|B\|_{F} = \|A\|_{F}$ . Note that 

$$
a _ {p p} ^ {2} + a _ {q q} ^ {2} + 2 a _ {p q} ^ {2} = b _ {p p} ^ {2} + b _ {q q} ^ {2} + 2 b _ {p q} ^ {2} = b _ {p p} ^ {2} + b _ {q q} ^ {2},
$$

## 8.3 Jacobi method

and then 

$$
\begin{array}{r c l} \mathrm{off} (B) ^ {2} & = & \| B \| _ {F} ^ {2} - \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} b _ {i i} ^ {2} \\ & = & \| A \| _ {F} ^ {2} - \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} a _ {i i} ^ {2} + (a _ {p p} ^ {2} + a _ {q q} ^ {2} - b _ {p p} ^ {2} - b _ {q q} ^ {2}) \\ & = & \mathrm{off} (A) ^ {2} - 2 a _ {p q} ^ {2}. \end{array}\tag{8.4}
$$

Our goal is to minimize off(B), the best choice of p, q should be 

$$
| a _ {p q} | = \max _ {1 \leqslant i <   j \leqslant n} | a _ {i j} |.
$$

This is the basic idea of the classical Jacobi method. 

## 8.3.2 Classical Jacobi method

Given a symmetric matrix $A \in R^{n \times n}$ and a tolerance $\epsilon > 0$ , the following algorithm overwrites A with $U^{T}AU$ , where U is orthogonal and off( $U^{T}AU$ ) $\leqslant \epsilon \|A\|_{F}$ . 

Algorithm 8.5 (Classical Jacobi method) 

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l} U = I _ {n}; \quad \text { eps } = \epsilon \| A \| _ {F} \\ \text { while } \quad \text { off } (A) > \text { eps } \\ \quad \text { choose } \quad p, q \quad \text { such   that } \quad | a _ {p q} | = \max _ {i \neq j} | a _ {i j} | \\ [ c, s ] = \mathbf {s y m} (A, p, q) \\ A = J (p, q, \theta) ^ {T} A J (p, q, \theta) \\ U = U J (p, q, \theta) \\ \text { end } \end{array} \right.
$$

Since $|a_{pq}|$ is the largest off-diagonal entry, we have 

$$
\mathrm{off} (A) ^ {2} \leqslant N (a _ {p q} ^ {2} + a _ {q p} ^ {2}),
$$

where $N = n(n - 1) / 2$ . It follows from (8.4) that 

$$
\operatorname{off} (B) ^ {2} \leqslant \left(1 - \frac {1}{N}\right) \operatorname{off} (A) ^ {2}.
$$

If $A_{k}$ denotes the matrix $A$ after the $k$ th Jacobi iteration and $A_0 = A$ , we have by induction, 

$$
\operatorname{off} (A _ {k}) ^ {2} \leqslant \left(1 - \frac {1}{N}\right) ^ {k} \operatorname{off} (A _ {0}) ^ {2}.
$$

This implies that the classical Jacobi method converges linearly. However, actually, the asymptotic convergence rate of the Jacobi method is quadratic. We can prove that for $k$ large enough, there is a constant $c$ such that 

$$
\operatorname{off} (A _ {k + N}) \leqslant c \cdot \operatorname{off} (A _ {k}) ^ {2}.
$$

See [32] and references therein. Therefore, the off-diagonal “norm” approaches to zero at a quadratic rate after a sufficient number of iterations. 

Another advantage of the Jacobi method is easy to compute the eigenvectors. If the iteration stops after the kth rotation, we then have 

$$
A _ {k} = J _ {k} ^ {T} J _ {k - 1} ^ {T} \dots J _ {1} ^ {T} A J _ {1} J _ {2} \dots J _ {k}.
$$

Denote 

$$
Q _ {k} = J _ {1} J _ {2} \dots J _ {k}.
$$

Thus 

$$
A Q _ {k} = Q _ {k} A _ {k}.
$$

Since off-diagonal entries of $A_{k}$ are tiny and then diagonal entries of $A_{k}$ are good approximations to the eigenvalues of A, the identity above shows that the columns of $Q_{k}$ are good approximations to the eigenvectors of A and all the approximate eigenvectors are orthonormal. We can obtain $Q_{k}$ , the approximate eigenvectors, during the Jacobi iterative process. 

## 8.3.3 Parallel Jacobi method

The Jacobi method to the symmetric eigenvalue problem is inherently parallelism. To illustrate this, let $A \in R^{8 \times 8}$ be symmetric. If one has a parallel computer with 4 processors, then one can group the 28 subproblems into 7 groups of rotations as follows: 

group (1): (1, 2), (3, 4), (5, 6), (7, 8); 

group (2): $(1,3),(2,4),(5,7),(6,8)$ ; 

group (3): $(1,4)$ , $(2,3)$ , $(5,8)$ , $(6,7)$ ; 

group (4): (1,5),(2,6),(3,7),(4,8); 

group (5): (1,6),(2,5),(3,8),(4,7); 

group (6): $(1,7),(2,8),(3,5),(4,6)$ ; 

group (7): $(1,8)$ , $(2,7)$ , $(3,6)$ , $(4,5)$ . 

Note that all 4 rotations within each group are nonconflicting. For instance, the subproblems $J(2i - 1, 2i, \theta_i)$ ( $i = 1, 2, 3, 4$ ) in the first group can be carried out in parallel. When we compute $J(1, 2, \theta_1)^T AJ(1, 2, \theta_1)$ , it has no effect on the rotations (3, 4), (5, 6) and (7, 8). Then the computation of 

$$
A = A J (1, 2, \theta_ {1}), \qquad A = A J (3, 4, \theta_ {2}),
$$

$$
A = A J (5, 6, \theta_ {3}), \qquad A = A J (7, 8, \theta_ {4})
$$

can be executed in parallel by 4 processors. Similarly, the computation of 

$$
A = J (1, 2, \theta_ {1}) ^ {T} A, \qquad A = J (3, 4, \theta_ {2}) ^ {T} A,
$$

$$
A = J (5, 6, \theta_ {3}) ^ {T} A, \qquad A = J (7, 8, \theta_ {4}) ^ {T} A
$$

can also be carried out in parallel by 4 processors. For the example above, it only needs 1/4 computing time of a computer with a single processor. A parallel Jacobi algorithm can be found in [32]. 

## 8.4 Bisection method

In this section we present the bisection method for symmetric tridiagonal eigenvalue problems. Combining the bisection method with the tridiagonal skill, we can obtain a numerical method for a specified eigenvalue and its corresponding eigenvector of a symmetric matrix. For a given tridiagonal matrix 

$$
T = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c} a _ {1} & b _ {1} & & \mathbf {0} \\ b _ {1} & a _ {2} & \ddots & \\ & \ddots & \ddots & b _ {n - 1} \\ \mathbf {0} & & b _ {n - 1} & a _ {n} \end{array} \right] \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n \times n},\tag{8.5}
$$

we consider the computation of eigenvalues of $T$ . Without loss of generality, we assume that $b_{i} \neq 0 (i = 1,2,\dots,n - 1)$ , i.e., $T$ is an irreducible symmetric tridiagonal matrix. Otherwise, $T$ can be divided into several smaller irreducible symmetric tridiagonal matrices. 

Let $p_i(\lambda)$ be the characteristic polynomial of the $i$ -by- $i$ leading principal submatrix $T_i$ of $T$ for each $i$ . Then these polynomials satisfy a three-term recurrence: 

$$
\begin{array}{l} p _ {0} (\lambda) \equiv 1, \qquad p _ {1} (\lambda) = a _ {1} - \lambda , \\ p _ {i} (\lambda) = (a _ {i} - \lambda) p _ {i - 1} (\lambda) - b _ {i - 1} ^ {2} p _ {i - 2} (\lambda), \qquad i = 2, 3, \ldots , n. \end{array}\tag{8.6}
$$

Since $T$ is symmetric, the roots of polynomial $p_i(\lambda) (i = 1,2,\ldots,n)$ are real. The following interlacing property is very important. 

Theorem 8.7 (Sturm Sequence Property) Let the symmetric tridiagonal matrix $T$ in (8.5) be irreducible. Then the eigenvalues of $T_{i-1}$ strictly separate the eigenvalues of $T_i$ : 

$$
\lambda_ {i} (T _ {i}) <   \lambda_ {i - 1} (T _ {i - 1}) <   \lambda_ {i - 1} (T _ {i}) <   \dots <   \lambda_ {2} (T _ {i}) <   \lambda_ {1} (T _ {i - 1}) <   \lambda_ {1} (T _ {i}).
$$

Moreover, if $s_{n}(\lambda)$ denotes the number of sign changes in the sequence 

$$
\left\{p _ {0} (\lambda), p _ {1} (\lambda), \dots , p _ {n} (\lambda) \right\},
$$

then $s_n(\lambda)$ is equal to the number of eigenvalues of $T$ that are less than $\lambda$ , where $p_i(\lambda)$ are defined by (8.6). If $p_i(\lambda) = 0$ , then $p_{i-1}(\lambda)p_{i+1}(\lambda) < 0$ . 

Proof It follows from Theorem 8.4 that the eigenvalues of $T_{i-1}$ weakly separate those of $T_i$ . Next we show that the separation must be strict. Assume that $p_i(\mu) = p_{i-1}(\mu) = 0$ for some $i$ and $\mu$ . Since $T$ is irreducible and, we note that by (8.6), 

$$
p _ {0} (\mu) = p _ {1} (\mu) = \dots = p _ {i} (\mu) = 0,
$$

which is a contradiction with $p_0(\lambda) \equiv 1$ . Thus we have a strict separation. The assertion about $s_n(\lambda)$ is developed in [95]. 

The bisection method for computing a specified eigenvalue of T can be stated as follows: 

Algorithm 8.6 (Bisection algorithm) Let $\lambda_1 < \lambda_2 < \cdots < \lambda_n$ be the eigenvalues of $T$ , i.e., the roots of $p_n(\lambda)$ , and $\epsilon$ be a tolerance. Suppose that the desired eigenvalue is $\lambda_m$ for a given $m \leqslant n$ . Then 

(1) Find an interval $[l_0, u_0]$ including $\lambda_m$ . Since $|\lambda_i| \leqslant \rho(T) \leqslant \|T\|_\infty$ , we can take $l_0 = -\|T\|_\infty$ and $u_0 = \|T\|_\infty$ . 

(2) Compute $r_{1}=\frac{l_{0}+u_{0}}{2}$ and $s_{n}(r_{1})$ . 

(3) If $s_n(r_1) \geqslant m$ , then $\lambda_m \in [l_0, r_1]$ , set $l_1 = l_0$ and $u_1 = r_1$ ; otherwise $\lambda_m \in [r_1, u_0]$ , set $l_1 = r_1$ and $u_1 = u_0$ . 

(4) If $|l_1 - u_1| < \epsilon$ , take $r_2 = \frac{l_1 + u_1}{2}$ as an approximate value of $\lambda_m$ . Otherwise go to (2). 

From the algorithm above, we can see that the main operation cost is to compute $s_{n}(\mu)$ . However in practice, $s_{n}(\mu)$ cannot be obtained through computing the value of $p_{i}(\mu)$ because it is difficult to evaluate polynomials of high order. In order to avoid such a problem, we define 

$$
q _ {i} (\lambda) = \frac {p _ {i} (\lambda)}{p _ {i - 1} (\lambda)}, \quad i = 1, 2, \dots , n.
$$

From (8.6), we have 

$$
q _ {1} (\lambda) = p _ {1} (\lambda) = a _ {1} - \lambda , \quad q _ {i} (\lambda) = a _ {i} - \lambda - \frac {b _ {i - 1} ^ {2}}{q _ {i - 1} (\lambda)}, \quad i = 2, 3, \dots , n.
$$

It is easy to check that $s_{n}(\mu)$ is exactly the number of negative values in the sequence of $q_{1}(\mu),\ldots,q_{n}(\mu)$ . The following is a practical algorithm for computing $s_{n}(\mu)$ . 

Algorithm 8.7 (Compute sign changes) 

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l} \mathbf {x} = [ a _ {1}, a _ {2}, \dots , a _ {n} ] \\ \mathbf {y} = [ 0, b _ {1}, \dots , b _ {n - 1} ] \\ s = 0; q = \mathbf {x} (1) - \mu \\ \text {for} k = 1: n \\ \quad \text {if} q <   0 \\ \quad s = s + 1 \\ \quad \text {end} \\ \quad \text {if} k <   n \\ \quad \text {if} q = 0 \\ \quad q = | \mathbf {y} (k + 1) | \mu_ {\epsilon} \\ \quad \text {end} \\ \quad q = \mathbf {x} (k + 1) - \mu - \mathbf {y} (k + 1) ^ {2} / q \\ \quad \text {end} \\ \text {end} \end{array} \right.
$$

where $\mu_{\epsilon}$ is the machine precision. 

When $q_{i}(\mu)=0$ , we treat $q_{i}$ as a positive number. Therefore, we can use a small positive number $|b_{i}|\mu_{\epsilon}$ instead of $q_{i}(\mu)$ in Algorithm 8.7. If we store $b_{i}^{2}$ in advance, then Algorithm 8.7 needs 3n operations. If an eigenvalue is computed by using the bisection method m times on average, then the operation cost is 3nm. Thus, it is efficient to compute the eigenvalues of any symmetric tridiagonal matrix by the bisection method. On the other hand, the rounding error analysis shows that the bisection method is numerically stable [95]. 

## 8.5 Divide-and-conquer method

A divide-and-conquer method is a numerical method developed by Dongarra and Sorensen in 1987 for computing all the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of symmetric tridiagonal matrices [32]. The basic idea is to tear the original symmetric tridiagonal matrix into $2^{k}$ symmetric tridiagonal matrices with smaller sizes and then compute the spectral decomposition of each smaller symmetric tridiagonal matrix. Once we obtained these smaller spectral decompositions, we then combine them together to form a spectral decomposition of the original matrix. Thus, this method is suitable for parallel computing. 

## 8.5.1 Tearing

Let $T \in R^{n \times n}$ be given as follows, 

$$
T = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c} a _ {1} & b _ {1} & & \mathbf {0} \\ b _ {1} & a _ {2} & \ddots & \\ & \ddots & \ddots & b _ {n - 1} \\ \mathbf {0} & & b _ {n - 1} & a _ {n} \end{array} \right].
$$

Without loss of generality, assume n = 2m. Let 

$$
\mathbf {v} = [ \underbrace {0 , \ldots , 0} _ {m - 1}, 1, \theta , \underbrace {0 , \ldots , 0} _ {m - 1} ] ^ {T} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n}.
$$

Consider the matrix 

$$
\tilde {T} = T - \rho \mathbf {v v} ^ {T},
$$

where $\theta, \rho \in \mathbb{R}$ are needed to be determined. It is easy to see that $\tilde{T}$ is identical to $T$ except its 4 middle entries: 

$$
\left[ \begin{array}{c c} a _ {m} - \rho & b _ {m} - \rho \theta \\ b _ {m} - \rho \theta & a _ {m + 1} - \rho \theta^ {2} \end{array} \right].
$$

If we set $\rho \theta = b_{m}$ , then 

$$
T = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} T _ {1} & \mathbf {0} \\ \mathbf {0} & T _ {2} \end{array} \right] + \rho \mathbf {v v} ^ {T},
$$

where 

$$
T _ {1} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c} a _ {1} & b _ {1} & & & \mathbf {0} \\ b _ {1} & a _ {2} & b _ {2} & & \\ & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & \\ & & b _ {m - 2} & a _ {m - 1} & b _ {m - 1} \\ \mathbf {0} & & & b _ {m - 1} & \tilde {a} _ {m} \end{array} \right]
$$

and 

$$
T _ {2} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c} \tilde {a} _ {m + 1} & b _ {m + 1} & & & \mathbf {0} \\ b _ {m + 1} & a _ {m + 2} & b _ {m + 2} & & \\ & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & \\ & & b _ {n - 2} & a _ {n - 1} & b _ {n - 1} \\ \mathbf {0} & & & b _ {n - 1} & a _ {n} \end{array} \right]
$$

with 

$$
\tilde {a} _ {m} = a _ {m} - \rho , \qquad \tilde {a} _ {m + 1} = a _ {m + 1} - \rho \theta^ {2}.
$$

Therefore, T is divided into a sum of a partitioned matrix and a rank-one matrix. If we divide $T_{1}$ and $T_{2}$ repeatedly, then finally we can divide T into $2^{k}$ blocks. 

## 8.5.2 Combining

Once we obtained the spectral decompositions of $T_{1}$ and $T_{2}$ : 

$$
Q _ {1} ^ {T} T _ {1} Q _ {1} = D _ {1}, \qquad Q _ {2} ^ {T} T _ {2} Q _ {2} = D _ {2},
$$

where $Q_{1}, Q_{2} \in R^{m \times m}$ are orthogonal matrices, and $D_{1}, D_{2} \in R^{m \times m}$ are diagonal matrices, our aim now is to compute the spectral decomposition of T from that of $T_{1}$ and $T_{2}$ , i.e., to find an orthogonal matrix V such that 

$$
V ^ {T} T V = \operatorname{diag} (\lambda_ {1}, \dots , \lambda_ {n}).
$$

Let 

$$
U = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} {Q _ {1}} & {\mathbf {0}} \\ {\mathbf {0}} & {Q _ {2}} \end{array} \right],
$$

then 

$$
\begin{array}{r c l} U ^ {T} T U & = & \left[ \begin{array}{c c} Q _ {1} & \mathbf {0} \\ \mathbf {0} & Q _ {2} \end{array} \right] ^ {T} \left(\left[ \begin{array}{c c} T _ {1} & \mathbf {0} \\ \mathbf {0} & T _ {2} \end{array} \right] + \rho \mathbf {v v} ^ {T}\right) \left[ \begin{array}{c c} Q _ {1} & \mathbf {0} \\ \mathbf {0} & Q _ {2} \end{array} \right] \\ & = & D + \rho \mathbf {z z} ^ {T}, \end{array}
$$

where 

$$
D = \operatorname{diag} (D _ {1}, D _ {2}), \quad \mathbf {z} = U ^ {T} \mathbf {v}.
$$

Now the problem of finding the spectral decomposition of T is reduced to the problem of computing the spectral decomposition of $D + \rho z z^{T}$ . We consider how to compute the spectral decomposition of $D + \rho z z^{T}$ quickly and stably. 

Lemma 8.1 Let $D = \text{diag}(d_1, \ldots, d_n) \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ with $d_1 > d_2 > \cdots > d_n$ . Assume that $0 \neq \rho \in \mathbb{R}$ and $\mathbf{z} = [z_1, z_2, \ldots, z_n]^T \in \mathbb{R}^n$ with $z_i \neq 0$ for all $i$ . Let $\mathbf{u} \in \mathbb{R}^n$ and $\lambda \in \mathbb{R}$ satisfy 

$$
(D + \rho \mathbf {z} \mathbf {z} ^ {T}) \mathbf {u} = \lambda \mathbf {u}, \quad \mathbf {u} \neq \mathbf {0}.
$$

Then $\mathbf{z}^T\mathbf{u}\neq 0$ and $D - \lambda I$ is nonsingular. 

Proof If $z^{T}u = 0$ , then $Du = \lambda u$ with $u \neq 0$ , i.e., $\lambda$ is the eigenvalue of D and u is the eigenvector associated with $\lambda$ . Since D is a diagonal matrix with distinct entries, there must exist some i such that $d_{i} = \lambda$ and $u = \alpha e_{i}$ with $\alpha \neq 0$ , where $e_{i}$ is the ith unit vector. Thus 

$$
0 = \mathbf {z} ^ {T} \mathbf {u} = \alpha \mathbf {z} ^ {T} \mathbf {e} _ {i} = \alpha z _ {i},
$$

which implies $z_{i}=0$ , a contradiction. Therefore, $z^{T}u\neq0$ . 

On the other hand, if $D - \lambda I$ is singular, then there exists some $i$ such that $\mathbf{e}_i^T (D - \lambda I) = 0$ , and then 

$$
0 = \mathbf {e} _ {i} ^ {T} (D - \lambda I) \mathbf {u} = - \rho \mathbf {z} ^ {T} \mathbf {u e} _ {i} ^ {T} \mathbf {z}.
$$

Since $\rho \mathbf{z}^T\mathbf{u}\neq 0$ , we have $\mathbf{e}_i^T\mathbf{z} = z_i = 0$ , a contradiction. Thus, $D - \lambda I$ is nonsingular. 

Theorem 8.8 Let $D = \text{diag}(d_1, \ldots, d_n) \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ with $d_1 > d_2 > \cdots > d_n$ . Assume that $0 \neq \rho \in R$ and $z = [z_1, z_2, \ldots, z_n]^T \in R^n$ with $z_i \neq 0$ for all i. Suppose that the spectral decomposition of $D + \rho z z^T$ is 

$$
V ^ {T} (D + \rho \mathbf {z z} ^ {T}) V = \operatorname{diag} (\lambda_ {1}, \dots , \lambda_ {n}),
$$

where $V = [v_{1} \mid v_{2} \mid \ldots \mid v_{n}]$ is an orthogonal matrix and $\lambda_{1} \geqslant \cdots \geqslant \lambda_{n}$ . Then 

(i) $\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}$ are n roots of the function 

$$
f (\lambda) = 1 + \rho \mathbf {z} ^ {T} (D - \lambda I) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {z}.
$$

(ii) If $\rho > 0$ , then 

$$
\lambda_ {1} > d _ {1} > \lambda_ {2} > \dots > \lambda_ {n} > d _ {n};
$$

if $\rho < 0$ , then 

$$
d _ {1} > \lambda_ {1} > d _ {2} > \dots > d _ {n} > \lambda_ {n}.
$$

(iii) There exists a constant $\alpha_{i} \neq 0$ such that 

$$
\mathbf {v} _ {i} = \alpha_ {i} (D - \lambda_ {i} I) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {z}, \quad i = 1, 2, \dots , n.
$$

Proof From the assumption, we have 

$$
(D + \rho \mathbf {z} \mathbf {z} ^ {T}) \mathbf {v} _ {i} = \lambda_ {i} \mathbf {v} _ {i}, \quad \| \mathbf {v} _ {i} \| _ {2} = 1.
$$

It follows from Lemma 8.1 that $D - \lambda_{i}I$ is nonsingular. Thus, 

$$
\mathbf {v} _ {i} = - \rho \mathbf {z} ^ {T} \mathbf {v} _ {i} (D - \lambda_ {i} I) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {z}, \quad i = 1, 2, \dots , n,\tag{8.7}
$$

thereby establishing (iii). Note that $D + \rho z z^{T}$ has distinct eigenvalues. Otherwise, if $\lambda_{i} = \lambda_{j}$ , then $v_{i}$ and $v_{j}$ are linearly dependent, which contradicts with the orthogonality between $v_{i}$ and $v_{j}$ . 

By multiplying $\mathbf{z}^T$ to the both sides of (8.7) and noting that $\mathbf{z}^T\mathbf{v}_i\neq 0$ , we have 

$$
1 = - \rho \mathbf {z} ^ {T} (D - \lambda_ {i} I) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {z},
$$

## 8.5 Divide-and-conquer method

i.e., 

$$
f (\lambda_ {i}) = 0, \quad i = 1, 2, \dots , n.
$$

Thus, $\lambda_{i}$ ( $i = 1,2,\ldots,n$ ) are the roots of $f(\lambda)$ . Next we prove that $f(\lambda)$ has exactly $n$ zeros. Note that 

$$
f (\lambda) = 1 + \rho \left(\frac {z _ {1} ^ {2}}{d _ {1} - \lambda} + \dots + \frac {z _ {n} ^ {2}}{d _ {n} - \lambda}\right),
$$

and moreover, 

$$
f ^ {\prime} (\lambda) = \rho \left(\frac {z _ {1} ^ {2}}{(d _ {1} - \lambda) ^ {2}} + \dots + \frac {z _ {n} ^ {2}}{(d _ {n} - \lambda) ^ {2}}\right).
$$

Thus, $f(\lambda)$ is strictly monotone between the poles $d_i$ and $d_{i+1}$ . If $\rho > 0$ , $f(\lambda)$ is strictly increasing; if $\rho < 0$ , $f(\lambda)$ is strictly decreasing. Therefore, it is easy to see that $f(\lambda)$ has exactly $n$ roots, one of each in the intervals 

$$
(d _ {n}, d _ {n - 1}), \dots , (d _ {2}, d _ {1}), (d _ {1}, \infty),
$$

if $\rho > 0$ ; and one of each in the intervals 

$$
(- \infty , d _ {n}), (d _ {n}, d _ {n - 1}), \dots , (d _ {2}, d _ {1}),
$$

if $\rho < 0$ . Thus, (i) and (ii) are established. 

By Theorem 8.8, we can compute the spectral decomposition of $D + \rho z z^{T}$ efficiently in the following two steps: 

(1) Find roots $\lambda_1, \lambda_2, \ldots, \lambda_n$ of $f(\lambda)$ . There is a unique root of $f(\lambda)$ in each of the intervals $(d_{i+1}, d_i)$ and $f(\lambda)$ is strictly monotone in the interval. Thus, this step can be implemented quickly and stably by using a Newton-like method [30]. 

(2) Compute 

$$
\mathbf {v} _ {i} = \frac {(D - \lambda_ {i} I) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {z}}{\| (D - \lambda_ {i} I) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {z} \| _ {2}}, \qquad i = 1, 2, \ldots , n.
$$

The spectral decomposition of a general $D + \rho z z^{T}$ can be turned into the case as in Theorem 8.8. To this end, we can prove the following theorem constructively. 

Theorem 8.9 Let $D = \mathrm{diag}(d_1, \ldots, d_n) \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ and $\mathbf{z} \in \mathbb{R}^n$ . Then there exists an orthogonal matrix $V$ and a permutation $\pi$ of $\{1, 2, \ldots, n\}$ such that 

(i) $V^{T}\mathbf{z} = [\eta_{1},\dots ,\eta_{r},0,\dots ,0]^{T}$ where $\eta_i\neq 0$ for $i = 1,2,\ldots ,r$ 

(ii) $V^{T}DV = \text{diag}(d_{\pi(1)}, \ldots, d_{\pi(n)})$ where $d_{\pi(1)} > d_{\pi(2)} > \cdots > d_{\pi(r)}$ . 

Proof Suppose that two indices i < j satisfy $d_{i} = d_{j}$ . Then we can set a rotation $P_{ij} = G(i, j, \theta)$ such that the jth component of $P_{ij}z$ is zero. It is easy to show that $P_{ij}^{T}DP_{ij} = D$ . After several steps, we can find an orthogonal matrix $V_{1}$ which is a product of some rotations such that 

$$
V _ {1} ^ {T} D V _ {1} = D, \quad V _ {1} ^ {T} \mathbf {z} = [ \xi_ {1}, \dots , \xi_ {n} ] ^ {T}
$$

with the property that if $\xi_i\xi_j\neq 0$ ( $i\neq j$ ), then $d_{i}\neq d_{j}$ . 

If $\xi_{i} = 0$ , $\xi_{j} \neq 0$ for $i < j$ , then we can find a permutation matrix to interchange the columns $i$ and $j$ . In such a way, we can find a permutation matrix $P_{1}$ which permutes all the nonzero $\xi_{j}$ to the front, i.e., 

$$
P _ {1} ^ {T} V _ {1} ^ {T} \mathbf {z} = [ \xi_ {\pi_ {1} (1)}, \dots , \xi_ {\pi_ {1} (n)} ] ^ {T}
$$

with 

$$
\xi_ {\pi_ {1} (i)} \neq 0, \qquad i = 1, 2, \ldots , r,
$$

and 

$$
\xi_ {\pi_ {1} (i)} = 0, \qquad i = r + 1, \dots , n.
$$

Here $\pi_1$ is a permutation of $\{1,2,\ldots,n\}$ . It follows from the construction of $P_1$ that 

$$
P _ {1} ^ {T} V _ {1} ^ {T} D V _ {1} P _ {1} = P _ {1} ^ {T} D P _ {1} = \operatorname{diag} (d _ {\pi_ {1} (1)}, \dots , d _ {\pi_ {1} (n)}),
$$

where the first $r$ diagonal entries $d_{\pi_1(1)}, \ldots, d_{\pi_1(r)}$ are distinct. 

Finally, we can find another permutation matrix $P_{2}$ of order $r$ such that 

$$
P _ {2} ^ {T} \operatorname{diag} (d _ {\pi_ {1} (1)}, \dots , d _ {\pi_ {1} (r)}) P _ {2} = \operatorname{diag} (\mu_ {1}, \dots , \mu_ {r}),
$$

where $\mu_{1} > \mu_{2} > \cdots > \mu_{r}$ . Let 

$$
V = V _ {1} P _ {1} \mathrm{diag} (P _ {2}, I _ {n - r})
$$

and $\pi$ be a permutation of $\{1,2,\ldots,n\}$ determined by $P_{1}$ and $P_{2}$ . Then, 

$$
V ^ {T} \mathbf {z} = [ \xi_ {\pi (1)}, \dots , \xi_ {\pi (r)}, 0, \dots , 0 ] ^ {T} = [ \eta_ {1}, \dots , \eta_ {r}, 0, \dots , 0 ] ^ {T},
$$

where $\eta_{i} \neq 0$ for $i = 1,2,\ldots,r$ , and 

$$
V ^ {T} D V = \operatorname{diag} (d _ {\pi (1)}, \dots , d _ {\pi (n)})
$$

with 

$$
d _ {\pi (1)} > d _ {\pi (2)} > \dots > d _ {\pi (r)}.
$$

The proof is complete. $\square$ 

For any $D = \mathrm{diag}(d_1, \ldots, d_n) \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ and $\mathbf{z} \in \mathbb{R}^n$ , by Theorem 8.9, we can construct an orthogonal matrix $V$ such that 

$$
V ^ {T} (D + \rho \mathbf {z z} ^ {T}) V = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} D _ {1} + \rho \mathbf {w w} ^ {T} & \mathbf {0} \\ \mathbf {0} & D _ {2} \end{array} \right],
$$

where 

$$
\begin{array}{c} D _ {1} = \operatorname{diag} (d _ {\pi (1)}, \ldots , d _ {\pi (r)}) \in \mathbb {R} ^ {r \times r}, \qquad d _ {\pi (1)} > \dots > d _ {\pi (r)}; \\ D _ {2} = \operatorname{diag} (d _ {\pi (r + 1)}, \ldots , d _ {\pi (n)}) \in \mathbb {R} ^ {(n - r) \times (n - r)}; \end{array}
$$

and 

$$
\mathbf {w} = [ \eta_ {1}, \dots , \eta_ {r} ] ^ {T}, \quad \eta_ {i} \neq 0, \quad i = 1, 2, \dots , r.
$$

Then we only need to compute the spectral decomposition of $D_{1} + \rho w w^{T}$ instead of $D + \rho z z^{T}$ . 

Finally, we briefly introduce the parallel computation of the divide-and-conquer method. For simplicity, we use a parallel computer with 4 processors to compute the spectral decomposition of a $4N$ -by-4N symmetric tridiagonal matrix $T$ . It can be summarized by the following 4 steps: 

(1) Tear 

$$
T = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} T _ {1} & \mathbf {0} \\ \mathbf {0} & T _ {2} \end{array} \right] + \rho \mathbf {v v} ^ {T}, \qquad T _ {1} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {2 N \times 2 N}, \qquad \mathbf {v} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {4 N};
$$

and 

$$
T _ {i} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} T _ {i 1} & \mathbf {0} \\ \mathbf {0} & T _ {i 2} \end{array} \right] + \rho_ {i} \mathbf {w} _ {i} \mathbf {w} _ {i} ^ {T},
$$

where $T_{ij} \in R^{N \times N}$ and $w_{i} \in R^{2N}$ for i = 1, 2. 

(2) Compute the spectral decompositions of $T_{11}, T_{12}, T_{21}$ and $T_{22}$ by 4 processors in parallel. 

(3) Combine the spectral decompositions of $T_{11}$ , $T_{12}$ to form a spectral decomposition of $T_{1}$ , and combine the spectral decompositions of $T_{21}$ , $T_{22}$ to form a spectral decomposition of $T_{2}$ . These can be implemented by 4 processors at the same time. 

(4) Combine the spectral decompositions of $T_{1}$ and $T_{2}$ to from a spectral decomposition of $T$ . This can be derived by 4 processors in parallel. 

From discussions above, we know that the divide-and-conquer method can be used for computing all the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of any large symmetric tridiagonal matrix in parallel. 

## Exercises:

1. Compute the Schur decomposition of 

$$
A = \left[ \begin{array}{l l} 1 & 2 \\ 2 & 3 \end{array} \right].
$$

2. Show that if $X \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times r}$ with $r \leqslant n$ , and $\| X^T X - I \|_2 = \tau < 1$ , then 

$$
\sigma_ {\min} (X) \geqslant 1 - \tau ,
$$

where $\sigma_{min}$ denotes the smallest singular value. 

3. Show that $A = B + \mathbf{i}C$ is Hermitian if and only if 

$$
M = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} B & - C \\ C & B \end{array} \right]
$$

is symmetric. Relate the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of A to those of M. 

4. Relate the singular values and the singular vectors of $A = B + \mathbf{i}C$ to those of 

$$
\left[ \begin{array}{c c} B & - C \\ C & B \end{array} \right],
$$

where $B, C \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ . 

5. Use the singular value decomposition to show that if $A \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ with $m \geqslant n$ , then there exist a matrix $Q \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ with $Q^T Q = I$ and a positive semi-definite matrix $P \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ such that $A = QP$ . 

6. Let 

$$
A = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} I & B \\ B ^ {*} & I \end{array} \right]
$$

with $\| B\| _2 <   1$ . Show that 

$$
\| A \| _ {2} \| A ^ {- 1} \| _ {2} = \frac {1 + \| B \| _ {2}}{1 - \| B \| _ {2}}.
$$

7. Let 

$$
A = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c} a _ {1} & b _ {1} & & & \mathbf {0} \\ c _ {1} & a _ {2} & b _ {2} & & \\ & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & \\ & & \ddots & \ddots & b _ {n - 1} \\ \mathbf {0} & & & c _ {n - 1} & a _ {n} \end{array} \right],
$$

where $b_{i}c_{i} > 0$ . Then there exists a diagonal $D$ such that $D^{-1}AD$ is a symmetric tridiagonal matrix. 

8. Let 

$$
T = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c} {2} & {- 1} & {0} & {0} \\ {- 1} & {2} & {- 1} & {0} \\ {0} & {- 1} & {2} & {- 1} \\ {0} & {0} & {- 1} & {2} \end{array} \right].
$$

(1) Is $T$ positive definite? 

(2) How many eigenvalues of $T$ lie in the interval [0, 2]? 

9. Let $A, E \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ be two symmetric matrices. Show that if $A$ is positive definite and $\| A^{-1}\| _2\| E\| _2 < 1$ , then $A + E$ is also positive definite. 

10. Let $A \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ with $m \geqslant n$ , and assume that the singular values of $A$ are ordered as 

$$
\sigma_ {1} \geqslant \sigma_ {2} \geqslant \dots \geqslant \sigma_ {n}.
$$

Show that 

$$
\sigma_ {i} = \max _ {\dim (S) = i} \min _ {\mathbf {0} \neq \mathbf {u} \in S} \frac {\| A \mathbf {u} \| _ {2}}{\| \mathbf {u} \| _ {2}} = \min _ {\dim (S) = n - i + 1} \max _ {\mathbf {0} \neq \mathbf {u} \in S} \frac {\| A \mathbf {u} \| _ {2}}{\| \mathbf {u} \| _ {2}},
$$

where S is any subspace of $R^{n}$ . 

11. Let $A = [a_{ij}] \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ be symmetric and satisfy 

(1) $a_{ii} > 0, \quad i = 1,2,\ldots,n.$ 

(2) $a_{ij} \leqslant 0, \quad i \neq j.$ 

(3) $\sum_{i=1}^{n} a_{i1} > 0$ . 

$$
(4) \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} a _ {i j} = 0, \quad j = 2, 3, \dots , n.
$$

Prove that the eigenvalues of $A$ are nonnegative. 

12. Let $A$ be symmetric and have bandwidth $p$ . Show that if we perform the shifted $QR$ iteration $A - \mu I = QR$ and $\tilde{A} = RQ + \mu I$ , then $\tilde{A}$ has bandwidth $p$ . 

## Chapter 9

# Applications in ODEs and DDEs

In this chapter, we briefly study some recent developments in using boundary value methods (BVMs) for solving initial value problems of systems of ordinary differential equations (ODEs). These methods require the solution of one or more nonsymmetric, large and sparse linear systems. Therefore, we use the GMRES method studied in Chapter 6 with some preconditioners for solving these linear systems. One of the main results is that if an $A_{\nu_1,\nu_2}$ -stable BVM is used for an $n$ -by- $n$ system of ODEs, then the preconditioned matrix can be decomposed as $I + L$ where $I$ is the identity matrix and the rank of $L$ is at most $2n(\nu_1 + \nu_2)$ . When the GMRES method is applied to the preconditioned systems, the method converges in at most $2n(\nu_1 + \nu_2) + 1$ iterations. Applications to different kinds of delay differential equations (DDEs) are also given. For a literature on BVMs for ODEs and DDEs, we refer to [5,8,10,17,19,44,45,47,48,55,58]. 

## 9.1 Introduction

Let us begin with the initial value problem: 

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l} \mathbf {y} ^ {\prime} (t) = J _ {n} \mathbf {y} (t) + \mathbf {g} (t), \quad t \in (t _ {0}, T ], \\ \mathbf {y} (t _ {0}) = \mathbf {z}, \end{array} \right.\tag{9.1}
$$

where $\mathbf{y}(t)$ , $\mathbf{g}(t):\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}$ , $z\in R^{n}$ , and $J_{n}\in R^{n\times n}$ . The initial value methods (IVMs), such as the Runge-Kutta methods, are well-known methods for solving (9.1) [83]. Recently, another class of methods called the boundary value methods (BVMs) has been proposed in [10]. Using BVMs to discretize (9.1), we obtain a linear system 

$$
M \mathbf {u} = \mathbf {b}.
$$

The advantage of using BVMs is that the methods are more stable and the resulting linear system $M\mathbf{u} = \mathbf{b}$ is hence more well-conditioned. However, this system is in general large and sparse (with band-structure), and solving it is a major 

## 9.2 Background of BVMs

problem in the application of BVMs. The GMRES method studied in Chapter 6 is used for solving $M\mathbf{u} = \mathbf{b}$ . In order to speed up the convergence of the GMRES iterations, a preconditioner $S$ called the Strang-type block-circulant preconditioner [19] is used to precondition the discrete system. The advantage of the Strang-type preconditioner is that if an $A_{\nu_1,\nu_2}$ -stable BVM is used for solving (9.1), then $S$ is invertible and the preconditioned matrix can be decomposed as 

$$
S ^ {- 1} M = I + L,
$$

where the rank of L is at most $2n(\nu_{1} + \nu_{2})$ which is independent of the integration step size. It follows that the GMRES method applied to the preconditioned system converges in at most $2n(\nu_{1} + \nu_{2}) + 1$ iterations in exact arithmetic. 

The outline of this chapter is as follows. In Section 9.2, we give some background knowledge about the linear multistep formulas (LMFs) and BVMs. Then, we investigate the properties of the Strang-type block-circulant preconditioner for ODEs in Section 9.3. The convergence and cost analysis of the method is also given with a numerical example. Finally, we discuss the applications of the Strang-type preconditioner with BVMs for solving different kinds of delay differential equations (DDEs) in Sections 9.4–9.6. 

## 9.2 Background of BVMs

We give some background knowledge on LMFs and BVMs in this section. 

## 9.2.1 Linear multistep formulas

Consider an initial value problem 

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l l} y ^ {\prime} = f (t, y), & t \in (t _ {0}, T ], \\ y (t _ {0}) = y _ {0}, \end{array} \right.
$$

where $y(t) : \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{R}$ and $f(t, y) : \mathbb{R}^{2} \to \mathbb{R}$ . The $\mu$ -step linear multistep formula (LMF) over a uniform mesh with step size h is defined as follows: 

$$
\sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\mu} \alpha_ {j} y _ {m + j} = h \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\mu} \beta_ {j} f _ {m + j}, \quad m = 0, 1, \dots ,\tag{9.2}
$$

where $y_{m}$ is the discrete approximation to $y(t_{m})$ and $f_{m}$ denotes $f(t_{m},y_{m})$ . 

To get the solution of (9.2), we need $\mu$ initial conditions 

$$
y _ {0}, y _ {1}, \dots , y _ {\mu - 1}.
$$

Since only $y_{0}$ is provided from the original problem, we have to find additional conditions for the remaining values 

$$
y _ {1}, y _ {2}, \dots , y _ {\mu - 1}.
$$

The equation (9.2) with $\mu - 1$ additional conditions is called initial value methods (IVMs). An IVM is called implicit if $\beta_{\mu} \neq 0$ and explicit if $\beta_{\mu} = 0$ . If an IVM is applied to an initial value problem on the interval $[t_{0}, t_{N+\mu-1}]$ , we have the following discrete problem 

$$
A _ {N} \mathbf {y} = h B _ {N} \mathbf {f} - \left[ \begin{array}{c} \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\mu - 1} (\alpha_ {i} y _ {i} - h \beta_ {i} f _ {i}) \\ \vdots \\ \alpha_ {0} y _ {\mu - 1} - h \beta_ {0} f _ {\mu - 1} \\ 0 \\ \vdots \\ 0 \end{array} \right],\tag{9.3}
$$

where 

$$
