\mathbf {y} = \left[ y _ {\mu}, y _ {\mu + 1}, \dots , y _ {N + \mu - 1} \right] ^ {T}, \quad \mathbf {f} = \left[ f _ {\mu}, f _ {\mu + 1}, \dots , f _ {N + \mu - 1} \right] ^ {T},
$$

$$
A _ {N} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c} \alpha_ {\mu} & & & & \mathbf {0} \\ \vdots & \ddots & & & \\ \alpha_ {0} & & \ddots & & \\ & \ddots & & \ddots & \\ \mathbf {0} & & \alpha_ {0} & \dots & \alpha_ {\mu} \end{array} \right], \quad B _ {N} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c} \beta_ {\mu} & & & & \mathbf {0} \\ \vdots & \ddots & & & \\ \beta_ {0} & & \ddots & & \\ & \ddots & & \ddots & \\ \mathbf {0} & & \beta_ {0} & \dots & \beta_ {\mu} \end{array} \right].
$$

Note that the matrices $A_{N}, B_{N} \in R^{N \times N}$ are lower triangular band Toeplitz matrices with lower bandwidth $\mu$ . We recall that a matrix is said to be Toeplitz if its entries are constant along its diagonals. Moreover, the linear system (9.3) can be solved easily by forward recursion. A classical example of IVM is the second order backward differentiation formula (BDF), 

$$
3 y _ {m + 2} - 4 y _ {m + 1} + y _ {m} = 2 h f _ {m + 1},
$$

which is a two-step method with $\alpha_0 = 1$ , $\alpha_1 = -4$ , $\alpha_2 = 3$ and $\beta_1 = 2$ . 

Instead of using an IVM with $\mu$ initial conditions for solving (9.1), we can also use the so-called boundary value methods (BVMs). Given $\nu_{1}, \nu_{2} \geqslant 0$ such that $\nu_{1} + \nu_{2} = \mu$ , then the corresponding BVM requires $\nu_{1}$ initial additional conditions 

$$
y _ {0}, y _ {1}, \dots , y _ {\nu_ {1} - 1},
$$

and $\nu_{2}$ final additional conditions 

$$
y _ {N}, y _ {N + 1}, \dots , y _ {N + \nu_ {2} - 1},
$$

which are called $(\nu_{1}, \nu_{2})$ -boundary conditions. Note that the class of BVMs contains the class of IVMs (i.e., $\nu_{1} = \mu$ , $\nu_{2} = 0$ ). 

The discrete problem generated by a $\mu$ -step BVM with $(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})$ -boundary conditions can be written in the following matrix form 

$$
A \mathbf {y} = h B \mathbf {f} - \left[ \begin{array}{c} \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\nu_ {1} - 1} (\alpha_ {i} y _ {i} - h \beta_ {i} f _ {i}) \\ \vdots \\ \alpha_ {0} y _ {\nu_ {1} - 1} - h \beta_ {0} f _ {\nu_ {1} - 1} \\ 0 \\ \vdots \\ 0 \\ \alpha_ {\mu} y _ {N} - h \beta_ {\mu} f _ {N} \\ \vdots \\ \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {\nu_ {2}} (\alpha_ {\nu_ {1} + i} y _ {N - 1 + i} - h \beta_ {\nu_ {1} + i} f _ {N - 1 + i}) \end{array} \right],
$$

where 

$$
\mathbf {y} = \left[ y _ {\nu_ {1}}, y _ {\nu_ {1} + 1}, \dots , y _ {N - 1} \right] ^ {T}, \quad \mathbf {f} = \left[ f _ {\nu_ {1}}, f _ {\nu_ {1} + 1}, \dots , f _ {N - 1} \right] ^ {T},
$$

A and $B \in \mathbb{R}^{(N-\nu_{1}) \times (N-\nu_{1})}$ are defined as follows, 

$$
A = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c} \alpha_ {\nu_ {1}} & \dots & \alpha_ {\mu} & & \mathbf {0} \\ \vdots & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & \\ \alpha_ {0} & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & \alpha_ {\mu} \\ & \ddots & & \ddots & \vdots \\ \mathbf {0} & & \alpha_ {0} & \dots & \alpha_ {\nu_ {1}} \end{array} \right], \quad B = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c} \beta_ {\nu_ {1}} & \dots & \beta_ {\mu} & & \mathbf {0} \\ \vdots & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & \\ \beta_ {0} & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & \beta_ {\mu} \\ & \ddots & & \ddots & \vdots \\ \mathbf {0} & & \beta_ {0} & \dots & \beta_ {\nu_ {1}} \end{array} \right].\tag{9.4}
$$

Note that the coefficient matrices are band Toeplitz with lower bandwidth $\nu_{1}$ and upper bandwidth $\nu_{2}$ . An example of BVMs is the third order generalized backward differentiation formula (GBDF), 

$$
2 y _ {m + 1} + 3 y _ {m} - 6 y _ {m - 1} + y _ {m - 2} = 6 h f _ {m},
$$

which is a three-step method with $(2, 1)$ -boundary conditions where 

$$
\alpha_ {0} = 1, \quad \alpha_ {1} = - 6, \quad \alpha_ {2} = 3, \quad \alpha_ {3} = 2, \quad \beta_ {2} = 6.
$$

Although IVMs are more efficient than BVMs (which cannot be solved by forward recursion), the advantage in using BVMs over IVMs comes from their stability properties. For example, the usual BDF are not $A$ -stable for $\mu > 2$ but the GBDF are $A_{\nu_1, \nu_2}$ -stable for any $\mu \geqslant 1$ . See for instance [1] and [10, p. 79 and Figures 5.1-5.3]. 

## 9.2.2 Block-BVMs and their matrix forms

Let $\mu = \nu_{1} + \nu_{2}$ . By using the $\mu$ -step block-BVM based on LMF over a uniform mesh $h = (T - t_0) / s$ for solving (9.1), we have: 

$$
\sum_ {i = - \nu_ {1}} ^ {\nu_ {2}} \alpha_ {i + \nu_ {1}} \mathbf {y} _ {m + i} = h \sum_ {i = - \nu_ {1}} ^ {\nu_ {2}} \beta_ {i + \nu_ {1}} \mathbf {f} _ {m + i}, \quad m = \nu_ {1}, \ldots , s - \nu_ {2}.\tag{9.5}
$$

Here, $\mathbf{y}_m$ is the discrete approximation to $\mathbf{y}(t_m)$ , 

$$
\mathbf {f} _ {m} = J _ {n} \mathbf {y} _ {m} + \mathbf {g} _ {m}, \quad \mathbf {g} _ {m} = \mathbf {g} (t _ {m}).
$$

Also, (9.5) requires $\nu_{1}$ initial conditions and $\nu_{2}$ final conditions which are provided by the following $\mu - 1$ additional equations: 

$$
\sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\mu} \alpha_ {i} ^ {(j)} \mathbf {y} _ {i} = h \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\mu} \beta_ {i} ^ {(j)} \mathbf {f} _ {i}, \quad j = 1, \dots , \nu_ {1} - 1,\tag{9.6}
$$

and 

$$
\sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\mu} \alpha_ {\mu - i} ^ {(j)} \mathbf {y} _ {s - i} = h \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\mu} \beta_ {\mu - i} ^ {(j)} \mathbf {f} _ {s - i}, \quad j = s - \nu_ {2} + 1, \ldots , s.\tag{9.7}
$$

The coefficients $\{\alpha^{(j)}\}$ , $\{\beta^{(j)}\}$ in (9.6) and (9.7) should be chosen such that the truncation errors for these initial and final conditions are of the same order as that in (9.5). By combining (9.5), (9.6), (9.7) and the initial condition $\mathbf{y}(t_0) = \mathbf{y}_0 = \mathbf{z}$ , the discrete system of (9.1) is given by the following block form 

$$
M \mathbf {y} \equiv (\widetilde {A} \otimes I _ {n} - h \widetilde {B} \otimes J _ {n}) \mathbf {y} = \mathbf {e} _ {1} \otimes \mathbf {z} + h (\widetilde {B} \otimes I _ {n}) \mathbf {g}.\tag{9.8}
$$

Here 

$$
\mathbf {e} _ {1} = [ 1, 0, \dots , 0 ] ^ {T} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {s + 1}, \quad \mathbf {y} = [ \mathbf {y} _ {0} ^ {T}, \dots , \mathbf {y} _ {s} ^ {T} ] ^ {T} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {(s + 1) n},
$$

$$
\mathbf {g} = \left[ \mathbf {g} _ {0} ^ {T}, \dots , \mathbf {g} _ {s} ^ {T} \right] ^ {T} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {(s + 1) n},
$$

$\widetilde{A}, \widetilde{B} \in \mathbb{R}^{(s+1) \times (s+1)}$ given by: 

$$
\widetilde {A} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c c c c} 1 & \dots & 0 & & & & \\ \alpha_ {0} ^ {(1)} & \dots & \alpha_ {\mu} ^ {(1)} & & & & \\ \vdots & \vdots & \vdots & & & & \\ \alpha_ {0} ^ {(\nu_ {1} - 1)} & \dots & \alpha_ {\mu} ^ {(\nu_ {1} - 1)} & & & & \\ \alpha_ {0} & \dots & \alpha_ {\mu} & & & & \\ & \alpha_ {0} & \dots & \alpha_ {\mu} & & & \\ & & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & & \\ & & & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & \\ & & & & \alpha_ {0} & \dots & \alpha_ {\mu} \\ & & 0 & & \alpha_ {0} ^ {(s - \nu_ {2} + 1)} & \dots & \alpha_ {\mu} ^ {(s - \nu_ {2} + 1)} \\ & & & & \vdots & & \vdots \\ & & & & \alpha_ {0} ^ {(s)} & \dots & \alpha_ {\mu} ^ {(s)} \end{array} \right],
$$

$$
\widetilde {B} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c c c c} 0 & \dots & 0 & & & & \\ \beta_ {0} ^ {(1)} & \dots & \beta_ {\mu} ^ {(1)} & & & & \\ \vdots & \vdots & \vdots & & & & \\ \beta_ {0} ^ {(\nu_ {1} - 1)} & \dots & \beta_ {\mu} ^ {(\nu_ {1} - 1)} & & & \mathbf {0} & \\ \beta_ {0} & \dots & \beta_ {\mu} & & & \\ & \beta_ {0} & \dots & \beta_ {\mu} & & \\ & & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & \\ & & & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & \\ & & & & \beta_ {0} & \dots & \beta_ {\mu} \\ & & \mathbf {0} & & \beta_ {0} ^ {(s - \nu_ {2} + 1)} & \dots & \beta_ {\mu} ^ {(s - \nu_ {2} + 1)} \\ & & & & \vdots & & \vdots \\ & & & & \beta_ {0} ^ {(s)} & \dots & \beta_ {\mu} ^ {(s)} \end{array} \right],
$$

and “ $\otimes$ ” is the tensor product. 

We recall that the tensor product of $A = [a_{ij}] \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ and $B \in \mathbb{R}^{p \times q}$ is defined as follows: 

$$
A \otimes B \equiv \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c} a _ {1 1} B & a _ {1 2} B & \dots & a _ {1 n} B \\ a _ {2 1} B & a _ {2 2} B & \dots & a _ {2 n} B \\ \vdots & \vdots & & \vdots \\ a _ {m 1} B & a _ {m 2} B & \dots & a _ {m n} B \end{array} \right]
$$

which is an $mp$ -by- $nq$ matrix. The basic properties of the tensor product can be found in [32, 42]. 

We remark that usually the linear system (9.8) is large and sparse (with band-structure), and solving it is a major problem in the application of the BVMs. We use the GMRES method in Chapter 6 for solving (9.8). In order to speed up the convergence rate of the GMRES iterations, we use a preconditioner S called the Strang-type block-circulant preconditioner. 

## 9.3 Strang-type preconditioner for ODEs

Now, we construct the Strang-type block-circulant preconditioner S for solving (9.8). We show that the main advantages of the Strang-type preconditioner are: 

(1) S is invertible if an $A_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}$ -stable BVM is used. 

(2) The spectrum of the preconditioned system is clustered. 

(3) The operation cost for each iteration of the preconditioned GMRES method is smaller than that of direct solvers. 

## 9.3.1 Construction of preconditioner

We first recall the definition of Strang's circulant preconditioner for Toeplitz matrices. Given any Toeplitz matrix 

$$
T _ {l} = [ t _ {i - j} ] _ {i, j = 1} ^ {l} = [ t _ {q} ],
$$

Strang's preconditioner $s(T_l)$ is a circulant matrix with diagonals given by 

$$
[ s (T _ {l}) ] _ {q} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} t _ {q}, & 0 \leqslant q \leqslant \lfloor l / 2 \rfloor , \\ t _ {q - l}, & \lfloor l / 2 \rfloor <   q <   l, \\ [ s (T _ {l}) ] _ {l + q}, & 0 <   - q <   l. \end{array} \right.
$$

See [16, 18, 42, 43, 77]. 

Neglecting the perturbations of $\widetilde{A}$ and $\widetilde{B}$ , we propose the following preconditioner $S \in \mathbb{R}^{(s + 1)n \times (s + 1)n}$ called the Strang-type block circulant preconditioner for $M$ given in (9.8): 

9.3 Strang-type preconditioner for ODEs 

$$
S = s (A) \otimes I _ {n} - h s (B) \otimes J _ {n},\tag{9.9}
$$

where 

$$
s (A) = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c c c c c} \alpha_ {\nu_ {1}} & \dots & \alpha_ {\mu} & & & & \alpha_ {0} & \dots & \alpha_ {\nu_ {1} - 1} \\ \vdots & \ddots & & \ddots & & & & \ddots & \vdots \\ \alpha_ {0} & & \ddots & & \ddots & & & & \alpha_ {0} \\ & \ddots & & \ddots & & \ddots & & \mathbf {0} \\ & & \ddots & & \ddots & & \ddots & \\ & \mathbf {0} & & \ddots & & \ddots & & \ddots \\ \alpha_ {\mu} & & & & \ddots & & \ddots & & \alpha_ {\mu} \\ \vdots & \ddots & & & & \ddots & & \ddots & \vdots \\ \alpha_ {\nu_ {1} + 1} & \dots & \alpha_ {\mu} & & & & \alpha_ {0} & \dots & \alpha_ {\nu_ {1}} \end{array} \right]
$$

and $s(B)$ is defined similarly by using $\{\beta_i\}_{i=0}^\mu$ instead of $\{\alpha_i\}_{i=0}^\mu$ in $s(A)$ . The $\{\alpha_i\}_{i=0}^\mu$ and $\{\beta_i\}_{i=0}^\mu$ here are the coefficients given in (9.5). We remark that actually $s(A)$ , $s(B)$ are just Strang's circulant preconditioners for Toeplitz matrices $A, B$ respectively, where $A, B$ are given by (9.4). 

We show that the preconditioner $S$ is invertible provided that the given BVM is $A_{\nu_1, \nu_2}$ -stable and the eigenvalues of $J_n$ are in 

$$
\mathbb {C} ^ {-} \equiv \{q \in \mathbb {C}: \operatorname{Re} (q) <   0 \},
$$

where $\mathrm{Re}(\cdot)$ denotes the real part of a complex number. The stability of a BVM is closely related to two characteristic polynomials of degree $\mu = \nu_{1} + \nu_{2}$ , defined as follows: 

$$
\rho (z) \equiv \sum_ {j = - \nu_ {1}} ^ {\nu_ {2}} \alpha_ {j + \nu_ {1}} z ^ {j + \nu_ {1}} \quad \text { and } \quad \sigma (z) \equiv \sum_ {j = - \nu_ {1}} ^ {\nu_ {2}} \beta_ {j + \nu_ {1}} z ^ {j + \nu_ {1}}.\tag{9.10}
$$

The $A_{\nu_1,\nu_2}$ -stability polynomial is defined by 

$$
\pi (z, q) \equiv \rho (z) - q \sigma (z),\tag{9.11}
$$

where $z, q \in C$ . 

Consider now the equation $\pi(z, q) = 0$ . It defines a mapping between the complex $z$ -plane and the complex $q$ -plane. For every $z \in \mathbb{C}$ which is a root of $\pi(z, q)$ , (9.11) provides 

$$
q = q (z) = \frac {\rho (z)}{\sigma (z)}.
$$

Let 

$$
\Gamma \equiv \left\{q \in \mathbb {C}: q = \frac {\rho (e ^ {\mathrm{i} \theta})}{\sigma (e ^ {\mathrm{i} \theta})}, 0 \leqslant \theta <   2 \pi \right\}.\tag{9.12}
$$

The $\Gamma$ is the set corresponding to the roots on the unit circumference and is called the boundary locus. We have the following definition and lemma [10]. 

Definition 9.1 Consider a BVM with an $A_{\nu_1,\nu_2}$ -stability polynomial $\pi(z,q)$ defined by (9.10). The region 

$\mathcal{D}_{\nu_1,\nu_2} = \{q\in \mathbb{C}:\pi (z,q)$ has $\nu_{1}$ zeros inside $|z| = 1$ and $\nu_{2}$ zeros outside $|z| = 1\}$ 

is called the region of $A_{\nu_1, \nu_2}$ -stability of the given BVM. Moreover, the BVM is said to be $A_{\nu_1, \nu_2}$ -stable if 

$$
\mathbb {C} ^ {-} \subseteq \mathcal {D} _ {\nu_ {1}, \nu_ {2}}.
$$

Lemma 9.1 If a BVM is $A_{\nu_1, \nu_2}$ -stable and $\Gamma$ is defined by (9.12), then $\operatorname{Re}(q) \geqslant 0$ for all $q \in \Gamma$ . 

Now, we want to show that the preconditioner S is invertible under the stability condition. 

Theorem 9.1 If the BVM for (9.1) is $A_{\nu_1, \nu_2}$ -stable and $h\lambda_k(J_n) \in \mathcal{D}_{\nu_1, \nu_2}$ where $\lambda_k(J_n)$ ( $k = 1, \ldots, n$ ) are the eigenvalues of $J_n$ , then the preconditioner $S$ defined by (9.9) is invertible. 

Proof Since $s(A)$ and $s(B)$ are circulant matrices, their eigenvalues are given by 

$$
g _ {A} (z) \equiv \alpha_ {\mu} z ^ {\nu_ {2}} + \dots + \alpha_ {\nu_ {1}} + \alpha_ {\nu_ {1} - 1} \frac {1}{z} + \dots + \alpha_ {0} \frac {1}{z ^ {\nu_ {1}}} = \frac {\rho (z)}{z ^ {\nu_ {1}}}
$$

and 

$$
g _ {B} (z) \equiv \beta_ {\mu} z ^ {\nu_ {2}} + \dots + \beta_ {\nu_ {1}} + \beta_ {\nu_ {1} - 1} \frac {1}{z} + \dots + \beta_ {0} \frac {1}{z ^ {\nu_ {1}}} = \frac {\sigma (z)}{z ^ {\nu_ {1}}},
$$

evaluated at $\omega_{j} = e^{\frac{2\pi\mathbf{i}j}{s + 1}}$ where $\mathbf{i}\equiv \sqrt{-1}$ for $j = 0,\dots ,s$ . See [18,29]. The eigenvalues $\lambda_{jk}(S)$ of $S$ are therefore given by 

$$
\lambda_ {j k} (S) = g _ {A} (\omega_ {j}) - h \lambda_ {k} (J _ {n}) g _ {B} (\omega_ {j}), \qquad j = 0, \ldots , s, \quad k = 1, \ldots , n.
$$

Since the BVM is $A_{\nu_1,\nu_2}$ -stable, the $\mu$ -degree polynomial 

$$
\pi [ z, h \lambda_ {k} (J _ {n}) ] = \rho (z) - h \lambda_ {k} (J _ {n}) \sigma (z)
$$

has no roots on the unit circle $|z| = 1$ if $h\lambda_k(J_n) \in \mathcal{D}_{\nu_1, \nu_2}$ . Thus for all $k = 1, \ldots, n$ , and any arbitrary $|z| = 1$ , we have 

$$
g _ {A} (z) - h \lambda_ {k} (J _ {n}) g _ {B} (z) = \frac {1}{z ^ {\nu_ {1}}} \pi [ z, h \lambda_ {k} (J _ {n}) ] \neq 0.
$$

It follows that 

$$
\lambda_ {j k} (S) \neq 0, \quad j = 0, \dots , s, \quad k = 1, \dots , n.
$$

Thus $S$ is invertible. 

## 9.3 Strang-type preconditioner for ODEs

In particular, we have 

Corollary 9.1 If the BVM is $A_{\nu_1, \nu_2}$ -stable and $\lambda_k(J_n) \in \mathbb{C}^-$ , then the preconditioner $S$ is invertible. 

## 9.3.2 Convergence rate and operation cost

We have the following theorem for the convergence rate. 

Theorem 9.2 We have 

$$
S ^ {- 1} M = I _ {n (s + 1)} + L,
$$

where 

$$
\operatorname{rank} (L) \leqslant 2 n \mu .
$$

Proof Let E = M - S. We have by (9.8) and (9.9), 

$$
E = (\widetilde {A} - s (A)) \otimes I _ {n} - h (\widetilde {B} - s (B)) \otimes J _ {n} = L _ {A} \otimes I _ {n} - h L _ {B} \otimes J _ {n}.
$$

It is easy to check that $L_{A}$ and $L_{B}$ are $(s+1)$ -by- $(s+1)$ matrices with nonzero entries only in the following four corners: a $\nu_{1}$ -by- $(\mu+1)$ block in the upper left; a $\nu_{1}$ -by- $\nu_{1}$ block in the upper right; a $\nu_{2}$ -by- $(\mu+1)$ block in the lower right; and a $\nu_{2}$ -by- $\nu_{2}$ block in the lower left. By noting that $\mu=\nu_{1}+\nu_{2}$ , we then have 

$$
\operatorname{rank} (L _ {A}) \leqslant \mu , \quad \operatorname{rank} (L _ {B}) \leqslant \mu .
$$

Therefore, 

$$
\operatorname{rank} (L _ {A} \otimes I _ {n}) = \operatorname{rank} (L _ {A}) \cdot n \leqslant n \mu
$$

and 

$$
\operatorname{rank} (L _ {B} \otimes J _ {n}) = \operatorname{rank} (L _ {B}) \cdot n \leqslant n \mu .
$$

Thus, 

$$
S ^ {- 1} M = I _ {n (s + 1)} + S ^ {- 1} E = I _ {n (s + 1)} + L,
$$

where the rank of $L$ is at most $2n\mu$ . 

Therefore, when the GMRES method is applied to 

$$
S ^ {- 1} M \mathbf {y} = S ^ {- 1} \mathbf {b},
$$

by Theorems 6.13 and 9.2, we know that the method converges in at most $2n\mu + 1$ iterations in exact arithmetic. 

Regarding the cost per iteration, the main work in each iteration for the GMRES method is the matrix-vector multiplication 

$$
S ^ {- 1} M \mathbf {z} = (s (A) \otimes I _ {n} - h s (B) \otimes J _ {n}) ^ {- 1} (\widetilde {A} \otimes I _ {n} - h \widetilde {B} \otimes J _ {n}) \mathbf {z}
$$

(see Section 6.5). Since $\widetilde{A}$ , $\widetilde{B}$ are band matrices and $J_{n}$ is assumed to be sparse, the matrix-vector multiplication 

$$
M \mathbf {z} = (\widetilde {A} \otimes I _ {n} - h \widetilde {B} \otimes J _ {n}) \mathbf {z}
$$

can be done very fast. 

Now we compute $S^{-1}(M\mathbf{z})$ . Note that any circulant matrix can be diagonalized by the Fourier matrix F (see Section 6.4 and [29]). Since $s(A)$ and $s(B)$ are circulant, we have the following decompositions by (6.19), 

$$
s (A) = F \Lambda_ {A} F ^ {*}, \quad s (B) = F \Lambda_ {B} F ^ {*},
$$

where $\Lambda_{A}$ , $\Lambda_{B}$ are diagonal matrices containing the eigenvalues of $s(A)$ , $s(B)$ respectively. It follows that 

$$
S ^ {- 1} (M \mathbf {z}) = (F ^ {*} \otimes I _ {n}) (\Lambda_ {A} \otimes I _ {n} - h \Lambda_ {B} \otimes J _ {n}) ^ {- 1} (F \otimes I _ {n}) (M \mathbf {z}).
$$

This product can be obtained by using FFTs and solving $s + 1$ linear systems of order n. Since $J_{n}$ is sparse, the matrix 

$$
\Lambda_ {A} \otimes I _ {n} - h \Lambda_ {B} \otimes J _ {n}
$$

is also sparse. Thus $S^{-1}(M\mathbf{z})$ can be obtained by solving $s+1$ sparse linear systems of order n. It follows that the total number of operations per iteration is 

$$
\gamma_ {1} n (s + 1) \log (s + 1) + \gamma_ {2} (s + 1) n q,
$$

where q is the number of nonzeros of $J_{n}$ , and $\gamma_{1}$ and $\gamma_{2}$ are some positive constants. For comparing the computational cost of the method with direct solvers for the linear system (9.8), we refer to [19]. 

## 9.3.3 Numerical result

Now we give an example to illustrate the efficiency of the preconditioner S by solving a test problem given in [8]. The experiments were performed in MATLAB. We used the MATLAB-provided M-file “gmres” to solve the preconditioned systems. We should emphasize that in all of our tests in this chapter, the zero vector is the initial guess and the stopping criterion is 

$$
\frac {\| \mathbf {r} _ {q} \| _ {2}}{\| \mathbf {r} _ {0} \| _ {2}} <   1 0 ^ {- 6},
$$

where $\mathbf{r}_q$ is the residual after $q$ iterations. The BVM we used is the third order generalized Adam's method (GAM). Its formula and the initial and final additional conditions can be found in [10]. 

Example 9.1 Heat equation: 

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \frac {\partial u}{\partial t} = \frac {\partial^ {2} u}{\partial x ^ {2}}, \\ u (0, t) = \frac {\partial u}{\partial x} (\pi , t) = 0, & t \in [ 0, 2 \pi ], \\ u (x, 0) = x, & x \in [ 0, \pi ]. \end{array} \right.
$$

We discretize the partial differential operator $\partial^{2}/\partial x^{2}$ with central differences and step size equals to $\pi/(n+1)$ . The system of ODEs obtained is: 

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \mathbf {y} ^ {\prime} (t) = T _ {n} \mathbf {y} (t), & t \in [ 0, 2 \pi ] \\ \mathbf {y} (0) = [ x _ {1}, x _ {2}, \ldots , x _ {n} ] ^ {T}, \end{array} \right.
$$

where $T_{n}$ is a scaled discrete Laplacian matrix 

$$
T _ {n} = \frac {(n + 1) ^ {2}}{\pi^ {2}} \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c} - 2 & 1 & & & \mathbf {0} \\ 1 & \ddots & \ddots & & \\ & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & \\ & & 1 & - 2 & 1 \\ \mathbf {0} & & & 1 & - 1 \end{array} \right].
$$

Table 9.1 lists the number of iterations required for convergence of the GMRES method for different n and s. In the table, I means no preconditioner is used and S denotes the Strang-type block-circulant preconditioner defined by (9.9). We see that the number of iterations required for convergence, when S is used, is much less than that when no preconditioner is used. The numbers under the column S stay almost a constant for increasing s and n. 


Table 9.1 Number of iterations for convergence.


<table><tr><td>n</td><td>s</td><td>I</td><td>S</td></tr><tr><td>24</td><td>6</td><td>19</td><td>4</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>12</td><td>70</td><td>4</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>24</td><td>152</td><td>4</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>48</td><td>227</td><td>3</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>96</td><td>314</td><td>3</td></tr></table>

<table><tr><td>n</td><td>s</td><td>I</td><td>S</td></tr><tr><td>48</td><td>6</td><td>47</td><td>4</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>12</td><td>167</td><td>4</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>24</td><td>359</td><td>4</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>48</td><td>&gt;400</td><td>3</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>96</td><td>&gt;400</td><td>3</td></tr></table>

## 9.4 Strang-type preconditioner for DDEs

Now, we study delay differential equations (DDEs). 

## 9.4.1 Differential equations with multi-delays

Consider the solution of differential equations with multi-delays: 

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \mathbf {y} ^ {\prime} (t) = J _ {n} \mathbf {y} (t) + D _ {n} ^ {(1)} \mathbf {y} (t - \tau_ {1}) + \dots + D _ {n} ^ {(s)} \mathbf {y} (t - \tau_ {s}) + \mathbf {f} (t), & t \geqslant t _ {0}, \\ \mathbf {y} (t) = \phi (t), & t \leqslant t _ {0}, \end{array} \right.\tag{9.13}
$$

where $\mathbf{y}(t)$ , $\mathbf{f}(t)$ , $\boldsymbol{\phi}(t):\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}$ ; $J_{n}$ , $D_{n}^{(1)}$ , $\ldots$ , $D_{n}^{(s)}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}$ ; and $\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{s}>0$ are some rational numbers. 

In order to find a reasonable numerical solution, we require that the solution of (9.13) is asymptotically stable. We have the following lemma [63, 64, 91]. 

Lemma 9.2 For any $s \geqslant 1$ , if 

$$
\eta (J _ {n}) \equiv \frac {1}{2} \lambda_ {\mathrm{max}} (J _ {n} + J _ {n} ^ {T}) <   0
$$

and 

$$
\eta (J _ {n}) + \sum_ {j = 1} ^ {s} \| D _ {n} ^ {(j)} \| _ {2} <   0,\tag{9.14}
$$

then the solution of (9.13) is asymptotically stable. 

In the following, for simplicity, we only consider the case of $s = 2$ in (9.13). The generalization to any arbitrary $s$ is straightforward. Let 

$$
h = \tau_ {1} / m _ {1} = \tau_ {2} / m _ {2}
$$

be the step size where $m_{1}$ and $m_{2}$ are positive integers with $m_{2} > m_{1} (\tau_{2} > \tau_{1})$ . For (9.13), by using a BVM with $(\nu_{1}, \nu_{2})$ -boundary conditions over a uniform mesh 

$$
t _ {j} = t _ {0} + j h, \quad j = 0, \dots , r _ {1},
$$

on the interval $[t_{0}, t_{0} + r_{1}h]$ , we have 

$$
\sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\mu} \alpha_ {i} \mathbf {y} _ {p + i - \nu_ {1}} = h \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\mu} \beta_ {i} (J _ {n} \mathbf {y} _ {p + i - \nu_ {1}} + D _ {n} ^ {(1)} \mathbf {y} _ {p + i - \nu_ {1} - m _ {1}} + D _ {n} ^ {(2)} \mathbf {y} _ {p + i - \nu_ {1} - m _ {2}} + \mathbf {f} _ {p + i - \nu_ {1}}),\tag{9.15}
$$

for $p = \nu_{1},\ldots ,r_{1} - 1$ , where $\mu = \nu_{1} + \nu_{2}$ . By providing the values 

$$
\mathbf {y} _ {- m _ {2}}, \dots , \mathbf {y} _ {- m _ {1}}, \dots , \mathbf {y} _ {0}, \quad \mathbf {y} _ {1}, \dots , \mathbf {y} _ {\nu_ {1} - 1}, \quad \mathbf {y} _ {r _ {1}}, \dots , \mathbf {y} _ {r _ {1} + \nu_ {2} - 1},\tag{9.16}
$$

(9.15) can be written in a matrix form as 

$$
R \mathbf {y} = \mathbf {b},
$$

## 9.4 Strang-type preconditioner for DDEs

where 

$$
\begin{array}{c} {R \equiv A \otimes I _ {n} - h B \otimes J _ {n} - h C ^ {(1)} \otimes D _ {n} ^ {(1)} - h C ^ {(2)} \otimes D _ {n} ^ {(2)},} \\ {\mathbf {y} = [ \mathbf {y} _ {\nu_ {1}} ^ {T}, \mathbf {y} _ {\nu_ {1} + 1} ^ {T}, \ldots , \mathbf {y} _ {r _ {1} - 1} ^ {T} ] ^ {T} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n (r _ {1} - \nu_ {1})},} \end{array}\tag{9.17}
$$

$\mathbf{b} \in \mathbb{R}^{n(r_1 - \nu_1)}$ depends on $\mathbf{f}$ , the boundary values and the coefficients of the method. The matrices $A, B \in \mathbb{R}^{(r_1 - \nu_1) \times (r_1 - \nu_1)}$ are defined as in (9.4) and $C^{(1)}, C^{(2)} \in \mathbb{R}^{(r_1 - \nu_1) \times (r_1 - \nu_1)}$ are defined as follows: 

$$
C ^ {(1)} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c c} \mathbf {0} & & & & & \mathbf {0} \\ \beta_ {\mu} & \ddots & & & & \\ \vdots & \ddots & \ddots & & & \\ \beta_ {0} & \dots & \beta_ {\mu} & \ddots & & \\ & \ddots & & \ddots & \ddots & \\ \mathbf {0} & & \beta_ {0} & \dots & \beta_ {\mu} & \mathbf {0} \end{array} \right], \quad C ^ {(2)} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c c} \mathbf {0} & & & & & \mathbf {0} \\ \beta_ {\mu} & \ddots & & & & \\ \vdots & \ddots & \ddots & & \\ \beta_ {0} & \dots & \beta_ {\mu} & \ddots & & \\ & \ddots & & \ddots & \ddots & \\ \mathbf {0} & & \beta_ {0} & \dots & \beta_ {\mu} & \mathbf {0} \end{array} \right]
$$

We remark that the first column of $C^{(1)}$ is given by 

$$
[ \underbrace {0 , \ldots , 0} _ {m _ {1} - \nu_ {2}}, \beta_ {\mu}, \ldots , \beta_ {0}, \underbrace {0 , \ldots , 0} _ {r _ {1} - m _ {1} - 2 \nu_ {1} - 1} ] ^ {T}
$$

and the first column of $C^{(2)}$ is given by 

$$
[ \underbrace {0 , \ldots , 0} _ {m _ {2} - \nu_ {2}}, \beta_ {\mu}, \ldots , \beta_ {0}, \underbrace {0 , \ldots , 0} _ {r _ {1} - m _ {2} - 2 \nu_ {1} - 1} ] ^ {T}.
$$

## 9.4.2 Construction of preconditioner

The Strang-type block-circulant preconditioner for (9.17) is defined as follows: 

$$
\widetilde {S} \equiv s (A) \otimes I _ {n} - h s (B) \otimes J _ {n} - h s (C ^ {(1)}) \otimes D _ {n} ^ {(1)} - h s (C ^ {(2)}) \otimes D _ {n} ^ {(2)},\tag{9.18}
$$

where $s(E)$ is Strang's circulant preconditioner of Toeplitz matrix $E$ , for $E = A, B, C^{(1)}, C^{(2)}$ respectively. 

Now we discuss the invertibility of the Strang-type preconditioner $\widetilde{S}$ defined by (9.18). Since any circulant matrix can be diagonalized by the Fourier matrix $F$ , we have by (6.19), 

$$
s (E) = F ^ {*} \Lambda_ {E} F,
$$

where $\Lambda_E$ is the diagonal matrix holding the eigenvalues of $s(E)$ , for $E = A$ , $B$ , $C^{(1)}$ , $C^{(2)}$ respectively. Therefore, we obtain 

$$
\widetilde {S} = (F ^ {*} \otimes I _ {n}) (\Lambda_ {A} \otimes I _ {n} - h \Lambda_ {B} \otimes J _ {n} - h \Lambda_ {C ^ {(1)}} \otimes D _ {n} ^ {(1)} - h \Lambda_ {C ^ {(2)}} \otimes D _ {n} ^ {(2)}) (F \otimes I _ {n}).
$$

Note that the $j$ th block of 

$$
\Lambda_ {A} \otimes I _ {n} - h \Lambda_ {B} \otimes J _ {n} - h \Lambda_ {C ^ {(1)}} \otimes D _ {n} ^ {(1)} - h \Lambda_ {C ^ {(2)}} \otimes D _ {n} ^ {(2)}
$$

is given by 

$$
\widetilde {S} _ {j} = [ \Lambda_ {A} ] _ {j j} I _ {n} - h [ \Lambda_ {B} ] _ {j j} J _ {n} - h [ \Lambda_ {C ^ {(1)}} ] _ {j j} D _ {n} ^ {(1)} - h [ \Lambda_ {C ^ {(2)}} ] _ {j j} D _ {n} ^ {(2)},
$$

for $j = 1,2,\ldots ,r_1 - \nu_1$ . Let 

$$
w _ {j} = e ^ {\frac {2 \pi \mathrm{i} j}{r _ {1} - \nu_ {1}}}.
$$

We have 

$$
[ \Lambda_ {A} ] _ {j j} = \rho (w _ {j}) / w _ {j} ^ {\nu_ {1}}, \qquad [ \Lambda_ {B} ] _ {j j} = \sigma (w _ {j}) / w _ {j} ^ {\nu_ {1}},
$$

$$
[ \Lambda_ {C ^ {(1)}} ] _ {j j} = \beta_ {\mu} w _ {j} ^ {- m _ {1} + \nu_ {2}} + \dots + \beta_ {0} w _ {j} ^ {- m _ {1} - \nu_ {1}} = \sigma (w _ {j}) / w _ {j} ^ {m _ {1} + \nu_ {1}},
$$

and 

$$
[ \Lambda_ {C ^ {(2)}} ] _ {j j} = \beta_ {\mu} w _ {j} ^ {- m _ {2} + \nu_ {2}} + \dots + \beta_ {0} w _ {j} ^ {- m _ {2} - \nu_ {1}} = \sigma (w _ {j}) / w _ {j} ^ {m _ {2} + \nu_ {1}},
$$

where $\rho(z)$ and $\sigma(z)$ are defined as in (9.10). Therefore, 

$$
\widetilde {S} _ {j} = \frac {1}{w _ {j} ^ {m _ {2} + \nu_ {1}}} \left[ w _ {j} ^ {m _ {2}} \left(\rho (w _ {j}) I _ {n} - h \sigma (w _ {j}) J _ {n} - h w _ {j} ^ {- m _ {1}} \sigma (w _ {j}) D _ {n} ^ {(1)}\right) - h \sigma (w _ {j}) D _ {n} ^ {(2)} \right].
$$

We therefore only need to prove that $\widetilde{S}_j$ ( $j = 1,2,\ldots,r_1 - \nu_1$ ) are invertible. We have the following theorem. 

Theorem 9.3 If the BVM with $(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})$ -boundary conditions is $A_{\nu_1,\nu_2}$ -stable and (9.14) holds, then for any arbitrary $\theta \in \mathbb{R}$ , the matrix 

$$
e ^ {\mathbf {i} m _ {2} \theta} \left(\rho (e ^ {\mathbf {i} \theta}) I _ {n} - h \sigma (e ^ {\mathbf {i} \theta}) J _ {n} - h e ^ {- \mathbf {i} m _ {1} \theta} \sigma (e ^ {\mathbf {i} \theta}) D _ {n} ^ {(1)}\right) - h \sigma (e ^ {\mathbf {i} \theta}) D _ {n} ^ {(2)}
$$

is invertible. It follows that the Strang-type preconditioner $\widetilde{S}$ defined by (9.18) is also invertible. 

Proof Suppose that there exist $\mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{C}^n$ with $\| \mathbf{x} \|_2 = 1$ and $\theta \in \mathbb{R}$ such that 

$$
\left[ e ^ {\mathbf {i} m _ {2} \theta} \left(\rho (e ^ {\mathbf {i} \theta}) I _ {n} - h \sigma (e ^ {\mathbf {i} \theta}) J _ {n} - h e ^ {- \mathbf {i} m _ {1} \theta} \sigma (e ^ {\mathbf {i} \theta}) D _ {n} ^ {(1)}\right) - h \sigma (e ^ {\mathbf {i} \theta}) D _ {n} ^ {(2)} \right] \mathbf {x} = \mathbf {0}.
$$

Then 

$$
\mathbf {x} ^ {*} \left[ \rho (e ^ {\mathbf {i} \theta}) I _ {n} - h \sigma (e ^ {\mathbf {i} \theta}) J _ {n} - h e ^ {- \mathbf {i} m _ {1} \theta} \sigma (e ^ {\mathbf {i} \theta}) D _ {n} ^ {(1)} - h e ^ {- \mathbf {i} m _ {2} \theta} \sigma (e ^ {\mathbf {i} \theta}) D _ {n} ^ {(2)} \right] \mathbf {x} = 0,
$$

i.e., 

$$
\rho (e ^ {\mathbf {i} \theta}) - h \sigma (e ^ {\mathbf {i} \theta}) \mathbf {x} ^ {*} J _ {n} \mathbf {x} - h e ^ {- \mathbf {i} m _ {1} \theta} \sigma (e ^ {\mathbf {i} \theta}) \mathbf {x} ^ {*} D _ {n} ^ {(1)} \mathbf {x} - h e ^ {- \mathbf {i} m _ {2} \theta} \sigma (e ^ {\mathbf {i} \theta}) \mathbf {x} ^ {*} D _ {n} ^ {(2)} \mathbf {x} = 0.
$$

We therefore have 

$$
\begin{array}{c} \rho (e ^ {\mathbf {i} \theta}) - (h \mathbf {x} ^ {*} J _ {n} \mathbf {x} + h e ^ {- \mathbf {i} m _ {1} \theta} \mathbf {x} ^ {*} D _ {n} ^ {(1)} \mathbf {x} + h e ^ {- \mathbf {i} m _ {2} \theta} \mathbf {x} ^ {*} D _ {n} ^ {(2)} \mathbf {x}) \sigma (e ^ {\mathbf {i} \theta}) \\ = \pi \left(e ^ {\mathbf {i} \theta}, h (\mathbf {x} ^ {*} J _ {n} \mathbf {x} + e ^ {- \mathbf {i} m _ {1} \theta} \mathbf {x} ^ {*} D _ {n} ^ {(1)} \mathbf {x} + e ^ {- \mathbf {i} m _ {2} \theta} \mathbf {x} ^ {*} D _ {n} ^ {(2)} \mathbf {x})\right) = 0, \end{array}
$$

where $\pi(z,q)$ is given by (9.11). Thus, 

$$
h \left(\mathbf {x} ^ {*} J _ {n} \mathbf {x} + e ^ {- \mathrm{i} m _ {1} \theta} \mathbf {x} ^ {*} D _ {n} ^ {(1)} \mathbf {x} + e ^ {- \mathrm{i} m _ {2} \theta} \mathbf {x} ^ {*} D _ {n} ^ {(2)} \mathbf {x}\right) \in \Gamma ,
$$

where $\Gamma$ is the boundary locus defined by (9.12). Since the BVM is $A_{\nu_1,\nu_2}$ -stable, from Lemma 9.1, we know that 

$$
\mathrm{Re} (\mathbf {x} ^ {*} J _ {n} \mathbf {x} + e ^ {- \mathbf {i} m _ {1} \theta} \mathbf {x} ^ {*} D _ {n} ^ {(1)} \mathbf {x} + e ^ {- \mathbf {i} m _ {2} \theta} \mathbf {x} ^ {*} D _ {n} ^ {(2)} \mathbf {x}) \geqslant 0.
$$

By Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have 

$$
\mathrm{Re} (e ^ {- \mathbf {i} m _ {1} \theta} \mathbf {x} ^ {*} D _ {n} ^ {(1)} \mathbf {x}) \leqslant | \mathbf {x} ^ {*} D _ {n} ^ {(1)} \mathbf {x} | \leqslant \| \mathbf {x} \| _ {2} \| D _ {n} ^ {(1)} \mathbf {x} \| _ {2} \leqslant \| D _ {n} ^ {(1)} \| _ {2} \| \mathbf {x} \| _ {2} = \| D _ {n} ^ {(1)} \| _ {2},
$$

and similarly, $\mathrm{Re}(e^{-\mathbf{i}m_1\theta}\mathbf{x}^* D_n^{(2)}\mathbf{x})\leqslant \| D_n^{(2)}\| _2$ . Note that 

$$
\eta (J _ {n}) = \max _ {\| \mathbf {x} \| _ {2} = 1} \operatorname{Re} (\mathbf {x} ^ {*} J _ {n} \mathbf {x}) \geqslant \operatorname{Re} (\mathbf {x} ^ {*} J _ {n} \mathbf {x}).
$$

Thus we have 

$$
\eta (J _ {n}) + \| D _ {n} ^ {(1)} \| _ {2} + \| D _ {n} ^ {(2)} \| _ {2} \geqslant 0,
$$

which is a contradiction to (9.14). Therefore, the matrix 

$$
e ^ {\mathbf {i} m _ {2} \theta} \left(\rho (e ^ {\mathbf {i} \theta}) I _ {n} - h \sigma (e ^ {\mathbf {i} \theta}) J _ {n} - h e ^ {- \mathbf {i} m _ {1} \theta} \sigma (e ^ {\mathbf {i} \theta}) D _ {n} ^ {(1)}\right) - h \sigma (e ^ {\mathbf {i} \theta}) D _ {n} ^ {(2)}
$$

is invertible and it follows that the Strang-type preconditioner $\widetilde{S}$ is also invertible. 

## 9.4.3 Convergence rate

Now, we discuss the convergence rate of the preconditioned GMRES method with the Strang-type block-circulant preconditioner. We have the following result for the spectra of preconditioned matrices [44]. 

Theorem 9.4 Let $R$ be given by (9.17) and $\tilde{S}$ be given by (9.18). Then we have 

$$
\widetilde {S} ^ {- 1} R = I _ {n (r _ {1} - \nu_ {1})} + L,
$$

where 

$$
\operatorname{rank} (L) \leqslant (2 \mu + m _ {1} + m _ {2} + 2 \nu_ {1} + 2) n.
$$

By Theorems 6.13 and 9.4, when the GMRES method is applied to 

$$
\widetilde {S} ^ {- 1} R \mathbf {y} = \widetilde {S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {b},
$$

the method converges in at most $(2\mu + m_{1} + m_{2} + 2\nu_{1} + 2)n + 1$ iterations in exact arithmetic. 

We know from Theorem 9.4 that if the step size $h = \tau_{1}/m_{1} = \tau_{2}/m_{2}$ is fixed, the number of iterations for convergence of the GMRES method, when applied to the preconditioned system 

$$
\widetilde {S} ^ {- 1} R \mathbf {y} = \widetilde {S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {b},
$$

is independent of $r_{1}$ and therefore is independent of the length of the interval that we considered. We should emphasize that the numerical example in Subsection 9.4.4 shows a much faster convergence rate than that predicted by the estimate provided by Theorem 9.4. For the operation cost of our algorithm, we refer to [44,58]. 

## 9.4.4 Numerical result

We illustrate the efficiency of our preconditioner by solving the following example. The BVM we used is the third order GBDF for $t \in [0, 4]$ . 

Example 9.2 Consider 

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \mathbf {y} ^ {\prime} (t) = J _ {n} \mathbf {y} (t) + D _ {n} ^ {(1)} \mathbf {y} (t - 0. 5) + D _ {n} ^ {(2)} \mathbf {y} (t - 1), & t \geqslant 0, \\ \mathbf {y} (t) = [ \sin t, 1, \ldots , 1 ] ^ {T}, & t \leqslant 0, \end{array} \right.
$$

where 

$$
J _ {n} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c} - 1 0 & 2 & & & \mathbf {0} \\ 2 & \ddots & \ddots & & \\ 1 & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & \\ & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & 2 \\ \mathbf {0} & & 1 & 2 & - 1 0 \end{array} \right], \quad D _ {n} ^ {(1)} = \frac {1}{n} \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c} 2 & - 1 & & \mathbf {0} \\ - 1 & \ddots & \ddots & \\ & \ddots & \ddots & - 1 \\ \mathbf {0} & & - 1 & 2 \end{array} \right],
$$

and 

$$
D _ {n} ^ {(2)} = \frac {1}{n} \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c} 2 & 1 & & \mathbf {0} \\ 1 & \ddots & \ddots & \\ & \ddots & \ddots & 1 \\ \mathbf {0} & & 1 & 2 \end{array} \right].
$$

In practice, we do not have the boundary values 

$$
\mathbf {y} _ {1}, \dots , \mathbf {y} _ {\nu_ {1} - 1}, \quad \mathbf {y} _ {r _ {1}}, \dots , \mathbf {y} _ {r _ {1} + \nu_ {2} - 1},
$$

provided by (9.16). Instead of giving the above values, as in Section 9.2, $\nu_{1} - 1$ initial additional equations and $\nu_{2}$ final additional equations are given. We remark that after introducing the additional equations, the matrices $A, B, C^{(1)}$ and $C^{(2)}$ in (9.17) are Toeplitz matrices with small rank perturbations. Neglecting the small rank perturbations, we can also construct the Strang-type preconditioner (9.18). 

Table 9.2 shows the number of iterations required for convergence of the GMRES method with different combinations of matrix size n and step size h. In the table, I means no preconditioner is used and $\widetilde{S}$ denotes the Strang-type block-circulant preconditioner defined by (9.18). We see that the numbers of iterations required for convergence increase slowly for increasing n and decreasing h under the column $\widetilde{S}$ .

## 9.5 Strang-type preconditioner for NDDEs

In this section, we study neutral delay differential equations (NDDEs). 

## 9.5.1 Neutral delay differential equations

We consider the solution of NDDE: 

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \mathbf {y} ^ {\prime} (t) = L _ {n} \mathbf {y} ^ {\prime} (t - \tau) + M _ {n} \mathbf {y} (t) + N _ {n} \mathbf {y} (t - \tau), & t \geqslant t _ {0}, \\ \mathbf {y} (t) = \phi (t), & t \leqslant t _ {0}, \end{array} \right.\tag{9.19}
$$

where $\mathbf{y}(t),\phi (t):\mathbb{R}\to \mathbb{R}^n$ $L_{n},M_{n},N_{n}\in \mathbb{R}^{n\times n}$ , and $\tau >0$ is a constant. 

As in Subsection 9.4.1, we want to find an asymptotically stable solution for (9.19). We have the following lemma [41,54]. 

Lemma 9.3 Let $L_{n}$ , $M_{n}$ and $N_{n}$ be any matrices with $\| L_{n}\|_{2} < 1$ . Then the solution of (9.19) is asymptotically stable if $\operatorname{Re}(\lambda_i) < 0$ where $\lambda_{i} (i = 1,2,\dots,n)$ are the eigenvalues of matrix 

$$
(I _ {n} - \eta L _ {n}) ^ {- 1} (M _ {n} + \eta N _ {n})
$$

with $|\eta| \leqslant 1$ . 

Let $h = \tau / k_1$ be the step size where $k_1$ is a positive integer. For (9.19), by using a BVM with $(\nu_1, \nu_2)$ -boundary conditions over a uniform mesh 

$$
t _ {j} = t _ {0} + j h, \quad j = 0, \dots , r _ {2},
$$

on the interval $[t_0, t_0 + r_2h]$ , we have 

$$
\sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\mu} \alpha_ {i} \mathbf {y} _ {p + i - \nu_ {1}} = \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\mu} \alpha_ {i} L _ {n} \mathbf {y} _ {p + i - \nu_ {1} - k _ {1}} + h \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\mu} \beta_ {i} (M _ {n} \mathbf {y} _ {p + i - \nu_ {1}} + N _ {n} \mathbf {y} _ {p + i - \nu_ {1} - k _ {1}}),\tag{9.20}
$$

for $p = \nu_{1},\ldots ,r_{2} - 1$ , where $\mu = \nu_{1} + \nu_{2}$ . By providing the values 

$$
\mathbf {y} - k _ {1}, \dots , \mathbf {y} _ {0}, \quad \mathbf {y} _ {1}, \dots , \mathbf {y} _ {\nu_ {1} - 1}, \quad \mathbf {y} _ {r _ {2}}, \dots , \mathbf {y} _ {r _ {2} + \nu_ {2} - 1},\tag{9.21}
$$

(9.20) can be written in a matrix form as 

$$
H \mathbf {y} = \mathbf {b},
$$

where 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & H \equiv A \otimes I _ {n} - A ^ {(1)} \otimes L _ {n} - h B \otimes M _ {n} - h B ^ {(1)} \otimes N _ {n}, \\ & \mathbf {y} = [ \mathbf {y} _ {\nu_ {1}} ^ {T}, \mathbf {y} _ {\nu_ {1} + 1} ^ {T}, \dots , \mathbf {y} _ {r _ {2} - 1} ^ {T} ] ^ {T} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n (r _ {2} - \nu_ {1})}, \end{array}\tag{9.22}
$$

$\mathbf{b} \in \mathbb{R}^{n(r_2 - \nu_1)}$ depends on the boundary values and the coefficients of the method. In (9.22), the matrices $A, B \in \mathbb{R}^{(r_2 - \nu_1) \times (r_2 - \nu_1)}$ are defined as in (9.4), and $A^{(1)}$ , $B^{(1)} \in \mathbb{R}^{(r_2 - \nu_1) \times (r_2 - \nu_1)}$ are given as follows [5]: 

$$
A ^ {(1)} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c c} \mathbf {0} & & & & & \mathbf {0} \\ \alpha_ {\mu} & \ddots & & & & \\ \vdots & \ddots & \ddots & & & \\ \alpha_ {0} & \dots & \alpha_ {\mu} & \ddots & & \\ & \ddots & & \ddots & \ddots & \\ \mathbf {0} & & \alpha_ {0} & \dots & \alpha_ {\mu} & \mathbf {0} \end{array} \right], \qquad B ^ {(1)} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c c} \mathbf {0} & & & & & \mathbf {0} \\ \beta_ {\mu} & \ddots & & & & \\ \vdots & \ddots & \ddots & & \\ \beta_ {0} & \dots & \beta_ {\mu} & \ddots & \\ & \ddots & & \ddots & \ddots \\ \mathbf {0} & & \beta_ {0} & \dots & \beta_ {\mu} & \mathbf {0} \end{array} \right]
$$

We remark that the first column of $A^{(1)}$ is given by: 

$$
[ \underbrace {0 , \ldots , 0} _ {k _ {1} - \nu_ {2}}, \alpha_ {\mu}, \ldots , \alpha_ {0}, \underbrace {0 , \ldots , 0} _ {r _ {2} - k _ {1} - 2 \nu_ {1} - 1} ] ^ {T}
$$

and the first column of $B^{(1)}$ is given by 

$$
[ \underbrace {0 , \ldots , 0} _ {k _ {1} - \nu_ {2}}, \beta_ {\mu}, \ldots , \beta_ {0}, \underbrace {0 , \ldots , 0} _ {r _ {2} - k _ {1} - 2 \nu_ {1} - 1} ] ^ {T}.
$$

## 9.5.2 Construction of preconditioner

The Strang-type block-circulant preconditioner for (9.22) is defined as follows: 

$$
\bar {S} \equiv s (A) \otimes I _ {n} - s (A ^ {(1)}) \otimes L _ {n} - h s (B) \otimes M _ {n} - h s (B ^ {(1)}) \otimes N _ {n},\tag{9.23}
$$

where $s(E)$ is Strang's circulant preconditioner of Toeplitz matrix $E$ , for $E = A, B, A^{(1)}, B^{(1)}$ respectively. By using (6.19) again, we have 

$$
\bar {S} = \left(F ^ {*} \otimes I _ {n}\right) \left(\Lambda_ {A} \otimes I _ {n} - \Lambda_ {A ^ {(1)}} \otimes L _ {n} - h \Lambda_ {B} \otimes M _ {n} - h \Lambda_ {B ^ {(1)}} \otimes N _ {n}\right) (F \otimes I _ {n}),
$$

where $\Lambda_{E}$ is the diagonal matrix holding the eigenvalues of $s(E)$ , for E = A, B, $A^{(1)}$ , $B^{(1)}$ respectively. 

Now we discuss the invertibility of the Strang-type preconditioner $\bar{S}$ . Let 

$$
w _ {j} = e ^ {\frac {2 \pi \mathrm{i} j}{r 2 - \nu_ {1}}}.
$$

We have 

$$
[ \Lambda_ {A} ] _ {j j} = \rho (w _ {j}) / w _ {j} ^ {\nu_ {1}}, \quad [ \Lambda_ {B} ] _ {j j} = \sigma (w _ {j}) / w _ {j} ^ {\nu_ {1}},
$$

$$
\left[ \Lambda_ {A ^ {(1)}} \right] _ {j j} = \alpha_ {\mu} w _ {j} ^ {- k _ {1} + \nu_ {2}} + \dots + \alpha_ {0} w _ {j} ^ {- k _ {1} - \nu_ {1}} = \rho (w _ {j}) / w _ {j} ^ {k _ {1} + \nu_ {1}},
$$

and 

$$
[ \Lambda_ {B ^ {(1)}} ] _ {j j} = \beta_ {\mu} w _ {j} ^ {- k _ {1} + \nu_ {2}} + \dots + \beta_ {0} w _ {j} ^ {- k _ {1} - \nu_ {1}} = \sigma (w _ {j}) / w _ {j} ^ {k _ {1} + \nu_ {1}},
$$

where $\rho(z)$ and $\sigma(z)$ are defined as in (9.10). Thus the jth block of 

$$
\Lambda_ {A} \otimes I _ {n} - \Lambda_ {A ^ {(1)}} \otimes L _ {n} - h \Lambda_ {B} \otimes M _ {n} - h \Lambda_ {B ^ {(1)}} \otimes N _ {n}
$$

in $\bar{S}$ is given by 

$$
\begin{array}{r c l} \bar {S} _ {j} & = & [ \Lambda_ {A} ] _ {j j} I _ {n} - [ \Lambda_ {A ^ {(1)}} ] _ {j j} L _ {n} - h [ \Lambda_ {B} ] _ {j j} M _ {n} - h [ \Lambda_ {B ^ {(1)}} ] _ {j j} N _ {n} \\ & = & \frac {1}{w _ {j} ^ {k _ {1} + \nu_ {1}}} \left[ w _ {j} ^ {k _ {1}} (\rho (w _ {j}) I _ {n} - h \sigma (w _ {j}) M _ {n}) - \rho (w _ {j}) L _ {n} - h \sigma (w _ {j}) N _ {n} \right], \end{array}
$$

for $j = 1, 2, \ldots, r_{2} - \nu_{1}$ . In order to prove that $\bar{S}$ is invertible, we only need to show that $\bar{S}_{j}$ ( $j = 1, 2, \ldots, r_{2} - \nu_{1}$ ) are invertible. Let 

$$
\begin{array}{r c l} \Delta & \equiv & e ^ {\mathbf {i} k _ {1} \theta} \left(\rho (e ^ {\mathbf {i} \theta}) I _ {n} - h \sigma (e ^ {\mathbf {i} \theta}) M _ {n}\right) - \rho (e ^ {\mathbf {i} \theta}) L _ {n} - h \sigma (e ^ {\mathbf {i} \theta}) N _ {n} \\ & = & e ^ {\mathbf {i} k _ {1} \theta} (I _ {n} - e ^ {- \mathbf {i} k _ {1} \theta} L _ {n}) D, \end{array}
$$

where 

$$
D \equiv \rho (e ^ {\mathbf {i} \theta}) I _ {n} - h (I _ {n} - e ^ {- \mathbf {i} k _ {1} \theta} L _ {n}) ^ {- 1} (M _ {n} + e ^ {- \mathbf {i} k _ {1} \theta} N _ {n}) \sigma (e ^ {\mathbf {i} \theta}).\tag{9.24}
$$

Hence, we are required to show that $\Delta$ is invertible for any $\theta \in \mathbb{R}$ in order to prove that $\bar{S}_j$ is invertible. Assume that $\| L_n\| _2 < 1$ , we have $I_{n} - e^{-\mathrm{i}k_{1}\theta}L_{n}$ is nonsingular for any $\theta \in \mathbb{R}$ . Therefore, we only need to show $D$ is invertible for any $\theta \in \mathbb{R}$ . We have the following theorem [5]. 

Theorem 9.5 If the BVM with $(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})$ -boundary conditions is $A_{\nu_1,\nu_2}$ -stable and $\operatorname{Re}(\lambda_i) < 0$ where $\lambda_{i}$ ( $i = 1,2,\ldots,n$ ) are the eigenvalues of matrix 

$$
(I _ {n} - \eta L _ {n}) ^ {- 1} (M _ {n} + \eta N _ {n})
$$

with $|\eta| \leqslant 1$ , then for any $\theta \in \mathbb{R}$ , the matrix $D$ defined by (9.24) is invertible. It follows that the Strang-type preconditioner $\bar{S}$ defined as in (9.23) is also invertible. 

Proof Let 

$$
U \equiv (I _ {n} - e ^ {- \mathbf {i} m \theta} L _ {n}) ^ {- 1} (M _ {n} + e ^ {- \mathbf {i} m \theta} N _ {n}).
$$

Then $D$ can be written as 

$$
D = \rho (z) I _ {n} - h U \sigma (z).
$$

Note that the eigenvalues of $D$ are given by 

$$
\lambda_ {i} (D) = \rho (z) - h \lambda_ {i} (U) \sigma (z), \quad i = 1, \dots , n,
$$

where $\lambda_{i}(U)$ ( $i = 1,2,\ldots,n$ ) denote the eigenvalues of $U$ . Since we know that 

$$
\operatorname{Re} [ \lambda_ {i} (U) ] <   0, \quad i = 1, \dots , n,
$$

it follows that $h\lambda_{i}(U) \in \mathbb{C}^{-}$ . Note that the BVM is $A_{\nu_1, \nu_2}$ -stable and then we have 

$$
h \lambda_ {i} (U) \in \mathbb {C} ^ {-} \subseteq \mathcal {D} _ {\nu_ {1}, \nu_ {2}}.
$$

Therefore, the $A_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}$ -stability polynomial defined by (9.11) 

$$
\pi [ z, h \lambda_ {i} (U) ] \equiv \rho (z) - h \lambda_ {i} (U) \sigma (z)
$$

has no roots on the unit circle $|z| = 1$ . Thus, for any $|z| = 1$ , we have 

$$
\lambda_ {i} (D) = \rho (z) - h \lambda_ {i} (U) \sigma (z) = \pi [ z, h \lambda_ {i} (U) ] \neq 0, \quad i = 1, \dots , n.
$$

It follows that $D$ is invertible. Therefore, the Strang-type preconditioner $\bar{S}$ defined as in (9.23) is also invertible. 

## 9.5.3 Convergence rate

We have the following result for the spectra of preconditioned matrices [5]. 

Theorem 9.6 Let $H$ be given by (9.22) and $\bar{S}$ be given by (9.23). Then we have 

$$
\bar {S} ^ {- 1} H = I _ {n (r _ {2} - \nu_ {1})} + L,
$$

where 

$$
\operatorname{rank} (L) \leqslant 2 (\mu + k _ {1} + \nu_ {1} + 1) n.
$$

By Theorems 6.13 and 9.6, when the GMRES method is applied to 

$$
\bar {S} ^ {- 1} H \mathbf {y} = \bar {S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {b},
$$

the method converges in at most $2(\mu + k_1 + \nu_1 + 1)n + 1$ iterations in exact arithmetic. 

We observe from Theorem 9.6 that if the step size $h = \tau / k_{1}$ is fixed, the number of iterations for convergence of the GMRES method, when applied for solving 

$$
\bar {S} ^ {- 1} H \mathbf {y} = \bar {S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {b},
$$

is independent of $r_{2}$ , i.e., the length of the interval that we considered. 

## 9.5.4 Numerical result

We illustrate the efficiency of our preconditioner by solving the following example. 

Example 9.3 Consider 

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \mathbf {y} ^ {\prime} (t) = L _ {n} \mathbf {y} ^ {\prime} (t - 1) + M _ {n} \mathbf {y} (t) + N _ {n} \mathbf {y} (t - 1), & t \geqslant 0, \\ \mathbf {y} (t) = [ 1, 1, \ldots , 1 ] ^ {T}, & t \leqslant 0, \end{array} \right.
$$

where 

$$
L _ {n} = \frac {1}{n} \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c} 2 & 1 & & \mathbf {0} \\ 1 & \ddots & \ddots & \\ & \ddots & \ddots & 1 \\ \mathbf {0} & & 1 & 2 \end{array} \right], \quad M _ {n} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c} - 8 & 2 & 1 & & \mathbf {0} \\ 2 & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & \\ 1 & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & 1 \\ & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & 2 \\ \mathbf {0} & & 1 & 2 & - 8 \end{array} \right],
$$

and 

$$
N _ {n} = \frac {1}{n} \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c} 2 & - 1 & & \mathbf {0} \\ - 1 & \ddots & \ddots & \\ & \ddots & \ddots & - 1 \\ \mathbf {0} & & - 1 & 2 \end{array} \right].
$$

Example 9.3 is solved by using the fifth order GAM for $t \in [0,4]$ . In practice, we do not have the boundary values 

$$
\mathbf {y} _ {1}, \dots , \mathbf {y} _ {\nu_ {1} - 1}, \quad \mathbf {y} _ {r _ {2}}, \dots , \mathbf {y} _ {r _ {2} + \nu_ {2} - 1},
$$

provided by (9.21). Again as in Section 9.2, instead of giving the above values, $\nu_{1} - 1$ initial additional equations and $\nu_{2}$ final additional equations are given. After introducing the additional equations, the matrices $A, A^{(1)}, B$ and $B^{(2)}$ in (9.22) are 

<table><tr><td>n</td><td>k1</td><td>I</td><td><eq>\bar{S}</eq></td></tr><tr><td>24</td><td>10</td><td>43</td><td>7</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>20</td><td>83</td><td>7</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>40</td><td>161</td><td>7</td></tr><tr><td></td><td>80</td><td>*</td><td>7</td></tr></table>

Toeplitz matrices with small rank perturbations. We can also construct the Strang-type preconditioner (9.23) by neglecting the small rank perturbations. 

Table 9.3 lists the number of iterations required for convergence of the GMRES method for different n and $k_{1}$ . In the table, I means no preconditioner is used and $\bar{S}$ denotes the Strang-type block-circulant preconditioner defined by (9.23). We see that the number of iterations required for convergence, when $\bar{S}$ is used, is much less than that when no preconditioner is used. We should emphasize that our numerical example shows a much faster convergence rate than that predicted by the estimate provided by Theorem 9.6. 


Table 9.3 Number of iterations for convergence ('*' means out of memory).


## 9.6 Strang-type preconditioner for SPDDEs

In this section, we study the solution of singular perturbation delay differential equations (SPDDEs). 

## 9.6.1 Singular perturbation delay differential equations

We consider the solution of SPDDE: 

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \mathbf {x} ^ {\prime} (t) = V ^ {(1)} \mathbf {x} (t) + V ^ {(2)} \mathbf {x} (t - \tau) + C ^ {(1)} \mathbf {y} (t) + C ^ {(2)} \mathbf {y} (t - \tau), & t \geqslant t _ {0}, \\ \epsilon \mathbf {y} ^ {\prime} (t) = F ^ {(1)} \mathbf {x} (t) + F ^ {(2)} \mathbf {x} (t - \tau) + G ^ {(1)} \mathbf {y} (t) + G ^ {(2)} \mathbf {y} (t - \tau), & t \geqslant t _ {0}, \\ \mathbf {x} (t) = \phi (t), & t \leqslant t _ {0}, \\ \mathbf {y} (t) = \psi (t), & t \leqslant t _ {0}, \end{array} \right.\tag{9.25}
$$

where 

$$
\begin{array}{l l} \mathbf {x} (t), \phi (t): \mathbb {R} \to \mathbb {R} ^ {m}; & \quad \mathbf {y} (t), \psi (t): \mathbb {R} \to \mathbb {R} ^ {n}; \\ V ^ {(1)}, V ^ {(2)} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {m \times m}; & \quad C ^ {(1)}, C ^ {(2)} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {m \times n}; \\ F ^ {(1)}, F ^ {(2)} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n \times m}; & \quad G ^ {(1)}, G ^ {(2)} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {n \times n}; \end{array}
$$

and $\tau > 0$ , $0 < \epsilon \ll 1$ are constants. We can rewrite SPDDE (9.25) as the following initial value problem [45]: 

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l l} {\mathbf {z} ^ {\prime} (t) = P \mathbf {z} (t) + Q \mathbf {z} (t - \tau),} & {t \geqslant t _ {0},} \\ & \\ {\mathbf {z} (t) = \left[ \begin{array}{c} {\boldsymbol {\phi} (t)} \\ {\boldsymbol {\psi} (t)} \end{array} \right],} & {t \leqslant t _ {0},} \end{array} \right.\tag{9.26}
$$

where $P$ and $Q \in \mathbb{R}^{(m+n) \times (m+n)}$ are defined as follows, 

$$
P = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} V ^ {(1)} & C ^ {(1)} \\ \epsilon^ {- 1} F ^ {(1)} & \epsilon^ {- 1} G ^ {(1)} \end{array} \right], \qquad Q = \left[ \begin{array}{c c} V ^ {(2)} & C ^ {(2)} \\ \epsilon^ {- 1} F ^ {(2)} & \epsilon^ {- 1} G ^ {(2)} \end{array} \right].
$$

Let 

$$
h = \tau / k _ {2}
$$

be the step size where $k_{2}$ is a positive integer. For (9.26), by using a BVM with $(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})$ -boundary conditions over a uniform mesh 

$$
t _ {j} = t _ {0} + j h, \quad j = 0, \dots , v,
$$

on the interval $[t_{0}, t_{0} + v h]$ , we have 

$$
\sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\mu} \alpha_ {i} \mathbf {z} _ {p + i - \nu_ {1}} = h \sum_ {i = 0} ^ {\mu} \beta_ {i} (P \mathbf {z} _ {p + i - \nu_ {1}} + Q \mathbf {z} _ {p + i - \nu_ {1} - k _ {2}}),\tag{9.27}
$$

for $p = \nu_{1}, \ldots, v - 1$ , where $\mu = \nu_{1} + \nu_{2}$ . By providing the values 

$$
\mathbf {Z} _ {- k _ {2}}, \dots , \mathbf {Z} _ {0}, \quad \mathbf {Z} _ {1}, \dots , \mathbf {Z} _ {\nu_ {1} - 1}, \quad \mathbf {Z} _ {v}, \dots , \mathbf {Z} _ {v + \nu_ {2} - 1},\tag{9.28}
$$

(9.27) can be written in a matrix form as 

$$
K \mathbf {v} = \mathbf {b},\tag{9.29}
$$

where 

$$
K \equiv A \otimes I _ {m + n} - h B \otimes P - h U \otimes Q.\tag{9.30}
$$

The vector $\mathbf{v}$ in (9.29) is defined by 

$$
\mathbf {v} = \left[ \mathbf {z} _ {\nu_ {1}} ^ {T}, \mathbf {z} _ {\nu_ {1} + 1} ^ {T}, \dots , \mathbf {z} _ {v - 1} ^ {T} \right] ^ {T} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {(m + n) (v - \nu_ {1})}.
$$

The right-hand side $\mathbf{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{(m+n)(v-\nu_{1})}$ of (9.29) depends on the boundary values and the coefficients of the method. The matrices A, $B\in\mathbb{R}^{(v-\nu_{1})\times(v-\nu_{1})}$ in (9.30) are defined as in (9.4) and $U\in\mathbb{R}^{(v-\nu_{1})\times(v-\nu_{1})}$ in (9.30) is defined as the matrix $C^{(1)}$ in (9.17). 

## 9.6.2 Construction of preconditioner

The Strang-type block-circulant preconditioner can be constructed for solving (9.29): 

$$
\widehat {S} \equiv s (A) \otimes I _ {m + n} - h s (B) \otimes P - h s (U) \otimes Q,\tag{9.31}
$$

where $s(E)$ is Strang's circulant preconditioner of matrix $E$ , for $E = A, B, U$ respectively. We have the following theorem for the invertibility of our preconditioner $\widehat{S}$ and for the convergence rate of our method [45]. 

Theorem 9.7 If the BVM with $(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})$ -boundary conditions is $A_{\nu_1,\nu_2}$ -stable, 

$$
\eta (P) \equiv \frac {1}{2} \lambda_ {\max} (P + P ^ {T}) <   0, \qquad \eta (P) + \| Q \| _ {2} <   0,
$$

then the Strang-type preconditioner $\widehat{S}$ defined by (9.31) is invertible. Moreover, when the GMRES method is applied to 

$$
\widehat {S} ^ {- 1} K \mathbf {v} = \widehat {S} ^ {- 1} \mathbf {b},
$$

the method converges in at most $\mathcal{O}(m + n)$ iterations in exact arithmetic. 

## 9.6.3 Numerical result

We test the following SPDDE. 

Example 9.4 Consider 

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \mathbf {x} ^ {\prime} (t) = A \mathbf {x} (t) + B \mathbf {x} (t - 1) + C \mathbf {y} (t) + D \mathbf {y} (t - 1), & t \geqslant 0, \\ \epsilon \mathbf {y} ^ {\prime} (t) = E \mathbf {x} (t) + F \mathbf {x} (t - 1) + G \mathbf {y} (t) + H \mathbf {y} (t - 1), & t \geqslant 0, \quad \epsilon = 0. 0 0 1, \\ \mathbf {x} (t) = [ 1, 1, \ldots , 1 ] ^ {T}, & t \leqslant 0, \\ \mathbf {y} (t) = [ 2, 2, \ldots , 2 ] ^ {T}, & t \leqslant 0, \end{array} \right.
$$

where 

$$
A = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c} - 1 0 & 2 & 1 & & \mathbf {0} \\ 2 & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & \\ 1 & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & 1 \\ & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & 2 \\ \mathbf {0} & & 1 & 2 & - 1 0 \end{array} \right] _ {m \times m}, \quad B = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c} - 2 & 1 & & \mathbf {0} \\ 1 & \ddots & \ddots & \\ & \ddots & \ddots & 1 \\ \mathbf {0} & & 1 & - 2 \end{array} \right] _ {m \times m},
$$

$$
C = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c} 1 & & \mathbf {0} \\ & \ddots & \\ & & 1 \\ \mathbf {0} & & \end{array} \right] _ {m \times n}, \quad D = 3 C, \quad E = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c c} 2 & - 1 & & & & \mathbf {0} \\ 1 & \ddots & \ddots & & \\ & \ddots & \ddots & - 1 & & \\ \mathbf {0} & & 1 & 2 & - 1 \end{array} \right] _ {n \times m},
$$

$$
F = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c c} 1 & - 1 & & & & \mathbf {0} \\ & \ddots & \ddots & & & \\ & & \ddots & - 1 & & \\ \mathbf {0} & & & 1 & - 1 \end{array} \right] _ {n \times m}, \quad G = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c} - 2 & & & \mathbf {0} \\ 1 & \ddots & & \\ & \ddots & \ddots & \\ \mathbf {0} & & 1 & - 2 \end{array} \right] _ {n \times n},
$$

and 

$$
H = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c} - 5 & - 1 & & \mathbf {0} \\ 2 & \ddots & \ddots & \\ & \ddots & \ddots & - 1 \\ \mathbf {0} & & 2 & - 5 \end{array} \right] _ {n \times n}.
$$

Example 9.4 is solved by using the third order GAM for $t \in [0,4]$ . Table 9.4 lists the number of iterations required for convergence of the GMRES method for different $m$ , $n$ and $k_{2}$ . In the table, $\widehat{S}$ denotes the Strang-type block-circulant preconditioner defined by (9.31).
