## Chapter 10

## Applications in FDEs

In this chapter, we briefly study the Crank-Nicolson weighted and shifted Grünwald difference (CN-WSGD) scheme for solving two-dimensional space-fractional diffusion equations. This method requires the solution of one or more nonsymmetric, large and full linear systems. The GMRES method introduced in Chapter 6 together with restart strategy and preconditioning techniques is used for solving these linear systems. One of the main results is that when the restarted GMRES method is applied to the preconditioned systems, the computational cost is $\mathcal{O}(N^2\log N)$ at each time step, where $N = N_{1} = N_{2}$ is the number of spatial partitions in $x$ -direction and $y$ -direction (see Section 10.2). For a literature on fractional diffusion equations (FDEs), one can refer to [7,31,56,60,68]. 

## 10.1 Introduction

We consider the following initial boundary value problem of two-dimensional space-fractional diffusion equation [62,89]: 

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l} \frac {\partial u (x , y , t)}{\partial t} = d _ {+} (x, y, t) _ {a} D _ {x} ^ {\alpha} u (x, y, t) + d _ {-} (x, y, t) _ {x} D _ {b} ^ {\alpha} u (x, y, t) \\ \qquad + e _ {+} (x, y, t) _ {c} D _ {y} ^ {\beta} u (x, y, t) + e _ {-} (x, y, t) _ {y} D _ {d} ^ {\beta} u (x, y, t) + f (x, y, t), \\ \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad \qquad (x, y, t) \in \Omega \times (0, T ], \\ u (x, y, 0) = u _ {0} (x, y), \qquad (x, y) \in \overline {{\Omega}}, \\ u (x, y, t) = 0, \qquad (x, y, t) \in \partial \Omega \times [ 0, T ], \end{array} \right.\tag{10.1}
$$

where $1 < \alpha, \beta < 2$ , $d_{\pm}(x,y,t), e_{\pm}(x,y,t) \geqslant 0$ , and $\Omega = (a,b) \times (c,d)$ . In addition, ${}_a D_x^\alpha, {}_x D_b^\alpha, {}_c D_y^\beta$ and ${}_y D_d^\beta$ are the left-sided and right-sided Riemann-Liouville 

fractional derivatives defined by 

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l l} _ {a} D _ {x} ^ {\alpha} u (x, y, t) \equiv \frac {1}{\Gamma (2 - \alpha)} \frac {\mathrm{d} ^ {2}}{\mathrm{d} x ^ {2}} \int_ {a} ^ {x} \frac {u (\xi , y , t)}{(x - \xi) ^ {\alpha - 1}} \mathrm{d} \xi , \\ _ {x} D _ {b} ^ {\alpha} u (x, y, t) \equiv \frac {1}{\Gamma (2 - \alpha)} \frac {\mathrm{d} ^ {2}}{\mathrm{d} x ^ {2}} \int_ {x} ^ {b} \frac {u (\xi , y , t)}{(\xi - x) ^ {\alpha - 1}} \mathrm{d} \xi , \\ _ {c} D _ {y} ^ {\beta} u (x, y, t) \equiv \frac {1}{\Gamma (2 - \beta)} \frac {\mathrm{d} ^ {2}}{\mathrm{d} y ^ {2}} \int_ {c} ^ {y} \frac {u (x , \eta , t)}{(y - \eta) ^ {\beta - 1}} \mathrm{d} \eta , \\ _ {y} D _ {d} ^ {\beta} u (x, y, t) \equiv \frac {1}{\Gamma (2 - \beta)} \frac {\mathrm{d} ^ {2}}{\mathrm{d} y ^ {2}} \int_ {y} ^ {d} \frac {u (x , \eta , t)}{(\eta - y) ^ {\beta - 1}} \mathrm{d} \eta . \end{array} \right.
$$

Here, the Gamma function $\Gamma(z)$ is defined by the following integral [68]: 

$$
\Gamma (z) \equiv \int_ {0} ^ {\infty} e ^ {- t} t ^ {z - 1} \mathrm{d} t, \qquad \operatorname{Re} (z) > 0,
$$

where $\operatorname{Re}(\cdot)$ denotes the real part of a complex number. 

To approximate FDE with a left-sided fractional derivative and the FDE with two-sided Riemann-Liouville fractional derivatives, Meerschaert and Tadjeran proposed a shifted Grünwald discretization scheme $[61,62]$ . Their methods were proved to be unconditionally stable. In 2012, the Crank-Nicolson weighted and shifted Grünwald difference (CN-WSGD) scheme was proposed by Tian, Zhou and Deng $[79]$ . Using the CN-WSGD scheme to discretize $(10.1)$ , we can obtain a sequence of linear systems 

$$
\underline {{{A}}} ^ {(m + 1)} \mathbf {u} ^ {(m + 1)} = \mathbf {b} ^ {(m + 1)}, \quad m = 0, 1, \dots , M - 1,
$$

where $M$ is the number of temporal partitions. 

The above coefficient matrices possess a special Toeplitz-like structure: it can be written as a sum of diagonal-multiply-Toeplitz matrices [46, 79, 90]. These linear systems are nonsymmetric and large, and the restarted GMRES method is used for solving these linear systems. Numerical result shows that the discretized systems may become very ill-conditioned when the diffusion coefficients are not small and the grid size is small. In order to accelerate the convergence of restarted GMRES method, a preconditioner defined by the incomplete LU factorization of a block-banded-banded-block approximation of the coefficient matrix is constructed by extending a banded preconditioner proposed in [59]. One advantage of this preconditioned iterative method is that it requires only $\mathcal{O}(N^{2}\log N)$ operations at each time step by using the Toeplitz-like structure of the coefficient matrix. 

The outline of this chapter is organized as follows. In Section 10.2, we discretize the FDE (10.1) by the CN-WSGD scheme, and the matrix form of this finite difference method is presented. Then we construct a preconditioner for the discretized linear systems of FDE (10.1) in Section 10.3. Finally, numerical result is given to show the efficiency of the preconditioner. 

## 10.2 The CN-WSGD scheme

## 10.2.1 Disretization of FDEs

In this subsection, we use the CN-WSGD scheme to discrete the FDE (10.1). The time interval $[0,T]$ is partitioned into M subintervals of length $\tau = T/M$ , and the spatial domain $\Omega$ is partitioned uniformly with grid sizes $h_{x} = (b - a)/(N_{1} + 1)$ and $h_{y} = (d - c)/(N_{2} + 1)$ , where M, $N_{1}$ and $N_{2}$ are positive integers. Let $x_{i} = a + ih_{x}$ for $i = 0, 1, \ldots, N_{1} + 1$ , $y_{j} = c + jh_{y}$ for $j = 0, 1, \ldots, N_{2} + 1$ , and $t^{(m)} = m\tau$ for $m = 0, 1, \ldots, M$ . Let $t^{(m+1/2)} = (t^{(m)} + t^{(m+1)})/2$ for $m = 1, 2, \ldots, M - 1$ . 

In the following, we consider numerical methods for the solution of the discretized linear systems, which are obtained by using the second-order accurate CN-WSGD scheme with shifting parameters $(p,q)=(1,0)$ given by [79]: 

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l} _ {L} \mathcal {D} _ {h _ {x}} ^ {\alpha} u (x _ {i}, y _ {j}, t ^ {(m)}) = \frac {1}{h _ {x} ^ {\alpha}} \sum_ {k = 0} ^ {i} w _ {k} ^ {(\alpha)} u (x _ {i - k + 1}, y _ {j}, t ^ {(m)}), \\ _ {R} \mathcal {D} _ {h _ {x}} ^ {\alpha} u (x _ {i}, y _ {j}, t ^ {(m)}) = \frac {1}{h _ {x} ^ {\alpha}} \sum_ {k = 0} ^ {N _ {1} - i + 1} w _ {k} ^ {(\alpha)} u (x _ {i + k - 1}, y _ {j}, t ^ {(m)}), \\ _ {L} \mathcal {D} _ {h _ {y}} ^ {\beta} u (x _ {i}, y _ {j}, t ^ {(m)}) = \frac {1}{h _ {y} ^ {\beta}} \sum_ {k = 0} ^ {j} w _ {k} ^ {(\beta)} u (x _ {i}, y _ {j - k + 1}, t ^ {(m)}), \\ _ {R} \mathcal {D} _ {h _ {y}} ^ {\beta} u (x _ {i}, y _ {j}, t ^ {(m)}) = \frac {1}{h _ {y} ^ {\beta}} \sum_ {k = 0} ^ {N _ {2} - j + 1} w _ {k} ^ {(\beta)} u (x _ {i}, y _ {j + k - 1}, t ^ {(m)}). \end{array} \right.
$$

Here the coefficients $w_{k}^{(\gamma)}$ are given by 

$$
w _ {0} ^ {(\gamma)} = \frac {\gamma}{2} g _ {0} ^ {(\gamma)}, \quad w _ {k} ^ {(\gamma)} = \frac {\gamma}{2} g _ {k} ^ {(\gamma)} + \frac {2 - \gamma}{2} g _ {k - 1} ^ {(\gamma)}, \quad k \geqslant 1,
$$

where $g_{k}^{(\gamma)} = (-1)^{k}\binom{\gamma}{k}$ with $\binom{\gamma}{k}$ being the fractional binomial coefficients. For $\gamma = \alpha, \beta$ , the fractional binomial coefficients $g_{j}^{(\gamma)}$ have the following properties [61, 

62, 66, 87]: 

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l l} g _ {0} ^ {(\gamma)} = 1, \quad g _ {1} ^ {(\gamma)} = - \gamma , \quad g _ {2} ^ {(\gamma)} > g _ {3} ^ {(\gamma)} > \dots > 0, \\ \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} g _ {j} ^ {(\gamma)} = 0, \quad \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {n} g _ {j} ^ {(\gamma)} <   0, \quad n \geqslant 1, \\ g _ {j} ^ {(\gamma)} = \mathcal {O} (j ^ {- (\gamma + 1)}). \end{array} \right.\tag{10.2}
$$

Similarly, the coefficients $w_{k}^{(\gamma)}$ have the following basic properties [79]. 

Lemma 10.1 The coefficients $w_k^{(\gamma)}$ satisfy the following properties for $1 < \gamma < 2$ , 

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l l} w _ {0} ^ {(\gamma)} = \frac {\gamma}{2}, \quad w _ {1} ^ {(\gamma)} = \frac {2 - \gamma - \gamma^ {2}}{2} <   0, \quad w _ {2} ^ {(\gamma)} = \frac {\gamma (\gamma^ {2} + \gamma - 4)}{4}, \\ 1 \geqslant w _ {0} ^ {(\gamma)} \geqslant w _ {3} ^ {(\gamma)} \geqslant w _ {4} ^ {(\gamma)} \geqslant \dots \geqslant 0, \\ \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {\infty} w _ {j} ^ {(\gamma)} = 0, \quad \sum_ {j = 0} ^ {n} w _ {j} ^ {(\gamma)} <   0, \quad n \geqslant 2. \end{array} \right.
$$

Moreover, we have 

$$
w _ {j} ^ {(\gamma)} = \mathcal {O} (j ^ {- (\gamma + 1)}).
$$

Let 

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \delta_ {x} ^ {\alpha , (m)} = \frac {d _ {i , j} ^ {+ , (m)} \tau}{2} _ {L} \mathcal {D} _ {h _ {x}} ^ {\alpha} + \frac {d _ {i , j} ^ {- , (m)} \tau}{2} _ {R} \mathcal {D} _ {h _ {x}} ^ {\alpha}, \\ \delta_ {y} ^ {\beta , (m)} = \frac {e _ {i , j} ^ {+ , (m)} \tau}{2} _ {L} \mathcal {D} _ {h _ {y}} ^ {\beta} + \frac {e _ {i , j} ^ {- , (m)} \tau}{2} _ {R} \mathcal {D} _ {h _ {y}} ^ {\beta}, \end{array} \right.
$$

where 

$$
\begin{array}{l l} d _ {i, j} ^ {+, (m)} = d _ {+} (x _ {i}, y _ {j}, t ^ {(m)}), & d _ {i, j} ^ {-, (m)} = d _ {-} (x _ {i}, y _ {j}, t ^ {(m)}), \\ e _ {i, j} ^ {+, (m)} = e _ {+} (x _ {i}, y _ {j}, t ^ {(m)}), & e _ {i, j} ^ {-, (m)} = e _ {-} (x _ {i}, y _ {j}, t ^ {(m)}). \end{array}
$$

The two-dimensional CN-WSGD finite difference method can be formulated as follows: 

$$
\left(1 - \delta_ {x} ^ {\alpha , (m + 1)} - \delta_ {y} ^ {\beta , (m + 1)}\right) u _ {i, j} ^ {(m + 1)} = \left(1 + \delta_ {x} ^ {\alpha , (m)} + \delta_ {y} ^ {\beta , (m)}\right) u _ {i, j} ^ {(m)} + \tau f _ {i, j} ^ {(m + 1 / 2)},\tag{10.3}
$$

where $u_{i,j}^{(m)} = u(x_i, y_j, t^{(m)})$ and $f_{i,j}^{(m+1/2)} = f(x_i, y_j, t^{(m+1/2)})$ . 

## 10.2.2 Matrix form of the CN-WSGD scheme

To introduce the matrix form of the scheme (10.3), we define the following notations. 

(1) Let $N = N_{1} = N_{2}$ . The $N^2$ -dimensional vectors $\mathbf{u}^{(m)}$ , $\mathbf{d}_{+}^{(m)}$ , $\mathbf{d}_{-}^{(m)}$ , $\mathbf{e}_{+}^{(m)}$ , $\mathbf{e}_{-}^{(m)}$ and $\mathbf{f}^{(m + 1 / 2)}$ are defined as follows: 

$$
\mathbf {u} ^ {(m)} = \left[ u _ {1, 1} ^ {(m)}, \ldots , u _ {N _ {1}, 1} ^ {(m)}, u _ {1, 2} ^ {(m)}, \ldots , u _ {N _ {1}, 2} ^ {(m)}, \ldots , u _ {1, N _ {2}} ^ {(m)}, \ldots , u _ {N _ {1}, N _ {2}} ^ {(m)} \right] ^ {T},
$$

$$
\mathbf {d} _ {+} ^ {(m)} = \left[ d _ {1, 1} ^ {+, (m)}, \dots , d _ {N _ {1}, 1} ^ {+, (m)}, d _ {1, 2} ^ {+, (m)}, \dots , d _ {N _ {1}, 2} ^ {+, (m)}, \dots , d _ {1, N _ {2}} ^ {+, (m)}, \dots , d _ {N _ {1}, N _ {2}} ^ {+, (m)} \right] ^ {T},
$$

$$
\mathbf {d} _ {-} ^ {(m)} = \left[ d _ {1, 1} ^ {-, (m)}, \dots , d _ {N _ {1}, 1} ^ {-, (m)}, d _ {1, 2} ^ {-, (m)}, \dots , d _ {N _ {1}, 2} ^ {-, (m)}, \dots , d _ {1, N _ {2}} ^ {-, (m)}, \dots , d _ {N _ {1}, N _ {2}} ^ {-, (m)} \right] ^ {T},
$$

$$
\mathbf {e} _ {+} ^ {(m)} = \left[ e _ {1, 1} ^ {+, (m)}, \dots , e _ {N _ {1}, 1} ^ {+, (m)}, e _ {1, 2} ^ {+, (m)}, \dots , e _ {N _ {1}, 2} ^ {+, (m)}, \dots , e _ {1, N _ {2}} ^ {+, (m)}, \dots , e _ {N _ {1}, N _ {2}} ^ {+, (m)} \right] ^ {T},
$$

$$
\mathbf {e} _ {-} ^ {(m)} = \left[ e _ {1, 1} ^ {-, (m)}, \dots , e _ {N _ {1}, 1} ^ {-, (m)}, e _ {1, 2} ^ {-, (m)}, \dots , e _ {N _ {1}, 2} ^ {-, (m)}, \dots , e _ {1, N _ {2}} ^ {-, (m)}, \dots , e _ {N _ {1}, N _ {2}} ^ {-, (m)} \right] ^ {T},
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathbf {f} ^ {(m + 1 / 2)} = \left[ f _ {1, 1} ^ {(m + 1 / 2)}, \ldots , f _ {N _ {1}, 1} ^ {(m + 1 / 2)}, f _ {1, 2} ^ {(m + 1 / 2)}, \ldots , f _ {N _ {1}, 2} ^ {(m + 1 / 2)}, \ldots , f _ {1, N _ {2}} ^ {(m + 1 / 2)}, \right. \\ \left. \ldots , f _ {N _ {1}, N _ {2}} ^ {(m + 1 / 2)} \right] ^ {T}. \end{array}
$$

(2) Define the following $N^2 \times N^2$ diagonal matrices: 

$$
D _ {+} ^ {(m + 1)} = \operatorname{diag} \left(r \mathbf {d} _ {+} ^ {(m + 1)}\right), \quad D _ {-} ^ {(m + 1)} = \operatorname{diag} \left(r \mathbf {d} _ {-} ^ {(m + 1)}\right),
$$

$$
E _ {+} ^ {(m + 1)} = \operatorname{diag} \left(s \mathbf {e} _ {+} ^ {(m + 1)}\right), \quad E _ {-} ^ {(m + 1)} = \operatorname{diag} \left(s \mathbf {e} _ {-} ^ {(m + 1)}\right),
$$

where $r = \frac{\tau}{2h_x^\alpha}$ and $s = \frac{\tau}{2h_y^\beta}$ . 

(3) Define the Toeplitz matrices $A_{Q}^{\gamma,L}$ and $A_{Q}^{\gamma,R}$ by 

$$
A _ {Q} ^ {\gamma , L} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c c} w _ {1} ^ {(\gamma)} & w _ {0} ^ {(\gamma)} & 0 & \dots & 0 & 0 \\ w _ {2} ^ {(\gamma)} & w _ {1} ^ {(\gamma)} & w _ {0} ^ {(\gamma)} & \ddots & \ddots & 0 \\ \vdots & w _ {2} ^ {(\gamma)} & w _ {1} ^ {(\gamma)} & \ddots & \ddots & \vdots \\ \vdots & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & 0 \\ w _ {Q - 1} ^ {(\gamma)} & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & w _ {1} ^ {(\gamma)} & w _ {0} ^ {(\gamma)} \\ w _ {Q} ^ {(\gamma)} & w _ {Q - 1} ^ {(\gamma)} & \dots & \dots & w _ {2} ^ {(\gamma)} & w _ {1} ^ {(\gamma)} \end{array} \right], A _ {Q} ^ {\gamma , R} = \left(A _ {Q} ^ {\gamma , L}\right) ^ {T}.
$$

(4) Define the $N^2 \times N^2$ matrices $A_x^{(m+1)}$ and $A_y^{(m+1)}$ by 

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l} A _ {x} ^ {(m + 1)} = D _ {+} ^ {(m + 1)} \big (I _ {N _ {2}} \otimes A _ {N _ {1}} ^ {\alpha , L} \big) + D _ {-} ^ {(m + 1)} \big (I _ {N _ {2}} \otimes A _ {N _ {1}} ^ {\alpha , R} \big), \\ A _ {y} ^ {(m + 1)} = E _ {+} ^ {(m + 1)} \big (A _ {N _ {2}} ^ {\beta , L} \otimes I _ {N _ {1}} \big) + E _ {-} ^ {(m + 1)} \big (A _ {N _ {2}} ^ {\beta , R} \otimes I _ {N _ {1}} \big). \end{array} \right.
$$

(5) Define $A^{(m + 1)} = A_x^{(m + 1)} + A_y^{(m + 1)}$ . 

Then the CN-WSGD finite difference method (10.3) can be written in the following matrix form 

$$
\left(I _ {N ^ {2}} - A ^ {(m + 1)}\right) \mathbf {u} ^ {(m + 1)} = \left(I _ {N ^ {2}} + A ^ {(m)}\right) \mathbf {u} ^ {(m)} + \tau \mathbf {f} ^ {(m + 1 / 2)},
$$

where $I_{Q}$ denotes the $Q \times Q$ identity matrix. Let 

$$
\underline {{{A}}} ^ {(m + 1)} = I _ {N ^ {2}} - A ^ {(m + 1)}, \quad \mathbf {b} ^ {(m + 1)} = \left(I _ {N ^ {2}} + A ^ {(m)}\right) \mathbf {u} ^ {(m)} + \tau \mathbf {f} ^ {(m + 1 / 2)}.
$$

The above linear system can be written as 

$$
\underline {{{A}}} ^ {(m + 1)} \mathbf {u} ^ {(m + 1)} = \mathbf {b} ^ {(m + 1)}.\tag{10.4}
$$

Let $\gamma_0 = (-1 + \sqrt{17}) / 2 \approx 1.562$ . It can be seen from Lemma 10.1 that if $\gamma \in [\gamma_0, 2)$ , then $w_2^{(\gamma)} \geqslant 0$ , and the coefficient matrix $\underline{A}^{(m+1)}$ is a strictly diagonally dominant M-matrix. In addition, the spectral radius of the matrix $(I_{N^2} - A^{(m+1)})^{-1}(I_{N^2} + A^{(m+1)})$ is less than 1. When the diffusion coefficients are time independent, the CN-WSGD scheme [79] is unconditionally stable and the truncation error is $\mathcal{O}(h_x^2 + h_y^2 + \tau^2)$ . Compared with the method given in [78], the CN-WSGD scheme is second-order accurate without the Richardson extrapolation scheme. 

## 10.3 Sparse banded preconditioner for FDEs

The discretized linear systems (10.4) are usually large and possess a special Toeplitz-like structure, solving these structured systems is a central problem in the applications of the CN-WSGD scheme. The GMRES method introduced in Chapter 6 with restart technique is used for solving (10.4). To speed up the convergence rate of the restarted GMRES method, a preconditioner defined by the incomplete LU factorization of a block-banded-banded-block approximation of the coefficient matrix is used. 

## 10.3.1 Construction of preconditioner

Notice that the Toeplitz matrices $A_{N_{1}}^{\alpha,L}$ , $A_{N_{1}}^{\alpha,R}$ , $A_{N_{2}}^{\beta,L}$ and $A_{N_{2}}^{\beta,R}$ have a special diagonal decay property: the $(i,j)$ th entry of each of the above-mentioned matrices is of order $\mathcal{O}(|i-j|^{-\gamma})$ , where $\gamma=\alpha,\beta$ ; refer to (10.2). Thus, we consider constructing preconditioners based on banded approximation of the coefficient matrix. As diagonal compensation is a useful technique in designing preconditioners [2,59], we also consider using this technique. 

First we consider banded preconditioners for the discretized systems (10.4). Let 

$$
A _ {Q, k} ^ {\gamma , L} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c} w _ {1} ^ {(\gamma)} & w _ {0} ^ {(\gamma)} & & & \\ \vdots & w _ {1} ^ {(\gamma)} & w _ {0} ^ {(\gamma)} & & \\ w _ {k} ^ {(\gamma)} & & \ddots & \ddots & \\ & \ddots & & \ddots & w _ {0} ^ {(\alpha)} \\ & & w _ {k} ^ {(\gamma)} & \dots & w _ {1} ^ {(\gamma)} \end{array} \right] _ {Q \times Q}, A _ {Q, k} ^ {\gamma , R} = \left(A _ {Q, k} ^ {\gamma , L}\right) ^ {T}.
$$

Let $C_Q^{(\gamma)}$ be the diagonal matrices defined by 

$$
C _ {Q} ^ {\gamma} = \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c c c c} 0 & & & & & & & \\ & \ddots & & & & & & \\ & & 0 & & & & & \\ & & & w _ {k + 1} ^ {(\gamma)} & & & & \\ & & & & \ddots & & \\ & & & & & \sum_ {j = k + 1} ^ {Q} w _ {j} ^ {(\gamma)} \end{array} \right] _ {Q \times Q}.
$$

The matrices $A_{x,k}^{(m + 1)}$ and $A_{y,k}^{(m + 1)}$ are defined by 

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l l} A _ {x, k} ^ {(m + 1)} = D _ {+} ^ {(m + 1)} \Big (I _ {N _ {2}} \otimes \big (A _ {N _ {1}, k} ^ {\alpha , L} + C _ {N _ {1}} ^ {\alpha} \big) \Big) + D _ {-} ^ {(m + 1)} \Big (I _ {N _ {2}} \otimes (A _ {N _ {1}, k} ^ {\alpha , R} + C _ {N _ {1}} ^ {\alpha}) \Big), \\ A _ {y, k} ^ {(m + 1)} = E _ {+} ^ {(m + 1)} \Big (\big (A _ {N _ {2}, k} ^ {\beta , L} + C _ {N _ {2}} ^ {\beta} \big) \otimes I _ {N _ {1}} \Big) + E _ {-} ^ {(m + 1)} \Big (\big (A _ {N _ {2}, k} ^ {\beta , R} + C _ {N _ {2}} ^ {\beta} \big) \otimes I _ {N _ {1}} \Big). \end{array} \right.
$$

Then the matrix $A^{(m + 1)}$ can be split into two parts 

$$
A ^ {(m + 1)} = A _ {k} ^ {(m + 1)} + B _ {k} ^ {(m + 1)},
$$

where 

$$
A _ {k} ^ {(m + 1)} = A _ {x, k} ^ {(m + 1)} + A _ {y, k} ^ {(m + 1)}, \quad B _ {k} ^ {(m + 1)} = A ^ {(m + 1)} - A _ {k} ^ {(m + 1)}.
$$

The matrix $A_{k}^{(m + 1)}$ is a sparse banded matrix containing $4k - 3$ nonzero diagonals of $A^{(m + 1)}$ only, while the bandwidth of $I_{N^2} - A_k^{(m + 1)}$ is $2N_{1}(k - 1) + 1$ . Since $w_{k}^{(\gamma)} = \mathcal{O}(k^{-(\gamma + 1)})$ for $\gamma = \alpha, \beta$ , it follows that 

$$
\frac {\| (I _ {N ^ {2}} - A ^ {(m + 1)}) - (I _ {N ^ {2}} - A _ {k} ^ {(m + 1)}) \| _ {\infty}}{\| I _ {N ^ {2}} - A ^ {(m + 1)} \| _ {\infty}} = \frac {\| B _ {k} ^ {(m + 1)} \| _ {\infty}}{\| I _ {N ^ {2}} - A ^ {(m + 1)} \| _ {\infty}} = \mathcal {O} (k ^ {- \varsigma}),
$$

where $\varsigma = \min \{\alpha, \beta\}$ . The relative distance between $I_{N^2} - A_k^{(m+1)}$ and $I_{N^2} - A^{(m+1)}$ is very small if $k$ is sufficiently large. Thus the sparse banded matrix $I_{N^2} - A_k^{(m+1)}$ is a good approximation of the coefficient matrix $\underline{A}^{(m+1)}$ . Therefore, we can consider constructing a preconditioner for $\underline{A}^{(m+1)}$ by using the incomplete LU factorization of the sparse banded matrix $I_{N^{2}} - A_{k}^{(m+1)}$ . 

Since the banded matrix $I_{N^2} - A_k^{(m+1)}$ has $4k - 3$ nonzero diagonals only, it is a very sparse matrix. However, the bandwidth of $I_{N^2} - A_k^{(m+1)}$ is $2N_1(k-1) + 1$ . It is very expensive to calculate its inverse or LU factorization since the sparsity of the matrix is destroyed. To overcome this difficulty, we calculate incomplete LU factorization with no fill-in (ILU(0)) of the banded matrix $I_{N^2} - A_k^{(m+1)}$ . Let $L$ and $U$ be the factors of its incomplete ILU(0) factorization, then both of them have $2k - 1$ nonzero diagonals only. Thus, $P_k^{(m+1)} = LU$ is also a sparse matrix. Since the matrix $P_k^{(m+1)}$ can be a good approximation of $I_{N^2} - A_k^{(m+1)}$ , it can also be a good approximation of the coefficient matrix $A^{(m+1)}$ . For the operation of the matrix $P_k^{(m+1)}$ and its inverse $(P_k^{(m+1)})^{-1}$ on a vector $\mathbf{x} \in \mathbb{R}^{N^2}$ , we have 

$$
P _ {k} ^ {(m + 1)} \mathbf {x} = L U \mathbf {x}, \quad \left(P _ {k} ^ {(m + 1)}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {x} = U ^ {- 1} L ^ {- 1} \mathbf {x}.
$$

Thus, the preconditioner $P_{k}^{(m+1)}$ is implicitly constructed by using the incomplete LU factorization of the spare banded matrix $I_{N^{2}} - A_{k}^{(m+1)}$ . 

## 10.3.2 Preconditioned GMRES(ρ) method

Now the preconditioner $P_{k}^{(m + 1)}$ is constructed for $\underline{A}^{(m + 1)}$ . Instead of solving the linear system (10.4), we try to solve the following preconditioned linear system 

$$
\left(P _ {k} ^ {(m + 1)}\right) ^ {- 1} \underline {{A}} ^ {(m + 1)} \mathbf {u} ^ {(m + 1)} = \left(P _ {k} ^ {(m + 1)}\right) ^ {- 1} \mathbf {b} ^ {(m + 1)}.\tag{10.5}
$$

We combine the GMRES method introduced in Chapter 6 with restart strategy to solve the above preconditioned linear system. Here we use $\mathrm{GMRES}(\rho)$ to denote the restarted GMRES method which is restarted after $\rho$ iterations. For an introduction to the restarted GMRES method, one can refer to [70]. For the sake of completeness, we state the preconditioned $\mathrm{GMRES}(\rho)$ method [59] with residual checking at each iteration for the preconditioned linear system (10.5). Here we use the following notations: the symbols $r_{t}$ and $r_{p}$ denote the current residual of the original linear system and that of the preconditioned linear system, respectively. Let IterMax stand for the maximal number of iteration, and $\epsilon > 0$ denote the given relative accuracy of the residual. 

At each time step $t^{(m+1)}$ , we choose a $u_{0}$ as an initial guess for $\mathbf{u}^{(m+1)}$ . The ILU(0) factorization of $I_{N^{2}} - A_{k}^{(m+1)}$ : $P^{(m+1)} = LU$ is computed. Then we calculate the residual $\mathbf{r} = \mathbf{b}^{(m+1)} - \underline{\mathbf{A}}^{(m+1)}\mathbf{u}^{(m+1)}$ and assign $r_{t} = r$ . Initially, $\mu = 0$ . In the iteration step, we have 

Algorithm 10.1 (Preconditioned GMRES(ρ) method) 

```latex
[\left\{ \begin{array} { l } \textbf { W h i l e } \mu \leqslant I t e r M a x \text { and } \| \mathbf { r } _ { t } \| _ { 2 } / \| \mathbf { b } ^ { ( m + 1 ) } \| _ { 2 } > \epsilon \\ \mu = \mu + 1 \\ \mathbf { r } _ { p } = U ^ { - 1 } L ^ { - 1 } \mathbf { r } ; \quad \beta = \| \mathbf { r } _ { p } \| ; \quad \mathbf { v } _ { 1 } = \mathbf { r } _ { p } / \beta \\ j = 0 \\ \textbf { W h i l e } j \leqslant \rho \text { and } \| \mathbf { r } _ { t } \| _ { 2 } / \| \mathbf { b } ^ { ( m + 1 ) } \| _ { 2 } > \epsilon \\ j = j + 1 \\ \mathbf { w } _ { j } = U ^ { - 1 } L ^ { - 1 } \underline { { A } } ^ { ( m + 1 ) } \mathbf { v } _ { j } \\ \textbf { f o r } i = 1 : j \\ h _ { i j } = \mathbf { w } _ { j } ^ { T } \mathbf { v } _ { i } \\ \mathbf { w } _ { j } = \mathbf { w } _ { j } - h _ { i j } \mathbf { v } _ { i } \\ \textbf { e n d } \\ h _ { j + 1 , j } = \| \mathbf { w } _ { j } \| _ { 2 } \\ \mathbf { v } _ { j + 1 } = \mathbf { w } _ { j } / h _ { j + 1 , j } \\ \textbf { c o m p u t e } \mathbf { y } _ { j } \text { the minimizer of } \| \beta \mathbf { e } _ { 1 } - \bar { H } _ { j } \mathbf { y } \| _ { 2 } \\ \mathbf { r } _ { t } = \mathbf { r } - L U V _ { j + 1 } \bar { H } _ { j } \mathbf { y } _ { j } \\ \textbf { e n d } \\ \mathbf { r } = \mathbf { r } _ { t } \\ \mathbf { u } ^ { ( m + 1 ) } = \mathbf { u } ^ { ( m + 1 ) } + V _ { j } \mathbf { y } _ { j } \\ \textbf { e n d } \end{array} \right.] 
```

where $\bar{H}_{j} = (h_{ik})_{1 \leqslant i \leqslant j+1, 1 \leqslant k \leqslant j}$ and $e_{1} = [1, 0, \ldots, 0]^{T} \in R^{j+1}$ . The matrix $V_{j} = [v_{1} \mid v_{2} \mid \ldots \mid v_{j}] \in R^{N^{2} \times j}$ with $j \leqslant N^{2}$ in the algorithm is a matrix with orthonormal columns. 

In the preconditioned $\mathrm{GMRES}(\rho)$ method, the main computational works at each iteration are as follows: 

(1) Compute $w = U^{-1}L^{-1}\underline{A}^{(m+1)}v_{j}$ 

(2) Compute $r_{t} = r - LUV_{j+1} \bar{H}_{j} y_{j}$ . 

Since $\underline{A}^{(m + 1)}$ has a special Toeplitz-like structure, the matrix-vector multiplication $\underline{A}^{(m + 1)}\mathbf{x}$ for $\mathbf{x}\in \mathbb{R}^{N^2}$ can be calculated very efficiently [16,43]. Notice that 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} \underline {{A}} ^ {(m + 1)} \mathbf {x} & = \mathbf {x} - D _ {+} ^ {(m + 1)} \big (I _ {N _ {2}} \otimes A _ {L} ^ {\alpha , N _ {1}} \big) \mathbf {x} - D _ {-} ^ {(m + 1)} \big (I _ {N _ {2}} \otimes A _ {R} ^ {\alpha , N _ {1}} \big) \mathbf {x} \\ & - E _ {+} ^ {(m + 1)} \big (A _ {L} ^ {\beta , N _ {2}} \otimes I _ {N _ {1}} \big) \mathbf {x} - E _ {-} ^ {(m + 1)} \big (A _ {R} ^ {\beta , N _ {2}} \otimes I _ {N _ {1}} \big) \mathbf {x}, \end{array}
$$

and 

$$
I _ {N _ {2}} \otimes A _ {R} ^ {\alpha , N _ {1}} = \left(I _ {N _ {2}} \otimes A _ {L} ^ {\alpha , N _ {1}}\right) ^ {T}, \quad A _ {R} ^ {\beta , N _ {2}} \otimes I _ {N _ {1}} = \left(A _ {L} ^ {\beta , N _ {2}} \otimes I _ {N _ {1}}\right) ^ {T}.
$$

The main costs of computing $\underline{A}^{(m + 1)}\mathbf{x}$ are 

$$
\left(I _ {N _ {2}} \otimes A _ {L} ^ {\alpha , N _ {1}}\right) \mathbf {x}, \quad \left(I _ {N _ {2}} \otimes A _ {R} ^ {\alpha , N _ {1}}\right) \mathbf {x}, \quad \left(A _ {L} ^ {\beta , N _ {2}} \otimes I _ {N _ {1}}\right) \mathbf {x}, \quad \left(A _ {R} ^ {\beta , N _ {2}} \otimes I _ {N _ {1}}\right) \mathbf {x}.
$$

The multiplications $(I_{N_{2}}\otimes A_{L}^{\alpha,N_{1}})\mathbf{x}$ and $(I_{N_{2}}\otimes A_{R}^{\alpha,N_{1}})\mathbf{x}$ can be calculated simultaneously by computing $(I_{N_{2}}\otimes A_{L}^{\alpha,N_{1}})(\mathbf{x}+\sqrt{-1}\breve{\mathbf{x}})$ , where $\breve{\mathbf{x}}=[x_{N},x_{N-1},\ldots,x_{1}]^{T}$ [66]. Similarly, we can also calculate $(A_{L}^{\beta,N_{2}}\otimes I_{N_{1}})\mathbf{x}$ and $(A_{R}^{\beta,N_{2}}\otimes I_{N_{1}})\mathbf{x}$ simultaneously. By using the FFT, the matrix-vector multiplication $\underline{A}^{(m+1)}\mathbf{x}$ needs $\mathcal{O}(N^{2}\log N)$ operations only. 

In addition, since both L and U are sparse triangular matrices with 2k-1 nonzero diagonals only, the computations of Lx, Ux, $L^{-1}x$ and $U^{-1}x$ require $\mathcal{O}(N^{2}k)$ operations only. Therefore, computing $w = U^{-1}L^{-1}\underline{A}^{(m+1)}v_{j}$ requires $\mathcal{O}(N^{2}\log N)$ operations. As for computation of $r_{t}$ , it can be obtained in $\mathcal{O}(N^{2}(j+k))$ operations since $V_{j+1}$ and $\bar{H}_{j}$ are matrices of sizes $N^{2} \times (j+1)$ and $(j+1) \times j$ . Thus the computational cost of the preconditioned GMRES method is $\mathcal{O}(N^{2}\log N)$ at each time step for large N. 

## 10.3.3 Numerical result

In this subsection, we present a numerical example to illustrate the performance of preconditioned $\mathrm{GMRES}(\rho)$ method versus $\mathrm{GMRES}(\rho)$ method without preconditioners. For the CN-WSGD scheme (10.4), we choose the initial guess as 

$$
\mathbf {u} _ {0} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \mathbf {u} ^ {(0)}, & m = 0, \\ \tilde {\mathbf {u}} ^ {(m + 1)} \equiv 2 \mathbf {u} ^ {(m)} - \mathbf {u} ^ {(m - 1)}, & m > 0. \end{array} \right.
$$

Since $\tilde{\mathbf{u}}^{(m + 1)}$ is a good approximation of $\mathbf{u}^{(m + 1)}$ [90], we set it as an initial guess. The stopping criterion is 

$$
\frac {\| \mathbf {r} _ {j} \| _ {2}}{\| \mathbf {b} ^ {(m + 1)} \| _ {2}} <   1 0 ^ {- 7},
$$

where $r_{j}$ is the residual vector after the jth iteration. In our numerical experiment, we set the parameter k = 4. Moreover, we choose $\rho = 20$ for the GMRES( $\rho$ ) method. 

In the following table, we use $N_{1}$ and $N_{2}$ to denote the numbers of spatial partitions in x-direction and y-direction respectively, and use M to denote the number of temporal partition. The maximal error between the true solution and the numerical solution at the last time step is denoted by Error. In addition, the preconditioned $\text{GMRES}(\rho)$ method is denoted as $\text{PGMRES}(\rho)$ . 

Example 10.1 Consider the equation (10.1) with $\alpha = 1.8$ , $\beta = 1.9$ . The left-sided 

and right-sided diffusion coefficients are given by 

$$
\begin{array}{l l} {d _ {+} (x, y, t) = 4 (1 + t) x ^ {1. 8} (1 + y),} & {d _ {-} (x, y, t) = 4 (1 + t) (1 - x) ^ {1. 8} (1 + y),} \\ {e _ {+} (x, y, t) = 4 (1 + t) (1 + x) y ^ {1. 9},} & {e _ {-} (x, y, t) = 4 (1 + t) (1 + x) (1 - y) ^ {1. 9}.} \end{array}
$$

The spatial interval is $\Omega = (0,1) \times (0,1)$ and the time interval is $[0,T] = [0,1]$ . The initial condition is $u_0(x,y) = x^3y^3(1 - x)^3(1 - y)^3$ . The source term is given by 

$$
\begin{array}{r c l} f (x, y, t) & = & - e ^ {- t} \Big [ \big (x ^ {3} (1 - x) ^ {3} y ^ {3} (1 - y) ^ {3} \big) \\ & & + 4 (1 + t) \Big (\frac {\Gamma (4)}{\Gamma (2 . 2)} \big (x ^ {3} + (1 - x) ^ {3} \big) - \frac {3 \Gamma (5)}{\Gamma (3 . 2)} \big (x ^ {4} + (1 - x) ^ {4} \big) \\ & & + \frac {3 \Gamma (6)}{\Gamma (4 . 2)} \big (x ^ {5} + (1 - x) ^ {5} \big) - \frac {\Gamma (7)}{\Gamma (5 . 2)} \big (x ^ {6} + (1 - x) ^ {6} \big) \Big) y ^ {3} (1 - y) ^ {3} (1 + y) \\ & & + 4 (1 + t) \Big (\frac {\Gamma (4)}{\Gamma (2 . 1)} \big (y ^ {3} + (1 - y) ^ {3} \big) - \frac {3 \Gamma (5)}{\Gamma (3 . 1)} \big (y ^ {4} + (1 - y) ^ {4} \big) \\ & & + \frac {3 \Gamma (6)}{\Gamma (4 . 1)} \big (y ^ {5} + (1 - y) ^ {5} \big) - \frac {\Gamma (7)}{\Gamma (5 . 1)} \big (y ^ {6} + (1 - y) ^ {6} \big) \Big) x ^ {3} (1 - x) ^ {3} (1 + x) \Big ]. \end{array}
$$

The solution to the fractional diffusion equation is 

$$
u (x, y, t) = e ^ {- t} x ^ {3} (1 - x) ^ {3} y ^ {3} (1 - y) ^ {3}.
$$

Numerical result for Example 10.1 is shown in Table 10.1. From Table 10.1, we can see that the GMRES(20) method requires much more iterations than the PGMRES(20) method. The number of iterations required by the PGMRES(20) method increases slowly as $N_{1}$ and $N_{2}$ increase. 


Table 10.1 Numerical result for Example 10.1.
